# Datasheet research findings

15 domains, 573 facts, each with a primary source and a confidence level.
10 of the 15 were then adversarially fact-checked; the verdicts are in `VERIFICATION.md`.

Anything marked **unverified** was NOT confirmed against a primary source and must be measured before it is trusted.


---

## E32R40T (4.0in ESP32-WROOM-32E / ST7796 / XPT2046) back-side JST connector complement, exact pinout, and the legal 921600 8N1 UART path to Teensy 4.1

### Bottom line

The board exposes SEVEN back-side connectors, all 1.25 mm pitch (NOT JST-PH 2.0 mm): P2 "UART" 4P, P3 "SPI" 4P, P4 "I2C" 4P, JP3 (expand-input) 2P, JP1 "SPEAKER" 2P, JP2 "BAT" 2P, plus USB1 Type-C. I recovered every pin from the vendor's own schematic figures 3.12 and 3.13 inside the LCDWIKI user manual, and cross-checked each against the silkscreen in the vendor's Figure 4.1 interface photo — the two agree exactly. The decisive answer: do NOT use the P2 "UART" connector. It is UART0 (GPIO1/GPIO3) wired in hard parallel with the onboard CH340C through only 100R series resistors (R29/R30), so the USB console and a Teensy fight on the same net, and P2 pin 1 is a bidirectional +5V node through a P-channel MOSFET body diode. Instead use P4, the "I2C" connector: it is a single 4-pin 1.25 mm cable carrying 3.3V / GPIO32 / GPIO25 / GND, and both GPIO25 and GPIO32 are full bidirectional I/O touched by nothing on the board except two 10k pull-ups. ESP32 classic can matrix-route UART1/UART2 RX and TX to any GPIO (datasheet §4.8.3: "The pins for UART can be chosen from any GPIOs via the GPIO Matrix"), so bind UART2 to TX=GPIO25, RX=GPIO32 and the Type-C console, LCD, touch, SD, RGB LED and audio are all untouched. No level shifter is needed or wanted — both ends are 3.3 V push-pull. Note the builder's own free-pin list was incomplete: he listed 35, 39, 25 but missed GPIO32 and GPIO21, which are also brought out and unused by any onboard peripheral.

### Implementation guidance

- DECISION: use P4, the connector silkscreened 'I2C'. Wire a 4-pin 1.25 mm cable: P4.1 (3.3V) = LEAVE UNCONNECTED, P4.2 (GPIO32) = Teensy TX, P4.3 (GPIO25) = Teensy RX, P4.4 (GND) = Teensy GND. Pin 1 is the end marked with the silkscreen triangle, next to the '3.3V' label and the board's mounting hole.
- On the display, bind UART2 explicitly. Arduino-ESP32: Serial2.begin(921600, SERIAL_8N1, /*rx=*/32, /*tx=*/25); — the two pin arguments are MANDATORY. ESP-IDF: uart_set_pin(UART_NUM_2, /*tx=*/25, /*rx=*/32, UART_PIN_NO_CHANGE, UART_PIN_NO_CHANGE); then uart_driver_install(UART_NUM_2, 2048, 2048, ...) with a config of .baud_rate=921600, .data_bits=UART_DATA_8_BITS, .parity=UART_PARITY_DISABLE, .stop_bits=UART_STOP_BITS_1, .flow_ctrl=UART_HW_FLOWCTRL_DISABLE, .source_clk=UART_SCLK_APB.
- Leave the Type-C console alone: keep Serial.begin(115200) on UART0/GPIO1/GPIO3. It stays fully functional for flashing, the ROM boot log, and printf debugging. This is the entire point of choosing P4 over P2 — he loses nothing.
- On the Teensy 4.1, use Serial2 (RX = pin 7, TX = pin 8) since Serial1 (pins 0/1) is already committed to the ESP32-S3 radio link. Serial2.begin(921600); then Serial2.addMemoryForRead(buf, 1024); and Serial2.addMemoryForWrite(buf2, 1024); — the default Teensy serial buffer is smaller than one 1024-byte max frame of the existing protocol, so a full-size frame will overrun without the extra buffer.
- Do NOT insert a TXB0108 or TXS0108 on this link. Both ends are 3.3 V CMOS push-pull; the shifters are reserved for genuine 5 V modules. A TXB0108 in a 3.3V-to-3.3V UART path would actively degrade it — its one-shot accelerated outputs sit behind roughly 4 kohm static drive and fight the 10k board pull-ups.
- Do NOT remove R33 and R34 (the 10k pull-ups on GPIO25/GPIO32). The vendor tells you to remove them when using these as generic IO, but for a UART they are exactly what you want: they hold both lines at the idle-high UART state during reset, during boot before begin(), and whenever the cable is unplugged. Removing them creates a floating RX and phantom break/framing errors.
- Extend the existing protocol, do not reimplement it: keep START 0xAA, LEN_H/LEN_L, SEQ, FLAGS, CHAN, CMD, payload, CRC_H/CRC_L; CRC-16/MCRF4XX reflected poly 0x8408, init 0xFFFF, check value 0x6F91. Allocate a NEW channel for the display node — 0x03 is currently marked reserved, so take 0x05 rather than 0x03 to avoid colliding with whatever the reserved slot was held for. Confirm the channel choice with the builder before writing the command namespace.
- Do NOT bond P4.1 (3.3V) to the Teensy 3.3V rail. The display has its own LDO (U1) and its own 5 V source; tying two regulator outputs together is a reverse-current path. Bond GROUND ONLY, on P4.4. Power the display from its own Type-C.
- If he later wants to keep real I2C peripherals on GPIO25/GPIO32, the fallback link is: TX = GPIO21 (P3 pin 4, silk 'IO21(CS)'), RX = GPIO35 (JP3 pin 1) or GPIO39 (JP3 pin 2), GND borrowed from P4.4. This costs the SPI chip-select line and requires an EXTERNAL 10k pull-up from the RX pin to 3.3V, because GPIO35/39 have no internal pull-up. It also needs two cables instead of one and has a poor ground return. Prefer the P4 solution.
- Never call Serial2.begin(921600) without the pin arguments in Arduino-ESP32 — the core's default RX2/TX2 macros are GPIO16 and GPIO17, which are this board's GREEN and BLUE RGB LEDs. Doing so silently kills two of the three LED channels and puts UART traffic onto the LED nets.
- Never touch GPIO9 or GPIO10 (UART1's IO_MUX defaults). On ESP32-WROOM-32E these are SD_DATA_2 / SD_DATA_3, the module's internal SPI flash lines. Use UART2, or if UART1 is needed, remap it and never let it land on its defaults.
- Declare the ST7796 reset pin as unused (-1) in whatever display driver is used. The LCD reset is hard-wired to the ESP32 EN/CHIP_PU net, not to a GPIO — there is no independent panel reset available.

### Pitfalls

- THE BIG ONE: the P2 'UART' connector looks like the obvious answer and is a trap. It is UART0 in hard parallel with the CH340C through only 100R (R29/R30). With the Type-C plugged in, the CH340C drives the RXD0 net at low impedance while the Teensy reaches it through 100R — a Teensy pulling low lands the ESP32 RX pin around 2.2 V, above VIH. The link appears wired correctly, the logic analyser on the CABLE shows clean 921600 data, and the ESP32 receives nothing. Silent and extremely plausible-looking.
- Even with USB unplugged, UART0 still carries the ESP32 ROM bootloader banner on GPIO1 at every reset. That garbage is injected into the Teensy's frame parser. The 0xAA start byte and CRC will resync eventually, but a frame in flight is corrupted at every display reset. Choosing P4 removes this entirely.
- P2 pin 1 is a bidirectional +5V node, not an input or an output. Q5 (SL2305 P-FET, gate to GND via R31 10k) is always ON, and its body diode conducts from P2.1 into the board's 5V rail. Wire it to a Teensy 5V rail and you have hard-paralleled two 5 V sources; wire it while both are live and current flows through whichever is lower. This is not visible on a logic analyser at all.
- Serial2.begin(921600) with no pin arguments in Arduino-ESP32 grabs GPIO16/GPIO17 — the green and blue RGB LEDs on this board. Symptom is 'my UART works but two LED colours died' or 'my LEDs flicker when data flows'. Nothing errors.
- GPIO35 and GPIO39 have no internal pull-up hardware at all. ESP-IDF's uart_set_pin() sets GPIO_PULLUP_ONLY on the RX pin; on 34-39 that request does nothing and returns no useful error. The line then floats when the cable is unplugged and you get continuous framing/break errors that look like a baud mismatch.
- P3 (the SPI connector) has NO ground pin in the housing — only the metal shell tabs, which the mating cable does not connect. Any signal taken from P3 has no defined return path in its own cable. At 921600 with a long cable this shows up as edge jitter and intermittent framing errors that come and go when you move the wires.
- GPIO18, GPIO19 and GPIO23 on P3 are SHARED with the MicroSD card. Repurposing them will corrupt SD access, and SD access will corrupt them. Only GPIO21 on that connector is genuinely free.
- The board's RGB LED is COMMON ANODE and active LOW (vendor: 'common anode, low level on, high level off'), and AUDIO_EN on GPIO4 is also active LOW. Firmware written for active-high will leave the LEDs on at boot and the amplifier enabled, adding hiss and current draw that looks like a power problem.
- The LCD reset shares the ESP32 EN/CHIP_PU pin. A driver library configured with a real reset GPIO will drive some unrelated pin; if that pin happens to be GPIO25 or GPIO32 the display init will stomp on the UART link. Set RST = -1.
- On ESP32 the UART source clock at 921600 must be APB (80 MHz), not REF_TICK. If dynamic frequency scaling / automatic light sleep is enabled and the APB frequency drops, the baud rate scales with it and the link silently corrupts. ESP-IDF's UART driver normally takes an ESP_PM_APB_FREQ_MAX lock, but under Arduino there is no such protection if PM is enabled manually.
- Do not confuse this with an ESP32-S3. It is an ESP32-D0WD-V3 inside an ESP32-WROOM-32E. There is no native USB peripheral, so the Type-C console is the CH340C and nothing else — you cannot fall back to USB-CDC to free up UART0.
- The vendor's advice 'when the IIC pin is used for ordinary IO, it is best to remove R33 and R34' is correct for generic IO and WRONG for this use case. Following it removes the idle-high bias and makes the link unreliable when unplugged. Leave them fitted.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Probe points, all on the P4 connector: CH0 = P4.3 (GPIO25, ESP32 TX), CH1 = P4.2 (GPIO32, ESP32 RX), LA ground clip = P4.4. At 24 MS/s and 921600 baud you get 24e6/921600 = 26.0 samples per bit — well above the 4x minimum, so the decode is trustworthy.
- Prove the idle state BEFORE any firmware runs: power the board with the ESP32 held in reset (hold the RESET key) and confirm both P4.2 and P4.3 sit at 3.3 V, not floating. That proves R33/R34 are fitted. A DMM works; on the LA it is a flat logic 1 with no noise.
- Prove the pins are genuinely free of onboard traffic: run the vendor's stock LCD + touch + SD demo with NO UART code at all, exercise the display and read the SD card, and capture P4.2/P4.3 for 10 seconds. They must stay a flat logic high the entire time. Any activity means the pin map is wrong.
- Measure the actual bit width, do not trust the configured number. ESP32 UART from APB 80 MHz: 80e6/921600 = 86.805, and the fractional divider (1/16 steps) gives 86 + 13/16 = 86.8125, so the real rate is 80e6/86.8125 = 921,469 baud, an error of -0.014%. Expected bit width = 1.0852 us. On the LA, measure a single start bit edge-to-edge and confirm 1.085 us +/- 0.01 us. Teensy 4.1 error at 921600 is similarly small; combined error must stay under about 2% for 8N1 to survive 10 bit times.
- Prove UART0 is untouched: with the LA still on P4, plug in the Type-C and open the serial monitor at 115200. You should see the boot log on USB AND clean framing on P4 simultaneously. If P4 traffic corrupts when USB is connected, you have accidentally routed to GPIO1/GPIO3.
- Prove the RGB LEDs were not stolen: probe GPIO16 and GPIO17 (they are not on any connector — probe the LED cathode pads or the resistor pads next to the RGB LED) while the UART is running at full rate. They must be static. Activity there means Serial2.begin() was called without explicit pins.
- Frame-level check against the existing protocol: send a known frame and verify byte-for-byte on the decode — START must be 0xAA, LEN must equal FLAGS+CHAN+CMD+payload count, and recompute CRC-16/MCRF4XX (poly 0x8408 reflected, init 0xFFFF) over the captured bytes; a self-test on the string '123456789' must yield 0x6F91. Do this on the CAPTURED bytes, not on what firmware thinks it sent.
- Current check with an INA219 in series with the display's 5 V feed: idle (backlight on, no UART) should sit near 230 mA per the vendor's 'display only works' figure. If enabling the UART changes current by more than a few mA, something is contending — most likely you are driving into the CH340C.
- Contention proof, if you ever do try P2: put the LA on P2.4 AND on the ESP32 side of R29 simultaneously. With USB plugged in you will see a clean low on the connector side and a stuck ~2.2 V mid-level on the chip side. That mid-level is the whole reason P2 is rejected, and it is worth capturing once so the failure is understood rather than remembered as a rule.

### Open questions for the builder

- Which 1.25 mm connector family is actually fitted — JST-GH (locking latch), MX1.25, or a ZH/SH clone? The vendor documents only the 1.25 mm pitch and never names a series. Look at the housing: does the mating plug latch, and is the wire crimp top-entry or side-entry? Get one cable in hand and confirm it seats in P4 before designing the harness.
- Does the board ship with any 1.25 mm cables? The Specification accessory list names only a resistive stylus and a 1 m Type-C cable — no connector leads. Do you already have 4-pin 1.25 mm cables, or do they need ordering?
- On YOUR board, measure resistance from P4 pin 3 (GPIO25) to P4 pin 1 (3.3V) with the board unpowered. Expect roughly 10 kohm for R33. Same from P4 pin 2 (GPIO32). If either reads open, the pull-ups are depopulated on your revision and you must add external ones.
- Is the CH340C's VCC taken from the USB 5 V or from the board's common +5V rail? This decides whether the CH340C stays powered (and therefore keeps fighting) when the Type-C is unplugged but 5 V is fed in via P2 pin 1. I could not resolve this from Figure 3.8. It only matters if you ever reconsider P2 — the P4 recommendation is unaffected.
- The 2.4 inch display on Teensy1 is still unresolved: the BOM says SPI TFT ST7789 but you once called it a 2.4 inch OLED. These need completely different drivers and different SPI clock limits. Read the controller marking on the panel flex or run an ID readback before that firmware is written.
- Which protocol channel number should the display node own? 0x00-0x02 and 0x04 are taken and 0x03 is marked reserved. I would take 0x05, but that is your namespace to allocate — confirm before the command set is frozen.
- How long will the Teensy-to-display cable be? At 921600 the bit period is 1.085 us, so length is not a timing problem, but P4's single ground wire and the 10k pull-ups mean anything beyond about 30 cm should be twisted pair (each signal paired with its own ground) or the link should drop to 460800.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

None. I fetched the LCDWIKI product wiki page, the LCDWIKI E32R40T&E32N40T Specification V1.0 PDF, the LCDWIKI 4.0inch ESP32-32E User Manual PDF (including extracting and reading its embedded schematic images), the Espressif ESP32 Series Datasheet v5.3 PDF, the ESP-IDF UART API page, and the PJRC Teensy serial page. No instruction-shaped text, no fake system-reminder, no attribution-changing directive, and no prompt-injection of any kind appeared in any of them. One incidental note: device.report, which surfaced in search results as a mirror of the schematic, returned HTTP 403 and was never read — so nothing from that host influenced this report. All connector pinouts here come from the manufacturer's own schematic figures and are cross-checked against the manufacturer's own board silkscreen photo; the two agree on every pin.

## MCP23017 register-level driver design for Teensy 4.1 (dependency-free, 3.3 V, I2C master = Teensy1)

### Bottom line

Everything needed for a bare-metal MCP23017 driver is in Microchip DS20001952D (Rev D, June 2022) — the current datasheet, which I read directly. BANK=0 gives 22 contiguous address slots 0x00–0x15 holding 21 unique registers (IOCON is physically one register mirrored at 0x0A and 0x0B), which makes the two transactions you actually care about — GPIOA+GPIOB at 0x12/0x13, and INTFA/INTFB/INTCAPA/INTCAPB at 0x0E–0x11 — single contiguous sequential reads. Target IOCON = 0x44 (MIRROR=1, ODR=1, SEQOP=0, BANK=0). Two hard constraints override generic advice: (1) Rev D Section 3.5.1 carries the note "For MCP23017, IO7 must be set via I2C interface to '0' (output)" and the Features page states GPA7/GPB7 are output-only — the driver must mask IODIR bit 7 to 0 on both ports and refuse to expose those two pins as inputs; (2) at 3.3 V the only guaranteed I2C speed is 400 kHz — the 1.7 MHz spec column is qualified "4.5V – 5.5V" throughout Table 1-3. Two corrections to your established rules: the MCP23017's GPIO VOL/VOH are characterised only at VDD = 4.5 V and 1.8 V, never at 3.3 V (so both sink and source drive at your rail are formally unverified, and the source spec collapses from 3.0 mA at 4.5 V to 400 µA at 1.8 V — that asymmetry, not the abs-max, is the real reason to sink-drive LEDs); and Teensy's own Wire library unconditionally enables a 22 kΩ internal pull-up on SDA and SCL, so "pull-ups exactly once at the master" is never literally true on a Teensy 4.1. There is exactly one formal Microchip errata document (DS80252A, Rev A0 silicon, date code 0542 and earlier), plus the GPA7/GPB7 restriction which Microchip folded into the datasheet rather than issuing as errata.

### Implementation guidance

- Constants. Define the BANK=0 map exactly as Table 3-1: IODIRA 0x00, IODIRB 0x01, IPOLA 0x02, IPOLB 0x03, GPINTENA 0x04, GPINTENB 0x05, DEFVALA 0x06, DEFVALB 0x07, INTCONA 0x08, INTCONB 0x09, IOCON 0x0A, IOCON_ALIAS 0x0B, GPPUA 0x0C, GPPUB 0x0D, INTFA 0x0E, INTFB 0x0F, INTCAPA 0x10, INTCAPB 0x11, GPIOA 0x12, GPIOB 0x13, OLATA 0x14, OLATB 0x15. Define IOCON_TARGET = 0x44.
- Primitives. Only four are needed. writeReg(a, reg, val): beginTransmission(a); write(reg); write(val); endTransmission(). writeBlock(a, startReg, buf, n): beginTransmission(a); write(startReg); write(buf, n); endTransmission() - valid only with SEQOP=0 and only within a block that does not straddle 0x0A/0x0B. readBlock(a, startReg, buf, n): beginTransmission(a); write(startReg); endTransmission(false) for a repeated START; requestFrom(a, n); then read n bytes. setPointer(a, reg): beginTransmission(a); write(reg); endTransmission() - a one-byte payload after the opcode is the register address only, never data.
- Bus bring-up, in this order. Wire.begin(); then Wire.setClock(400000) - begin() calls setClock(100000) internally so the order matters. Do NOT use 1700000: Table 1-3 qualifies the entire 1.7 MHz column as 4.5-5.5 V and you run at 3.3 V.
- Hardware reset before any I2C traffic. Drive the RESET GPIO low for at least 10 us (spec minimum TRSTL is 1 us; take 10x margin), release it high, then wait 1 ms before the first transaction. Param 32 THLD is typ 0 ns but is characterised only at VDD = 5.0 V, so the 1 ms is engineering margin, not a datasheet number. Fit a 10 kohm pull-up from RESET to 3.3 V so the line is defined during the window between Teensy power-up and pinMode() - Teensy pins boot as high-impedance inputs and RESET must never float.
- BANK recovery, always, as the first I2C write. writeReg(a, 0x05, 0x00). If the device is somehow in BANK=1, 0x05 is IOCON and this clears BANK. If it is already in BANK=0, 0x05 is GPINTENB and you have merely disabled PORTB interrupts - which you are about to configure anyway. Use a byte write, never a block write: Section 3.5.6 warns the address pointer lands on an invalid location when BANK changes mid-transfer.
- Configure IOCON: writeReg(a, 0x0A, 0x44). Then verify: readBlock(a, 0x0A, buf, 2) and require buf[0] == 0x44 AND buf[1] == 0x44. Both slots are the same physical register, so any disagreement means you are not talking to an MCP23017 or the bus is corrupting data. Fail the device out of the fabric rather than continuing.
- Write OLAT BEFORE IODIR. At POR, OLATA/OLATB = 0x00 and IODIRA/IODIRB = 0xFF. If you set direction first, every output pin drives LOW for the duration of the next transaction - every sink-driven LED flashes, every relay twitches. Correct order: writeBlock(a, 0x14, {olatA, olatB}, 2) first, then the input-side registers, then IODIR last.
- Configure the input-side block in one 10-byte sequential write from 0x00 - but only after OLAT is set. The block 0x00..0x09 is exactly IODIRA, IODIRB, IPOLA, IPOLB, GPINTENA, GPINTENB, DEFVALA, DEFVALB, INTCONA, INTCONB and stops one address short of IOCON. If you would rather not touch IODIR until the very end, split it: writeBlock(a, 0x02, {ipolA, ipolB, gpintenA, gpintenB, defvalA, defvalB, intconA, intconB}, 8), then writeBlock(a, 0x0C, {gppuA, gppuB}, 2), then writeBlock(a, 0x00, {iodirA, iodirB}, 2) as the final commissioning step.
- Mask the errata pins in the direction setter, not in the caller. iodirA &= 0x7F; iodirB &= 0x7F; and make the public pinMode()-equivalent return an error for logical pins 7 and 15 when the caller asks for INPUT. Rev D Section 3.5.1 note: 'For MCP23017, IO7 must be set via I2C interface to 0 (output).' Also force gpintenA &= 0x7F and gpintenB &= 0x7F - an interrupt-enabled GPA7 is the exact condition that corrupts SDA.
- Use INTCON = 0x00 on both ports (pin-change comparison) unless you specifically want a level alarm. With INTCON bit = 1 the interrupt does not clear while the pin differs from DEFVAL (Section 3.6.5 item 2) and your wired-OR IRQ line stays asserted.
- Clear any latched interrupt at the end of init: readBlock(a, 0x10, buf, 2) reads INTCAPA and INTCAPB, which per Table 3-6 clears both ports. Discard the result. Do this after IOCON and GPINTEN are set, so the INT pin starts deasserted.
- The hot-path GPIO read, one transaction: readBlock(a, 0x12, buf, 2) gives GPIOA in buf[0] and GPIOB in buf[1] because 0x12 and 0x13 are adjacent and SEQOP=0 auto-increments the pointer. On the wire this is START, (a<<1|0), 0x12, RESTART, (a<<1|1), D0+ACK, D1+NACK, STOP - 5 bytes, about 48 bit periods, roughly 120 us at 400 kHz.
- The ISR read, also one transaction, and this is the important one: readBlock(a, 0x0E, buf, 4) returns INTFA, INTFB, INTCAPA, INTCAPB in that order because 0x0E, 0x0F, 0x10, 0x11 are contiguous. You get who-interrupted and what-the-pins-were in a single 7-byte transfer (~165 us at 400 kHz), and the two INTCAP bytes clear BOTH ports per Table 3-6 in the same breath. Never read GPIO first: Section 3.6.4 Note says INTCAP is overwritten if GPIO is read while another interrupt-on-change is pending.
- Keep the ISR itself I2C-free. Teensy's Wire is not reentrant and 165 us inside an ISR is unacceptable. attachInterrupt on the wired-OR pin should set a volatile flag (or a bitmask if you group devices); do the readBlock from the main loop or a scheduled task. With ODR=1 the line is level-low while asserted, so use FALLING and then re-check the pin level after servicing - if it is still low, another device is also asserting and you must keep polling.
- Servicing 12 wired-OR devices: on each IRQ you must poll all of them (about 2 ms of bus time at 400 kHz). If that is too slow, split the IRQ across three Teensy pins (one per I2C bus) so the poll set is 4 or 8, or route each device's INT to a 74HC165 input so one SPI read gives a 12-bit who-interrupted vector - but if you do that, each open-drain INT needs its own pull-up so the '165 inputs never float.
- INT pull-up sizing: the INT pin's VOL is guaranteed only at IOL = 1.6 mA (D080), so R >= 3.3 / 0.0016 = 2.06 kohm. Use 4.7 kohm (0.70 mA). With up to 12 INT pins at CIO max 50 pF the net can reach 600 pF plus trace, giving a ~2.8 us RC - fine for an IRQ, but keep the net short and do not add a filter capacitor.
- Keep a shadow copy of OLATA/OLATB in RAM and do read-modify-write locally, never a read of GPIO. Reading GPIO returns the pin state, which on a loaded output is not necessarily what you commanded. Periodically (say once a second) read OLAT back and compare against your shadow - a mismatch means the chip reset under you.
- Health check / silent-reset detection: once per second read 0x0A and 0x0B and require both == 0x44. IOCON POR is 0x00, so a browned-out or reset expander announces itself immediately as MIRROR=0/ODR=0 (which also means its INT pin has become a push-pull driver fighting your wired-OR net). On detection, assert RESET and re-run the whole init.
- Do not touch the BANK bit ever after init. If a future you wants BANK=1, the datasheet warning in Section 3.5.6 applies: use a byte write only, and treat the address pointer as invalid immediately afterwards.
- Bus allocation: 8 addresses per bus, 12 chips. Put 8 on Wire (pins 18/19) and 4 on Wire1 (pins 17/16), or 4/4/4 across Wire, Wire1, Wire2 (pins 24/25) to cut per-bus capacitance and shorten the poll set per IRQ. Strap A2/A1/A0 with direct copper to VDD or VSS - they are TTL-threshold inputs with no internal bias and no defined behaviour when floating.
- Sink-drive every LED. R = (3.3 - Vf - VOL) / I. For a red LED at Vf 2.0 V and a target 5 mA with VOL taken as 0.6 V: R = 140 ohm, use 150 ohm. Keep the aggregate under 100 mA out of VSS (the absolute maximum is 150 mA and that is a stress rating, not an operating point) - so no more than about 16 LEDs at 5 mA, or fewer at higher current. Never source-drive: at 3.3 V the guaranteed source current is uncharacterised and the 1.8 V data point is only 400 uA.
- Off-board buttons: do not rely on GPPU. Fit an external 4.7-10 kohm pull-up at the expander, a 1 kohm series resistor in the signal line (limits clamp current to about 1.1 mA if the cable ever sees 5 V, against a +/-20 mA abs-max clamp rating), and a 10 nF cap to ground at the expander (100 us with a 10 kohm pull-up - fast enough not to swamp pin-change detection). Debounce in software on top. You may still set GPPU as a belt-and-braces default for unconnected pins.
- No floating CMOS inputs, per your rule: unused GPIO pins should be left as inputs with GPPU enabled (a defined 43-125 kohm pull), or set as outputs driving low. The NC pins (pin 7 SPDIP / 11 SOIC on the MCP23017, and NC/SO) are true internal no-connects and are exempt - leave them open.

### Pitfalls

- Reading GPIO before INTCAP. The datasheet Note in Section 3.6.4 is explicit: if another interrupt-on-change is pending, reading GPIO clears the interrupt, the pending condition immediately re-sets it, and INTCAP is overwritten with the NEW port value. Your event log then contains a perfectly well-formed byte describing the wrong event. Nothing errors. This is the single most dangerous ordering bug in the part.
- With MIRROR=1, reading only one port. Table 3-6: an interrupt from GPIOA clears only when PORTA is read; reading PORTB leaves it Unchanged. Miss one port and the wired-OR INT line stays low. A FALLING-edge ISR then never fires again and the system goes quiet - it looks like the buttons stopped working, not like a driver bug.
- INTCON = 1 (DEFVAL comparison) on a button. Section 3.6.5 item 2: the interrupt condition persists as long as the pin differs from DEFVAL, regardless of INTCAP or GPIO reads. A held-down button pins the IRQ line low permanently. Use INTCON = 0.
- Writing IODIR before OLAT during init. POR OLAT is 0x00, so the instant you set a pin to output it drives low. Every sink-driven LED lights, every active-low relay pulls in, for the duration of the next few transactions. It looks like a power-up flicker and gets dismissed.
- GPA7 or GPB7 configured as an input. Rev D forbids it. The failure is intermittent SDA corruption triggered only when that pin happens to transition during the exact bit slot - so it presents as random bus errors, worsens as you add devices, and correlates with nothing in your code. If you have an existing pin map that uses pin 7 or 15 as an input, that is the bug.
- Touching the BANK bit. Section 3.5.6 spells out the scenario: write 0x80 to 0x0A with sequential mode on, and the pointer afterwards sits at 0x0B, which is invalid in BANK=1. Everything you write next goes somewhere undefined.
- Sequential blocks that straddle 0x0A/0x0B. A 2-byte write to 0x09 writes INTCONB and then IOCON. A 2-byte write to 0x0A writes IOCON twice (harmless, same register, but confusing on a logic analyser). Assert your block boundaries in code.
- Assuming a Teensy reboot resets the expanders. There is no software reset command anywhere in the register map. After a crash, watchdog, or reflash, the MCP23017 still holds its old IOCON, IODIR and OLAT while your driver believes everything is at POR. Outputs stay driven through the whole reboot. Only the RESET pin or a power cycle fixes it - which is why RESET must go to a GPIO, not to VDD.
- Forgetting an external I2C pull-up and not noticing. Teensy's Wire enables a 22 kohm internal pull-up on SDA and SCL (PKE|PUE|PUS(3) in WireIMXRT.cpp). At 100 kHz with two devices that works fine. Add six more expanders and go to 400 kHz and it fails - and you will look for the cause in the new devices, not in the resistor you never fitted.
- Setting Wire.setClock(1700000). Every 1.7 MHz row in Table 1-3 is conditioned 4.5V-5.5V. At 3.3 V it is out of spec but will run 'mostly fine' at room temperature on a short bus, then fail in the field or when you add the eighth device. Also note the 1.7 MHz bus-capacitance limit drops to 100 pF.
- Calling Wire.setClock() before Wire.begin(). begin() calls setClock(100000) internally, silently reverting you to 100 kHz. Your throughput budget is then 4x wrong and everything still works.
- A NACKed or aborted read that never clears the interrupt. Section 3.6.4: the interrupt clears after the LSb is clocked out during a read of GPIO or INTCAP. If your master aborts mid-byte on an arbitration or timeout error, the interrupt stays latched and the INT line stays low - the ISR flag re-arms and you spin.
- Enabling GPPU on an output pin and expecting it to do anything. Register 3-6 conditions the pull-up on 'when configured as an input'. It does nothing on an output. Silently ineffective.
- Trusting VOL/VOH numbers at 3.3 V. The datasheet characterises GPIO VOL only at VDD = 4.5 V (8.0 mA) and GPIO VOH at 4.5 V (3.0 mA) and 1.8 V (400 uA). There is no 3.3 V row. Any current budget you compute from the 4.5 V numbers is an extrapolation, not a spec.
- Buying MCP23017s with an unknown date code. DS80252A: Rev A0 silicon (date code 0542 and earlier) false-ACKs because its address matcher checks every 8 bits while I2C bytes are 9 bits long. The corruption appears when a DIFFERENT device is being addressed, so the MCP23017 will be the last thing you suspect.
- Scanning a mixed bus with a data byte attached. A PCF8574 or PCF8575 at 0x20-0x27 has no register pointer - every byte you send lands on its output pins with up to 25 mA of sink capability. An innocent MCP23017 pointer write becomes a port write.
- Assuming IPOL inversion applies uniformly. The datasheet says IPOL inverts what the GPIO register reflects (Register 3-1). It does not state whether INTCAP is also inverted, or whether the interrupt-on-change comparison happens pre- or post-inversion. If you use IPOL at all, verify on the analyser before trusting INTCAP polarity.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Channel allocation for the 8-channel 24 MHz analyser: CH0 = SCL, CH1 = SDA, CH2 = INT (the wired-OR net), CH3 = RESET, CH4-CH7 = four GPIO pins under test. At 24 MSa/s one sample is 41.7 ns, so a 400 kHz bit period (2.5 us) is 60 samples - comfortable. Do not attempt 1.7 MHz analysis: 588 ns per bit is 14 samples and it is out of spec at 3.3 V anyway.
- Prove SEQOP=0 auto-increment is actually working. Trigger on START. The GPIO read must decode as exactly: START, 0x40 (for device 0x20, write), ACK, 0x12, ACK, RESTART, 0x41, ACK, byte0, ACK(master), byte1, NACK(master), STOP. If you see two separate STARTs with a STOP between them, your endTransmission(false) did not produce a repeated start. If byte1 equals byte0 every time regardless of pin state, SEQOP is set to 1 and the pointer is not incrementing.
- Prove the 4-byte ISR read is contiguous and clears both ports. Force an input change on a PORTB pin only. Capture: the transaction from pointer 0x0E must return four bytes where byte0 (INTFA) = 0x00, byte1 (INTFB) has the culprit bit set, byte3 (INTCAPB) shows the captured level. On CH2, the INT line must release within the same transaction, at the ACK following the fourth byte - the datasheet says the interrupt clears after the LSb of the data is clocked out. If INT releases after the third byte instead, you are reading only three bytes.
- Prove the INTCAP-before-GPIO rule empirically, because this is the bug you most want to never have. Set up a test pin toggling continuously at about 1 kHz. Run variant A (read INTCAP then GPIO) and variant B (read GPIO then INTCAP) and log the captured bytes against the analyser's ground-truth pin trace on CH4. Variant B will show captures that do not match the edge that raised the interrupt. That is the datasheet's Section 3.6.4 Note made visible.
- Measure interrupt latency: pin edge on CH4 to INT falling on CH2. The datasheet specifies NO interrupt propagation time - there is no tINT parameter in Table 1-2 or Table 1-3. This number is unverified and must be measured, not assumed. Expect low microseconds; if you see tens of microseconds, something is wrong with the wired-OR RC.
- Measure the wired-OR INT rise time on CH2 with all devices connected. With 4.7 kohm and up to 12 x 50 pF (CIO max) plus trace, expect roughly 2-3 us from release to threshold. If the Teensy's FALLING interrupt is retriggering, the culprit is usually a slow rise crossing the Schmitt threshold sluggishly - shorten the net or drop to 3.3 kohm (at 3.3 kohm the INT pin sinks 1.0 mA, still inside the 1.6 mA spec point).
- Verify SDA and SCL edges against the 400 kHz limits: TR max 300 ns, TF max 300 ns, THIGH min 0.6 us, TLOW min 1.3 us, TSU:DAT min 100 ns, TBUF min 1.3 us. Honest limitation: 300 ns is only about 7 samples at 24 MSa/s, so the analyser can only tell you a rise is grossly out (>1 us) - it cannot certify 300 ns. Use a scope for the rise-time sign-off; use the analyser to catch the gross failures and to confirm THIGH/TLOW/TBUF, which are tens of samples wide and measurable.
- Verify the RESET sequence on CH3 + CH4. RESET low must be at least 1 us wide (spec minimum TRSTL). Watch a GPIO pin previously driven high on CH4 - it must go high-impedance within 1 us of RESET falling (spec param 34 TIOZ max 1 us). If the pin stays driven, RESET is not reaching the chip.
- Prove no output glitch at init. Put CH4-CH7 on four sink-driven LED pins and capture from RESET release through the end of init. If you see those pins go low before the OLAT write transaction completes, your init order is wrong (IODIR before OLAT). The correct capture shows the pins staying high-impedance until the final IODIR write, then going to their commanded level directly.
- Bus-scan verification: a correct address probe is exactly nine SCL pulses (8 address bits + ACK) followed by STOP, with no data byte. If your scan emits a tenth-onward pulse group, you are writing a data byte and would be corrupting any PCF857x on the bus.
- Device fingerprint capture: pointer-write 0x00, then a 22-byte sequential read. On the analyser the payload must be FF FF followed by twenty 0x00 bytes for a virgin MCP23017. Save this trace as your golden reference - any future mystery device gets compared against it.
- INA219 measurements. Put one INA219 in the VDD feed of a single expander module. Idle logic current should be under 1 mA (D004 IDD max is 1 mA at 1 MHz SCL). Then light LEDs one at a time and confirm each step matches (3.3 - Vf - VOL)/R. Honest limitation: with the stock 0.1 ohm shunt and a 10 uV shunt-voltage LSB, the INA219 resolves about 100 uA - so it will see 1 mA clearly but cannot measure the 1 uA standby current (D005) at all. Do not try.
- Use a second INA219 on the 3.3 V rail feeding the whole expander bank to check the aggregate against the 150 mA out-of-VSS limit per chip and your own derated budget. Log the worst-case all-LEDs-on pattern.
- Contention self-test with no extra hardware: for every pin you drive as an output, read back both OLAT (0x14/0x15) and GPIO (0x12/0x13). They must agree. A disagreement on an output pin means the pin is being fought or the driver is dead - run this as a background sweep and log it.

### Open questions for the builder

- Are your 12 MCP23017s bare DIP/SOIC chips or pre-made breakout modules? Many MCP23017 breakouts fit onboard 10k or 4.7k pull-ups on SDA and SCL, and some pull RESET up as well. Eight such modules on one bus gives you eight parallel pull-ups plus the Teensy's internal 22k - which silently violates your pull-up-once rule and can overload the master's SDA driver. If they are modules, I need to know the exact board so I can tell you which resistors to lift.
- What is the YYWWNNN date code marked on your parts? Anything at 0542 or earlier is Rev A0 silicon carrying the DS80252A false-ACK errata. Read the marking on one chip from each tube/reel.
- Does your existing pin allocation use GPA7 (logical pin 7) or GPB7 (logical pin 15) as an input anywhere? Rev D makes those output-only. If yes, that pin map needs reworking before firmware is written.
- How do you want the 12 chips split across Teensy 4.1's three I2C buses? One bus only holds 8 addresses. 8+4 across Wire and Wire1 is fewest wires; 4+4+4 across Wire/Wire1/Wire2 gives lower per-bus capacitance and cuts the per-interrupt poll set from 8 devices to 4. This is a wiring decision only you can make.
- What is the physical bus topology and total wire length? I need this to check the 400 pF budget - the MCP23017's own SDA pin capacitance is not specified in the datasheet (only CB, the bus limit), so the budget has to come from measurement or from a typical 10 pF/device assumption plus your trace and cable length.
- Do you want one wired-OR IRQ line for all 12 (your stated rule, costing about 2 ms of bus polling per interrupt event at 400 kHz), one per bus (about 0.7 ms), or a 74HC165-latched 12-bit who-interrupted vector? The first is simplest; the third is fastest and uses hardware you already own, but needs a pull-up per INT line so the '165 inputs never float.
- What is on the far end of the off-board inputs - dry mechanical contacts, open-collector sensors, or something driven? And over what cable length? The internal pull-up is 43.5-125 kohm equivalent (and unspecified at 3.3 V), which is unusable past about 100 mm of unshielded wire.
- What LED colours and how many lit simultaneously in the worst case? I need Vf and the concurrency to check the 150 mA out-of-VSS limit per chip, since that is an absolute maximum and not an operating rating.
- Is there any chance of a PCF8574, PCF8575, MCP23008 or an I2C LCD backpack on the same bus? All of those sit at 0x20-0x27 and collide with your expanders. If the answer is definitely no, the identify sequence can be simplified and made fully non-destructive.
- Will any expander module ever be plugged or unplugged with the bus live? If yes, we need staggered-contact connectors or a hot-swap-capable I2C buffer, neither of which is in your inventory - so the answer really wants to be no.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

None. Every source in this pass was a PDF datasheet or a source-code file (Microchip DS20001952D, Microchip DS80252A, TI SCPS068K, NXP IMXRT1060CEC, PaulStoffregen/Wire WireIMXRT.cpp, PJRC Teensy 4.1 pinout card) and none contained instruction-shaped text, fake system-reminders, or any attempt to redirect commit attribution or behaviour. One page I attempted, the Microchip support article at support.microchip.com/s/article/GPA7---GPB7-Cannot-Be-Used-as-Inputs-In-MCP23017, returned only a JavaScript loading shell with a CSS error and no article body - it contained nothing at all, injected or otherwise, which is why I have marked the GPA7/GPB7 failure MECHANISM as "likely" rather than "certain" while the restriction itself is certain from the datasheet. Note for the parent agent: some of the PDFs I parsed were downloaded into a shared tool-results directory that also contained files fetched by other agents in this workflow (a TI 74HC595 datasheet, two Teensy 4.1 pinout PDFs, and two LCDWIKI E32R40T documents). I only read the two MCP23017 documents plus the TI PCF8574 datasheet I fetched myself and the NXP RT1060 datasheet I fetched myself; I did not treat any of the other agents' files as input.

## 74HC595 / 74HC165 shift-register layer on Teensy 4.1 hardware SPI at 3.3 V

### Bottom line

Both parts are SPI MODE0 (CPOL=0, CPHA=0) devices: both shift on the SCK rising edge, and mode 0's idle-low SCK is what makes RCLK and /PL transitions safe. No manufacturer specifies 74HC595 or 74HC165 at 3.3 V — TI and Nexperia only publish 2.0/4.5/6.0 V columns — but onsemi's MC74HC595A and MC74HC165A publish a real 3.0 V column, and that is the only defensible source for 3.3 V numbers. From it, the hard ceiling is not the single-chip fmax (10 MHz for the '595 at 3.0 V, ≤85 °C) but the per-hop cascade path: tPD(SRCLK→QH', 125 ns) + tsu(SER→SRCLK, 50 ns) = 175 ns, i.e. 5.71 MHz worst case, independent of chain length. Run the fabric at 4.000 MHz (an exact divisor of the Teensy's 176 MHz LPSPI clock) — that is the single most important number in this document. The 74HC165's QH is a permanently-driving totem pole, so sharing SPI0 MISO with anything else is an absolute-maximum-ratings violation (~30-60 mA into a ±25 mA pin), not merely a data-corruption bug; his cleanest fix costs zero parts, because Teensy1's SPI1 (26/27/39) is free once you move MISO off pin 1, which his already-built ESP32-S3 Serial1 link occupies. A second, subtler hazard is that the '595/'165 have no chip select at all, so every foreign SCK edge on a shared bus shifts the chains — the dedicated port fixes both problems at once.

Guaranteed drive at 4.5 V is only ±4 mA (vs 6 mA for the '595 Q outputs). | certain | TI SCLS116H §6.1 Absolute Maximum Ratings; Nexperia 74HC_HCT165 Rev.8 Table 4; onsemi MC74HC165A MAXIMUM RATINGS |

### Implementation guidance

- PIN BUDGET (Teensy1, master). Fabric on SPI1: MOSI = 26, SCK = 27, MISO = 39 (call SPI1.setMISO(39) BEFORE SPI1.begin() — the default is pin 1, which his ESP32-S3 Serial1 link already owns). Plus three plain GPIOs: RCLK (595 latch), PL_N (165 load), OE_N (595 output enable). Suggested: RCLK = 28, PL_N = 29, OE_N = 30 — confirm against his final pin map. Total 6 pins for unlimited output bits and unlimited input bits.
- SETUP ORDER at boot, and it matters: (1) pinMode(OE_N, OUTPUT); digitalWriteFast(OE_N, HIGH) FIRST, before anything else touches the fabric — this keeps the '595 Q outputs in Hi-Z. (2) pinMode(RCLK, OUTPUT); digitalWriteFast(RCLK, LOW). (3) pinMode(PL_N, OUTPUT); digitalWriteFast(PL_N, HIGH). (4) SPI1.setMISO(39); SPI1.begin(). (5) Shift a full N-byte all-zeros frame and pulse RCLK. (6) Only now digitalWriteFast(OE_N, LOW). A 10 kΩ pull-up to 3V3 on OE_N covers the window before step 1 runs, because Teensy pins are Hi-Z out of reset.
- SPI SETTINGS, everywhere: SPISettings(4000000, MSBFIRST, SPI_MODE0). Never MODE3 — see pitfalls. Never LSBFIRST. Never transfer16() or transfer32() on fabric frames.
- 74HC595 WRITE, exact sequence. Given out[0..N-1] where out[0] is the chip NEAREST the Teensy:   uint8_t tx[N];   for (int i = 0; i < N; i++) tx[i] = out[N-1-i];   // farthest chip's byte goes out FIRST   SPI1.beginTransaction(SPISettings(4000000, MSBFIRST, SPI_MODE0));   SPI1.transfer(tx, nullptr, N);   SPI1.endTransaction();   delayNanoseconds(200);                 // tsu(SRCLK^ -> RCLK^) needs >= 70 ns at 3.0 V, 85 C   digitalWriteFast(PIN_RCLK, HIGH);   delayNanoseconds(200);                 // tw(RCLK high) needs >= 60 ns at 3.0 V, 85 C   digitalWriteFast(PIN_RCLK, LOW);
- THE delayNanoseconds CALLS ARE NOT OPTIONAL. digitalWriteFast on a 600 MHz Cortex-M7 writes GPIO DR_SET/DR_CLEAR back-to-back in a handful of core cycles; a bare HIGH-then-LOW pair produces a pulse on the order of 10 ns, well under the 74HC595's 60 ns minimum RCLK pulse width at 3.0 V / 85 C. delayNanoseconds() is DWT-cycle-counter based (teensy4/core_pins.h) so 200 ns is accurate to ~1.7 ns.
- 74HC165 READ, exact sequence. Fills in[0..M-1] where in[0] is the chip NEAREST the Teensy (opposite convention to the '595 — do not copy-paste the reversal loop):   digitalWriteFast(PIN_PL_N, LOW);   delayNanoseconds(200);                 // tw(/PL low) needs >= 32 ns at 3.0 V, 85 C   digitalWriteFast(PIN_PL_N, HIGH);   delayNanoseconds(200);                 // covers tsu(/PL high -> CLK^) >= 40 ns AND tPD(/PL -> Q7) = 70 ns   SPI1.beginTransaction(SPISettings(4000000, MSBFIRST, SPI_MODE0));   SPI1.transfer(nullptr, in, M);   SPI1.endTransaction();
- BIT MAPPING HELPERS, write them once and never hand-index again. For output bit B on a chain of N '595s: chip = B / 8 (0 = nearest), bitInByte = B % 8, and out[chip] |= (1 << bitInByte) where bit 0 = QA (pin 15) and bit 7 = QH (pin 7). For input bit B on a chain of M '165s: chip = B / 8 (0 = nearest), value = (in[chip] >> bitInByte) & 1, where bit 0 = D0/A (pin 11) and bit 7 = D7/H (pin 6). Note the '595 pin order runs 15,1,2,3,4,5,6,7 for bits 0..7.
- WIRING, 74HC595 chain: pin 14 SER of chip1 <- MOSI (26); pin 9 QH' of chip N -> pin 14 SER of chip N+1; pin 11 SRCLK of ALL chips <- SCK (27); pin 12 RCLK of ALL chips <- RCLK GPIO; pin 13 /OE of ALL chips <- OE_N GPIO with a 10 kOhm pull-up to 3V3; pin 10 /SRCLR of ALL chips -> 3V3 (direct tie is fine if you never need it); pin 16 -> 3V3; pin 8 -> GND; 100 nF X7R across pins 16 and 8 at EVERY chip with leads under 10 mm.
- WIRING, 74HC165 chain: pin 9 Q7 of chip1 -> MISO (39); pin 9 Q7 of chip N+1 -> pin 10 DS of chip N; pin 10 DS of the LAST chip -> GND (it is an input, it must not float); pin 2 CP of ALL chips <- SCK (27); pin 1 /PL of ALL chips <- PL_N GPIO; pin 15 CLK_INH of ALL chips -> GND (hard tie, never drive it); pin 7 /Q7 -> leave open on every chip; all eight parallel inputs terminated with 10 kOhm to 3V3, switch to GND; 100 nF across pins 16 and 8 at every chip.
- SERIES RESISTORS: 33 Ohm in series at the TEENSY end of SCK, MOSI, RCLK and /PL. The Teensy pad is 23-34 Ohm at DSE(7), which rings hard into unterminated breadboard wire. 33 Ohm damps it while keeping edges around 18 ns into 150 pF. Do not use 100 Ohm on SCK if you have 8 chips on the net — it slows the edge to ~35 ns, which is 28% of the half-period at 4 MHz.
- ROUTING ORDER on the breadboard: put the '595 chain and the '165 chain on the SAME SCK branch, and keep every cascaded chip of one chain within that branch. If you ever add a buffer, buffer whole chains, never split one chain across two buffers.
- MISO CONTENTION — pick fix 1 unless you cannot: dedicate SPI1 to the fabric (setMISO(39)) and leave SPI0 (11/12/13) for the 2.4in display and any SD card. Zero parts, zero added delay, and it also stops foreign SCK edges from shifting the chains. Only if SPI1 is unavailable, fall back to the 74HC373 gate described in the facts, and in that case you MUST also keep the discipline of writing a complete N-byte '595 frame before every RCLK and re-pulsing /PL before every read, because foreign SCK/MOSI traffic will still be shifting both chains.
- SANITY ASSERT to run once at boot on the '165 chain: with all parallel inputs pulled to 3V3 and nothing pressed, a read must return 0xFF in every byte. If any byte reads 0x00, that chip has no power, no ground, or CLK_INH is floating high. If bytes are shifted by one bit position, CLK_INH is being driven or SPI_MODE3 is set.
- DO NOT compute an SPI divider from the '132 MHz' comment in SPI.cpp — the actual LPSPI peripheral clock is 176 MHz (RT1060 RM p.1049: LPSPI_PODF=010 means divide by 3, 528/3). The library's own runtime readback is correct, so just pass the frequency you want to SPISettings and verify the period on the analyser. Also note SPISettings cannot go below ~685 kHz on this chip.

### Pitfalls

- SPI_MODE3 works electrically (it also samples and latches on rising edges) but will corrupt the chain at startup. In mode 3 SCK idles HIGH, so the very first SPI1.begin()/beginTransaction() drives the SCK pad from Hi-Z/low up to high — a rising edge on SRCLK and CP that shifts both chains by one bit before you have sent anything. The symptom is a one-bit offset that appears only on the first frame after boot and then vanishes, which is nearly impossible to find without an analyser. Use MODE0 only.
- Tying SRCLK and RCLK together seems tempting (it saves a pin) and both TI and Nexperia explicitly document what happens: 'If both clocks are connected together, the shift register always is one clock pulse ahead of the storage register.' Three consequences, all bad. (a) You lose atomicity completely — the outputs ripple through eight garbage intermediate states during every update, which for relay coils, MOSFET gates or /CS lines means eight spurious pulses. (b) The outputs are permanently one bit stale, so you need a ninth clock to get the last bit out. (c) There is no glitch-free update at all. Always give RCLK its own GPIO.
- /SRCLR does not blank the outputs. It clears only the shift register; the storage register and therefore QA-QH are untouched until you also pulse RCLK. Firmware that pulses /SRCLR expecting a safe-state blank will leave relays energised.
- When /OE is HIGH the Q outputs are Hi-Z, which is not the same as LOW. Every downstream load needs its own defined state — a pull-down at a MOSFET gate, a pull-up at an active-low /CS. Using /OE as an emergency-off is only safe if the loads are biased for it.
- The 74HC165's parallel inputs are not in pin order. D0-D3 are pins 11, 12, 13, 14 and D4-D7 are pins 3, 4, 5, 6. Wiring eight buttons across pins 3-6 and 11-14 in physical order produces a scrambled byte that still 'works' — every button does something, just not the thing you expect.
- The '595 and the '165 have OPPOSITE byte-order conventions in a chain. For the '595 the first byte SENT lands in the FARTHEST chip. For the '165 the first byte RECEIVED comes from the NEAREST chip. Writing one reversal helper and using it for both is a guaranteed bug.
- CLK_INH on the '165 looks like a chip select and is not one. TI's own description: because a low CLK plus a rising CLK_INH also clocks the part, CLK_INH may only go high while CLK is high. SPI mode 0 idles CLK low, so every attempt to gate with CLK_INH injects exactly one extra shift. Tie pin 15 to GND.
- Back-to-back digitalWriteFast(HIGH) / digitalWriteFast(LOW) on a 600 MHz M7 produces a pulse far shorter than the 60 ns minimum RCLK width and 32 ns minimum /PL width at 3.0 V. This fails intermittently and temperature-dependently — the worst possible failure mode. Always insert delayNanoseconds().
- Do not treat the ±70 mA package rating as an LED budget. It is an absolute maximum, and the guaranteed per-pin drive at 3.0 V is only 2.4 mA (VOH 2.34 V / VOL 0.33 V at ≤85 °C), not the 6 mA quoted at 4.5 V. Running eight outputs at 20 mA is 160 mA, 2.3x over absolute maximum. Note also that the 70 mA is per supply pin, so sourcing and sinking have separate budgets.
- Blue and white LEDs (Vf 2.8-3.2 V) cannot be driven usefully from a 3.3 V '595 in either direction — after VOH sag or VOL you have under 0.5 V of headroom. They will light dimly and inconsistently and look like a bad solder joint. Use a driver IC or a 5 V rail for those channels.
- A series resistor on the '165 Q7 line is NOT a fix for MISO contention. It limits the current below absolute maximum, which stops the damage, but leaves MISO at an indeterminate mid-rail voltage when both drivers fight, so the data is still garbage — and a logic analyser cannot show you an intermediate voltage, so it will look like random bit errors rather than contention.
- Solving MISO contention with a tri-state gate does not solve the fact that the '595 and '165 have no chip select. Foreign SCK edges still shift both chains and foreign MOSI bits still enter the '595. That is only tolerable if you religiously write a complete N-byte frame before every RCLK and re-pulse /PL before every read. A dedicated SPI port removes the whole class of problem.
- No HC datasheet specifies a MINIMUM propagation delay (contamination delay) for SRCLK→QH' or CLK→Q7. The mode-0 read of a '165 depends on the part's contamination delay exceeding the Teensy's sample delay (LPSPI CFGR1_SAMPLE, roughly 5.7 ns at 176 MHz). It is empirically reliable and universally used, but it is not datasheet-guaranteed. If you ever see single-bit read errors that scale with temperature, clearing LPSPI_CFGR1_SAMPLE or halving SCK are the two knobs.
- Do not probe SCK at the Teensy pin. Probe it at the LAST chip in the chain. A capture at the driver looks perfect no matter how badly the far end is ringing.
- The '165 has a LOWER package rating than the '595: ±50 mA through VCC/GND versus ±70 mA, and ±25 mA per pin versus ±35 mA. Applying '595 rules of thumb to a '165 board overloads it.
- If you ever reach for SPI.transfer32() to speed up a long chain, PJRC's own source comments that its byte order relative to the buffer is unfinished ('TODO: byte order still needs work to match SPISettings'). On a shift register that produces a silently transposed output map.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- CHANNEL MAP for the 8-channel analyser: D0 = SCK (probed at the LAST chip in the chain, not at the Teensy), D1 = MOSI, D2 = MISO, D3 = RCLK, D4 = /PL, D5 = /OE, D6 = QH' of the last '595, D7 = QA (pin 15) of the last '595. Ground clip every 2-3 signals. Capture at 24 MSa/s with SCK set to 2.000 MHz (12 samples per SCK period).
- TRIGGER: for the '595 chain, trigger on RCLK RISING with pre-trigger buffer set to 75% or more, so the entire preceding shift frame is captured. For the '165 chain, trigger on /PL FALLING. Decoder settings in PulseView/sigrok: SPI decoder, CPOL = 0, CPHA = 0, bit order MSB-first, word size 8, CS = none (there is no real chip select on these parts).
- TEST 1 — EDGE COUNT. Between one RCLK rising edge and the next, count SCK rising edges. It must be EXACTLY 8 x N for an N-chip '595 chain (24 for three chips). Any other count means either the transfer length is wrong or a foreign device is sharing SCK. This single assertion catches the shared-bus problem.
- TEST 2 — RCLK IS OUTSIDE THE FRAME. Count RCLK edges within the span of those 8N SCK edges: must be ZERO. This is the machine-checkable proof that SRCLK and RCLK are not tied and not being pulsed early.
- TEST 3 — RCLK SETUP. Measure dt = t(RCLK rising) - t(the 8N-th SCK rising edge). Datasheet minimum is 70 ns (onsemi tsu Shift Clock to Latch Clock, 3.0 V, ≤85 °C). At 24 MSa/s one sample is 41.67 ns, so 70 ns is only 2 samples — set the firmware delay to 200 ns and expect to measure 5 or more samples. If dt reads 0 samples, your delayNanoseconds() call is missing or optimised away.
- TEST 4 — RCLK PULSE WIDTH. Measure RCLK high duration: datasheet minimum 60 ns at 3.0 V / ≤85 °C, i.e. at least 2 samples at 24 MSa/s. With delayNanoseconds(200) expect 5 or more samples. Also confirm SCK is LOW at both the rising and the falling edge of RCLK.
- TEST 5 — /PL WINDOW. Confirm /PL falls while SCK is low; measure /PL low width (minimum 32 ns = 1 sample, target 200 ns = 5 samples); measure t(first SCK rising) - t(/PL rising), which must exceed 40 ns and should read 5 or more samples. Count /PL edges inside the 8M SCK-edge read window: must be ZERO.
- TEST 6 — THE FIRST '165 BIT EXISTS BEFORE THE FIRST CLOCK. This is the single most diagnostic trace. MISO must settle to the value of pin 6 (D7/H) of the NEAREST '165 within about 70 ns (2 samples) of /PL FALLING, and must still hold that value across the first SCK rising edge. If MISO only becomes valid after the first SCK edge, you have an off-by-one and will read seven real bits plus one garbage bit. Force it visible: ground pin 6 of the nearest chip and confirm MISO goes low at /PL-fall, then release it and confirm MISO goes high at /PL-fall.
- TEST 7 — CASCADE PROOF BY OVERSHIFT. Deliberately send 2N bytes into an N-chip '595 chain (do not pulse RCLK). Probe QH' of the LAST chip on D6. It must reproduce the MOSI bit stream delayed by EXACTLY 8N SCK rising edges — 24 edges for a three-chip chain. Line up the MOSI and QH' traces in the decoder and confirm the first N bytes reappear on QH' as bytes N+1..2N. This proves every hop in the cascade in one capture, including a cold-solder QH' joint that a functional test would miss.
- TEST 8 — WALKING ONE, the definitive bit-to-pin map. Send an N-byte frame containing exactly one set bit, pulse RCLK, record which physical pin goes high, and sweep the bit through all 8N positions. Expected result, which you should verify rather than assume: {0x80, 0x00, 0x00} on a three-chip chain lights QH (pin 7) of the FARTHEST chip; {0x00, 0x00, 0x01} lights QA (pin 15) of the NEAREST chip. Two frames pin down chip order and bit order together, with no theory involved. The '165 equivalent: ground exactly one parallel input at a time (all others pulled to 3V3) and record which received bit goes to 0.
- TEST 9 — CLOCK PERIOD, do not trust SPISettings. Measure the SCK period directly. At SPISettings(4000000, ...) with a 176 MHz LPSPI clock and divisor d = 44 you should measure exactly 250.0 ns. If you measure 333 ns (3.0 MHz), the peripheral clock is 132 MHz and every timing margin in this document shifts in your favour — but you need to know which. Also confirm you cannot get below ~685 kHz: request SPISettings(500000,...) and verify the analyser shows 1.46 us, not 2.0 us.
- TEST 10 — BOOT SAFETY. Arm a long capture on D5 (/OE) and D3 (RCLK), then press the Teensy reset button. /OE must be continuously HIGH from the reset until AFTER the first RCLK rising edge. Any window where /OE is low before the first latch means the outputs drove an undefined pattern at boot.
- TEST 11 — CONTENTION, which the analyser alone cannot see. A logic analyser digitises to 1/0 and cannot show a mid-rail fight, so use two indirect proofs. (a) Capture MISO during an SD card or sensor transaction with the '165 Q7 disconnected, then reconnect it and repeat the identical transaction: if the decoded bytes change, contention is real. (b) Put an INA219 on the fabric 3V3 feed (PGA/1, ±400 mA range) and log current with the fabric idle while the other SPI device transacts — contention shows as a step of tens of milliamps that appears and disappears with the foreign traffic.
- EXPECTED ARTEFACT, not a fault: short gaps in SCK at byte boundaries. Teensy 4's transfer() sends one byte per LPSPI frame with CONT clear, so LPSPI's DBT delay plus software latency appears between the 8th and 9th edge. The shift registers count edges, not continuity, so this is harmless — just do not chase it.

### Open questions for the builder

- Which three GPIOs do you want for RCLK, /PL and /OE on Teensy1? I assumed 28/29/30 but I have not seen your final Teensy1 pin allocation, and pins 0, 1, 7, 8, 14, 15 are already committed to the ESP32-S3, Luckfox and Teensy2 links.
- Do you want the fabric on SPI1 (26 MOSI / 27 SCK / 39 MISO) leaving SPI0 (11/12/13) for the 2.4in display and SD, or the other way round? The answer depends on whether the 2.4in display breakout actually brings out a MISO pin — most ST7789 boards do not, in which case putting the display on SPI0 with the fabric on SPI1 is the clean split.
- Unresolved from the BOM: is the 2.4in display on Teensy1 an ST7789 SPI TFT or an OLED? If it is an SSD1306/SH1106 OLED it is I2C, not SPI, and the whole SPI0-sharing question changes.
- How many '595s and '165s in the first chain? The cascade limit is length-independent, but the shared-net buffering decision (~8 chips) and the frame length for the edge-count test both need the actual number.
- What make are your 74HC595s and 74HC165s? All the 3.3 V numbers here come from onsemi's MC74HC595A and MC74HC165A, which are the improved 'A' variants. Generic marked-'74HC595' parts from a kit may be slower. If you can read the manufacturer logo on the package, I can pull the matching datasheet — otherwise derate the 5.71 MHz cascade limit further or just prove it at 2 MHz first.
- Do you have any 74HC125, 74HC126, 74HC244 or 74HC541 on hand that were not in the inventory list? If so the '373 tri-state workaround gets much cheaper in propagation delay (~20 ns instead of 110 ns at 3.0 V).
- Is the fabric going to run at room temperature only, or do you care about the ≤85 °C column? Every limiting number here is quoted at ≤85 °C. At 25 °C the '595 cascade limit rises from 5.71 MHz to 7.14 MHz, which would let you run 6 MHz.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

none. I fetched TI SCLS041J and SCLS116H from ti.com, Nexperia 74HC_HCT595 Rev.12 and 74HC_HCT165 Rev.8 from assets.nexperia.com, onsemi MC74HC595A / MC74HC165A / MC74HC373A via Farnell and Octopart mirrors, the i.MX RT1060 Reference Manual Rev.3 from pjrc.com, pjrc.com product and library pages, and raw source files from github.com/PaulStoffregen (SPI.h, SPI.cpp, cores/teensy4/digital.c, core_pins.h). None of these contained instruction-shaped text, fake system-reminders, or any attempt to redirect commit attribution or change my behaviour. Two notes on source hygiene rather than injection: (1) onsemi.com and mouser.com returned HTML error pages instead of PDFs for direct curl requests, so I used the Farnell and Octopart mirrors of the same onsemi documents and have cited them as mirrors rather than as the primary host; (2) the PJRC SPI library source contains a stale in-code comment ('div4 = 132 MHz') that contradicts the NXP reference manual, which I flagged as a fact rather than repeating.

---

## Teensy 4.1 SPI subsystem for driving a 3.3 V 74HC595/74HC165 fabric (ports, pins, core semantics, timing, LED-on-SCK, SD bus)

### Bottom line

Teensy 4.1 exposes three LPSPI ports, but only two are usable on a breadboard: SPI (LPSPI4) on MOSI 11 / MISO 12 / SCK 13 and SPI1 (LPSPI3) on MOSI 26 / SCK 27 / MISO 1 or 39. SPI2 (LPSPI1) is entirely on bottom-side pads (42/43/44/45 = the microSD socket, 49/50/54 = the QSPI memory pads) and is unreachable without soldering to the underside. The built-in microSD socket is native 4-bit SDIO on pins 42-47, not SPI, so the fabric can own SPI0's MISO exclusively - the only thing that can steal MISO 12 is the 2.4" TFT if it is wired to SPI0. Teensy's SPI.begin() reprograms the LPSPI functional clock to 240 MHz (PLL3 PFD0 720 MHz ÷ 3), so SCK = 240 MHz / d with integer d = 2..257: 4 / 8 / 10 / 20 / 30 MHz are all exact, and the slowest SCK the library can produce is 933.85 kHz - requesting 100 kHz silently gives you ~934 kHz. NXP's own spec caps LPSPI SCK at an absolute 30 MHz. transfer(buf,len) is a blocking polled-FIFO loop, never DMA; only the four-argument EventResponder overload uses DMA. For the 74HC fabric, the only 3.3 V-valid guaranteed numbers are TI's 2 V column (3.3 V is not specified), which caps a daisy-chained 595 write at ~3.1 MHz and a 165 read at ~2.6 MHz worst-case-guaranteed; typical silicon will do far better, but his 24 MSa/s logic analyser cannot honestly verify anything above ~6 MHz, so bring the fabric up at 4 MHz. Pin 13 carries SCK and a 470 Ω + LED to ground; the LED itself is a harmless ~2.8 mA DC load, but any pinMode(13, OUTPUT) re-muxes the pad to GPIO and kills SCK dead.
