### Implementation guidance

- Port choice: put the 74HC fabric on SPI1 (LPSPI3), not SPI. Call SPI1.setMISO(39); SPI1.setSCK(27); SPI1.setMOSI(26); BEFORE SPI1.begin(). Using pin 39 for MISO and pin 38 (or any plain GPIO) for chip selects keeps pins 0 and 1 free for Serial1, and keeps pin 13/LED out of the fabric entirely. If the fabric must live on SPI (LPSPI4), it is MOSI 11 / MISO 12 / SCK 13 and you accept the LED on SCK.
- Never call pinMode(13, ...) or pinMode(LED_BUILTIN, ...) anywhere in a sketch that uses SPI (LPSPI4). pinMode() ends with *(mux) = 5|0x10 and hard-disconnects LPSPI4 SCK from the pad. Delete any Blink leftovers. Also avoid digitalWrite(13,...): because SPI.begin() leaves pin 13's GPIO direction bit clear, digitalWrite takes the input branch and overwrites the pad register with a 22k pull-up + hysteresis + slow SPEED, silently degrading SCK edges.
- Do not call SPI.begin() at all if you are not using LPSPI4 - it reprograms CCM_CBCMR (LPSPI clock select and PODF) globally for all three LPSPI ports, and it takes over pins 11/12/13's pad settings.
- Order of operations in setup(): SPI1.begin(); then pinMode() every CS/RCLK//PL/enable pin OUTPUT and drive it to its inactive level, then power or release the fabric. On reset all Teensy pins are inputs, so the fabric's RCLK, /PL, /SRCLR, /OE and 138 enable must be held inactive by external resistors (10k), not by firmware - firmware runs too late.
- Standard 595 write: SPI1.beginTransaction(SPISettings(4000000, MSBFIRST, SPI_MODE0)); SPI1.transfer(buf, len) with the buffer already reversed so buf[0] reaches the FARTHEST chip; then delayNanoseconds(100); digitalWriteFast(RCLK_PIN, HIGH); delayNanoseconds(100); digitalWriteFast(RCLK_PIN, LOW); SPI1.endTransaction();  The first delayNanoseconds(100) covers the 595's tsu SRCLK-before-RCLK (94 ns worst case at the 2 V bound); the second guarantees the RCLK high pulse exceeds tw (100 ns at the 2 V bound).
- Standard 165 read: digitalWriteFast(PL_PIN, LOW); delayNanoseconds(100); digitalWriteFast(PL_PIN, HIGH); delayNanoseconds(100); SPI1.beginTransaction(SPISettings(4000000, MSBFIRST, SPI_MODE0)); SPI1.transfer(nullptr_or_dummy_buf, rxbuf, len); SPI1.endTransaction();  The /PL low pulse must exceed tw = 80 ns and the parallel data must be stable 100 ns before /PL falls. Remember bit order: H exits first, so rxbuf byte bit7 = input H and bit0 = input A.
- Use literal pin numbers with digitalWriteFast, e.g. digitalWriteFast(30, HIGH). A runtime variable pin silently falls back to the slow digitalWrite() table lookup. If you need runtime-selectable pins, cache portSetRegister(pin)/portClearRegister(pin) and the bitmask once at init and write them directly.
- Never rely on a bare digitalWriteFast pair to produce a visible pulse: two back-to-back stores give ~3.3 ns, which no 74HC138 will decode cleanly and which your 24 MSa/s analyser cannot see. Every CS/RCLK//PL/enable pulse must be bracketed by delayNanoseconds(100) minimum. For 74HC138 break-before-make, deassert the E3 global enable, delayNanoseconds(100), change A0-A2, delayNanoseconds(100), reassert E3.
- Pick SCK values that divide 240 MHz exactly so the LA capture matches the arithmetic: 4 MHz (d=60), 8 MHz (d=30), 10 MHz (d=24), 20 MHz (d=12). Bring up at SPISettings(4000000, ...). Do not request anything below ~934 kHz - the library clamps at 240 MHz/257 = 933.85 kHz and you will get a clock 9x faster than you asked for.
- Never request more than 30000000 in SPISettings on any Teensy 4 port - that is NXP's absolute maximum LPSPI operating frequency, independent of what the 74HC parts can do.
- If any ISR (MCP23017 INT handler, IntervalTimer, encoder) will ever touch the fabric bus, register it: SPI1.usingInterrupt(digitalPinToInterrupt(MCP_INT_PIN)) - but understand that on Teensy 4 this masks IRQ_GPIO6789, i.e. ALL attachInterrupt pins, for the duration of every transaction. Keep transactions short (<= 64 bytes) or accept the latency. Better design: never touch SPI from an ISR at all; have the ISR set a volatile flag and do the transfer from loop().
- Do not assume SPI1.transfer(buf, len) is DMA - it is a blocking polled-FIFO loop. Budget CPU time as len * 8 / SCK. At 4 MHz, 64 bytes = 128 us of blocking spin. If you need the CPU back, use the four-argument form: SPI1.transfer(txbuf, rxbuf, count, eventResponder), check its bool return (false = channels unavailable or a transfer already active), and assert/deassert CS in the completion callback, not around the call.
- If you use the async DMA form with buffers in DMAMEM, declare them DMAMEM and 32-byte aligned: DMAMEM __attribute__((aligned(32))) uint8_t rxbuf[64]; The library calls arm_dcache_delete() on any address >= 0x20200000, which operates on whole 32-byte cache lines and will corrupt anything sharing a line with an unaligned buffer. Ordinary stack/global buffers (DTCM, below 0x20200000) need no alignment and get no cache maintenance.
- Use SPI1.transfer16() when you want two 595s clocked as one uninterrupted 16-bit frame - the byte-at-a-time path inserts a hardware DBT gap of roughly half an SCK period between frames (beginTransaction programs CCR DBT = SCKDIV/2), which you will see on the analyser as a gap between bytes.
- Put 47-100 ohm series resistors at the Teensy end of SCK, MOSI, RCLK and /PL. The RT1062 pad is ~37 ohm (DSE=7 = R0/7 with R0 = 260 ohm at 3.3 V) and SPI.begin() additionally sets SPEED(2) on the SPI pins, making them the fastest-slewing, most reflective drivers on the board. 68 ohm is a good single value: it lands near the ~45 ohm critical-damping estimate with margin.
- If the built-in microSD is used (SD.begin(BUILTIN_SDCARD)), it is native SDIO on pins 42-47 and shares nothing with SPI or SPI1 - MISO 12 stays exclusively yours. But it does consume every SPI2 pin, so treat SPI2 as permanently unavailable rather than 'reserved for later'.

### Pitfalls

- The stale comment trap: SPI.cpp's begin() says '// CBCMR[LPSPI_CLK_SEL] - PLL2 = 528 MHz // CBCMR[LPSPI_PODF] - div4 = 132 MHz' but the code immediately below selects CLK_SEL(1) = PLL3 PFD0 = 720 MHz and PODF(2) = /3 = 240 MHz. Every blog post and forum answer that quotes '132 MHz' is quoting the comment, not the code. If you size dividers from 132 MHz, every SCK you compute will be off by a factor of 1.82 and you will not notice until the analyser disagrees.
- SPISettings clamps low frequencies silently. SPISettings(100000) or SPISettings(400000) produce 933.85 kHz, not what you asked for. There is no error, no warning. A 'let's start slow and safe' bring-up at 100 kHz is actually a 934 kHz bring-up.
- A leftover pinMode(LED_BUILTIN, OUTPUT) after SPI.begin() kills LPSPI4 SCK completely, and the failure looks like a dead fabric rather than a pin conflict, because MOSI and CS still toggle perfectly on the analyser. Only SCK is missing.
- digitalWrite(13, ...) after SPI.begin() does not break SPI outright - it quietly rewrites the pad control register, replacing DSE(7)|SPEED(2) with DSE(7)|PKE|PUE|PUS(3)|HYS. SCK keeps working but with a 22k pull-up, hysteresis and the slow SPEED setting. The symptom is marginal timing that appears only at higher clock rates and only after a particular code path runs.
- Every 74HC speed number you will find quoted for 3.3 V is an interpolation. TI specifies HC only at 2 V, 4.5 V and 6 V. The rigorous position is that 3.3 V behaviour is bounded above by 4.5 V figures and guaranteed no worse than 2 V figures. Designing to the 4.5 V column (15 MHz cascade limit) is a guess; designing to the 2 V column (3.1 MHz cascade limit) is a guarantee. Anything between must be measured.
- The 595 daisy-chain limit is NOT fclock. fclock (5 MHz guaranteed at 3.3 V via the 2 V column) applies to a single device. In a chain the binding constraint is tpd(SRCLK->QH') + tsu(SER before SRCLK) <= one SCK period = 200 + 125 = 325 ns worst case, i.e. 3.08 MHz. The failure mode is that only the FIRST chip in the chain gets correct data and the rest are shifted by one bit - which reads as a wiring error, not a timing error.
- A 24 MSa/s logic analyser cannot verify anything you care about above ~6 MHz SCK, and cannot see a bare digitalWriteFast CS pulse at all (3.3 ns vs a 41.7 ns sample period). Worse, an FX2-based 8-channel analyser at 24 MSa/s on all 8 channels is at the USB 2.0 bulk-transfer limit and can drop samples on long captures - a dropped sample looks exactly like a missing clock edge. Capture 4 channels at 24 MHz, or 8 channels at 12 MHz, for anything longer than a few ms.
- usingInterrupt() on Teensy 4 is far blunter than on Teensy 3: it masks IRQ_GPIO6789, which is every attachInterrupt pin on the board. Register it for one pin and you have registered it for all of them. A 1024-byte transfer at 4 MHz masks all pin interrupts for 2.05 ms.
- beginTransaction() gives you no mutual exclusion against code that did not call usingInterrupt(). If a timer ISR or a library (display driver, SD, audio) touches the same SPI object without registering, the two transfers interleave inside the FIFO and you get plausible-looking but wrong bytes - not a crash.
- transfer(buf, len) transfers IN PLACE: it calls transfer(buf, buf, count), so your transmit buffer is overwritten with the received bytes. If you re-send the same buffer expecting the original contents, you will send back whatever the 165 chain returned.
- delayNanoseconds() is a minimum, not an exact delay, because interrupts stay enabled throughout. Never use it to bound a maximum interval (e.g. 'RCLK must rise within 200 ns of the last SCK edge'). A systick or USB interrupt landing in the middle will stretch it by microseconds and you will only see it on a rare capture.
- SPI2 looks available in the API and compiles cleanly, but every one of its pins is on the underside of the board. Code that references SPI2 will build, run, and do nothing observable on a breadboard.
- The i.MX RT1062 LPSPI timing spec assumes a 30 pF maximum load on the SPI pins. A breadboard fabric with 8 shift registers on a shared clock is at 100-150 pF - 3 to 5x over spec. The NXP-guaranteed tV = 8 ns output valid time does not apply at that load; the real edge rate is set by the pad's ~37 ohm impedance driving your actual capacitance.
- Teensy programs CCR DBT = SCKDIV/2, so consecutive bytes in a multi-byte transfer are separated by a gap of roughly half an SCK period. When you count edges on the analyser this looks like a stutter in the clock and can be mistaken for a dropped bit. It also means the 595 cascade path gets extra slack at byte boundaries but not inside a byte, so a chain that fails will fail on specific bit positions, not uniformly.
- 74HC595 fmax is specified at CL = 50 pF. TI's own 150 pF rows show tpd(RCLK->Q) degrading from 187 ns to 250 ns at 2 V and 37 ns to 50 ns at 4.5 V - about +33%. Your derated clock ceiling scales the same way once the shared net exceeds ~8 loads.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Prove the LPSPI root clock is 240 MHz, not 132 MHz: run SPI1.beginTransaction(SPISettings(4000000, MSBFIRST, SPI_MODE0)) and clock out 0x55 repeatedly. Measure the SCK period on the analyser. 240 MHz/60 = 4.000 MHz (250.0 ns period). If the root were 132 MHz you would get 132/33 = 4.000 MHz too - so use a discriminating request instead: ask for 7000000. With a 240 MHz root, d = 240/7 = 34 (check 240/34 = 7.06 > 7, so d=35) -> 6.857 MHz. With a 132 MHz root you would get 132/19 = 6.947 MHz. Those differ by 90 kHz, resolvable by averaging over 100 periods at 24 MSa/s.
- Measure the minimum SCK: SPISettings(100000), clock 16 bytes, measure the period. Expect 240/257 = 933.85 kHz (1.0708 us period), NOT 100 kHz. This single capture confirms both the clamp and the 240 MHz root simultaneously.
- Measure the inter-byte gap: SPI1.transfer(buf, 8) at 4 MHz. Look for a gap between byte boundaries of roughly half an SCK period (~125 ns, 3 samples at 24 MSa/s). Compare it against SPI1.transfer16() of the same data, which should show 16 continuous clocks with no gap. This proves the DBT = SCKDIV/2 behaviour and tells you which path to use for the 595 cascade.
- Verify RCLK ordering on the 595 chain: capture SCK, MOSI and RCLK simultaneously. Assert that (a) RCLK's rising edge occurs strictly after the last SCK edge, (b) the gap is >= 94 ns (>= 3 samples at 24 MSa/s), and (c) there are exactly 8*N SCK edges between consecutive RCLK rising edges for an N-chip chain. Any RCLK edge inside the SCK burst is the classic 'outputs are one clock ahead' bug.
- Verify /PL on the 165 chain: capture /PL, SCK and MISO. Assert (a) the /PL low pulse is >= 80 ns (>= 3 samples), (b) /PL returns high >= 100 ns before the first SCK edge, and (c) exactly 8*N SCK edges occur between /PL rising and the next /PL falling.
- Walking-1 bit-map test: drive a single 1 through the 595 chain, one position per frame, and photograph which output goes high each time. Do the same on the 165 by grounding one input at a time. This is the only reliable way to prove the byte order (first byte -> farthest 595) and the 165's H-first bit order without guessing.
- Find the real 3.3 V speed ceiling empirically: run the walking-1 test at 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 15 and 20 MHz and record the first frequency at which any bit lands in the wrong position. Note that above 6 MHz the analyser can no longer confirm the SCK waveform (fewer than 4 samples per bit), so at those rates trust the OUTPUT test (which is a static DC read of the 595 pins) rather than the clock capture. Derate the first failing frequency by 2x for temperature and part-to-part spread.
- Measure the actual digitalWriteFast CS pulse width - you cannot do it directly (3.3 ns vs a 41.7 ns sample period). Instead, loop N = 100000 toggle pairs, capture the total burst duration on the analyser or read ARM_DWT_CYCCNT before and after, and divide. Expect ~4 core cycles (6.67 ns) per full period at 600 MHz. Do the same to measure delayNanoseconds()'s real floor: time a loop of 100000 delayNanoseconds(0) calls with ARM_DWT_CYCCNT.
- Verify the ringing hypothesis before adding series resistors: probe SCK at the FARTHEST 74HC input (not at the Teensy). A 24 MSa/s digital analyser cannot see a 130 MHz ring directly, but it CAN see its consequence - clock an all-zeros pattern into a long chain and look for extra bits appearing. If a chain that works at 2 MHz fails at 8 MHz with extra shifted bits, that is double-clocking from ringing, and 68 ohm series resistors at the driver are the fix. Re-run the same test to confirm the fix.
- Capture with 4 channels at 24 MSa/s rather than 8, for any capture longer than a few milliseconds. An FX2-based 8-channel analyser at 24 MSa/s across all 8 channels is at the USB 2.0 bulk limit; dropped samples masquerade as missing clock edges and will send you chasing a hardware fault that does not exist.
- Channel budget for a full fabric capture (4 channels, safe): SCK, MOSI, RCLK, and one 595 output pin. Second capture (4 channels): SCK, MISO, /PL, and one 165 input pin. Do not try to capture everything at once.
- INA219 check, not LA: measure the fabric rail current with the outputs all-high vs all-low at 1 Hz toggle. Confirm you are under the 74HC595's +/-70 mA per-package VCC/GND limit and the 74HC165's tighter +/-50 mA limit. Also measure the delta when the fabric clock goes from 1 MHz to 8 MHz - dynamic current scales with Cpd (400 pF typ per 595) times VCC^2 times f, so 20 chips at 8 MHz and 3.3 V is roughly 20 * 400p * 3.3^2 * 8M = 700 mA-equivalent of switching current at peak. If your measurement is far from that estimate, one of the assumptions is wrong.

### Open questions for the builder

- Which Teensy 4.1 hardware serial port carries the already-built, already-tested ESP32-S3 UART link? If it is Serial1 (pins 0 and 1), then SPI1's default MISO (pin 1) and default hardware CS (pin 0) both collide with it, and you must call SPI1.setMISO(39) and use pin 38 or another free GPIO for chip select. I need the port number to finalise the SPI1 pin map.
- Is the 2.4 inch display on Teensy1 wired to SPI (pins 11/12/13) or to SPI1 (26/27/39)? This decides whether the 74HC165 chain can own a MISO line exclusively, and whether the fabric and the display can ever run at different SCK frequencies without a beginTransaction fight.
- Is the 2.4 inch display an ST7789 SPI TFT (as the BOM says) or something else? An ST7789 is write-only on SPI (no MISO), which would mean it can share MOSI/SCK with the fabric and never contend for MISO. If it is a module with a MISO pin, that changes the answer.
- Will BUILTIN_SDCARD be used on Teensy1? It costs nothing in SPI pins (it is SDIO on 42-47) but permanently rules out SPI2 and means pins 42-47 must never be repurposed.
- Do you want the fabric on SPI (11/12/13, includes the LED on SCK) or SPI1 (26/27/39, LED-free)? My recommendation is SPI1 for the fabric and SPI for the display, but that depends on the two questions above.
- How many 74HC595s and 74HC165s will sit on one shared SCK net in stack 1, and what is the total jumper length of that net? Chip count sets the capacitive load (10 pF max each) and length sets the inductance - together they set the real ringing frequency and the series resistor value.
- What is the worst-case ambient temperature inside the BENCH-TOWER enclosure? Every TI number above has a 25 C column and a -40 to +85 C column that is roughly 20-25% slower. If the enclosure runs warm, use the full-temperature figures.
- Will the fabric be powered from the same 3.3 V rail as the Teensy, and will it be powered before or after the Teensy comes out of reset? Teensy pins are inputs at reset, so any 74HC control input that is not externally pulled to its inactive level will float during the power-up window.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

No prompt injection was found in any fetched page or PDF. All content I read was ordinary technical material: PJRC store/pinout pages, the Teensy 4.1 pinout card PDFs (card11a_rev4_web.pdf, card11b_rev4_web.pdf), schematic41.png, raw source files from github.com/PaulStoffregen (SPI.h, SPI.cpp, core_pins.h, digital.c, startup.c, imxrt.h), TI datasheets SCLS041J and SCLS116H, the NXP IMXRT1060CEC datasheet, and two PJRC forum threads. Nothing in any of it contained instruction-shaped text, fake system-reminders, or attempts to alter commit attribution or my behaviour. Two things worth naming so you can discount them: (1) the WebSearch tool appends its own boilerplate line ("REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above...") to its results - that is the search tool's own output format, not content from any fetched page, and I treated it as such; (2) the previous research pass left several downloaded PDFs in this session's tool-results directory, which I reused rather than re-downloading - I verified their content matched the expected TI datasheets (correct literature numbers SCLS041J and SCLS116H and correct part numbers) before citing them.

## Teensy 4.1 (i.MX RT1062) I2C / LPI2C for a bank of 8 MCP23017 expanders — pins, real clock rates, timeouts, bus recovery, transaction arithmetic, yield() reentrancy, and pull-up sizing at 3.3 V

### Bottom line

On Teensy 4.1 all three usable I2C buses land on top-side header pins: Wire = SDA 18 / SCL 19, Wire1 = SDA 17 / SCL 16, Wire2 = SDA 25 / SCL 24; only Wire1's alternates (SDA 44 / SCL 45) are back-side SMT pads. Wire.setClock() is quantised to exactly three profiles (<400000 -> 100 kHz; 400000..999999 -> 400 kHz; >=1000000 -> 1 MHz), all derived from a 24.000 MHz LPI2C functional clock. The "runs ~7% fast" folklore is wrong and I can show where it comes from: 24 MHz / (CLKHI+CLKLO+2) = 428.6 kHz, but the i.MX RT1060 RM's real period formula includes SCL_LATENCY, giving nominal 100.840 kHz / 400.000 kHz / 1.000 MHz with zero rise time — and because SCL_LATENCY absorbs the actual SCL rise time, real buses run SLOWER than nominal, not faster (expect roughly 358–375 kHz on a 2.2k / ~130 pF bus). One MCP23017 GPIOA+GPIOB read is 45 SCL pulses = 2842 LPI2C cycles = 118.42 us at the ideal 400.000 kHz, or ~132 us at a realistic 358 kHz; eight chips is ~0.96–1.07 ms of bus time if the inter-transaction gap is the RM's (CLKLO+1), but ~2.2 ms if the MCFGR2[BUSIDLE] 150 us gap applies — that ambiguity is the single biggest unknown and must be measured. Wire::endTransmission() and requestFrom() both call yield() every poll iteration, which on Teensy 4 dispatches serialEvent()/serialEventN() handlers with no interrupt-context check, so any Wire access from inside a serial event handler will silently corrupt an in-flight transaction. There is no setWireTimeout() API; timeouts are hard-coded at 15.0 ms (LPI2C pin-low/stretch), 16 ms (wait_idle, which then attempts a private 9-pulse recovery with no STOP), and 50 ms (transaction abort).

### Facts

| Claim | Value | Confidence | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Wire (LPI2C1) SDA/SCL pins on Teensy 4.1 — no alternates exist in the library | SDA = pin 18, SCL = pin 19. Both are top-side through-hole header pins (front card, right-hand column, labelled A4/SDA and A5/SCL). Pad regs: pin18 = GPIO_AD_B1_01, pin19 = GPIO_AD_B1_00. Library table: {{18, 3\|0x10, &IOMUXC_LPI2C1_SDA_SELECT_INPUT, 1}, {0xff,...}} — the second slot is a terminator, so setSDA()/setSCL() have nothing to switch to. | certain | PaulStoffregen/Wire, WireIMXRT.cpp lines 421-430 (i2c1_hardware); PJRC Teensy 4.1 front pinout card https://www.pjrc.com/teensy/card11a_rev4_web.pdf;  |
| Wire1 (LPI2C3) SDA/SCL pins on Teensy 4.1 — two selectable pin pairs | Default SDA = pin 17, SCL = pin 16 (top-side headers, A3/TX4/SDA1 and A2/RX4/SCL1). Alternate SDA = pin 44, SCL = pin 45 — these are BACK-SIDE SMT pads (44 = CLK/CS2/SDA1, 45 = CMD/SCK2/SCL1) that are shared with the SD card socket signals. Select with Wire1.setSDA(44)/Wire1.setSCL(45). | certain | WireIMXRT.cpp lines 431-441 (i2c3_hardware, ARDUINO_TEENSY41 branch); PJRC Teensy 4.1 back card https://www.pjrc.com/teensy/card11b_rev4_web.pdf |

So setClock(50000), setClock(200000) and setClock(399999) all give the identical 100 kHz profile; setClock(400000) and setClock(999999) give the identical 400 kHz profile. | certain | WireIMXRT.cpp lines 483-516 |
| RM period formula for LPI2C SCL | t_SCL = (CLKHI + CLKLO + 2 + SCL_LATENCY) x (2^PRESCALE) LPI2C clock cycles, where SCL_LATENCY = ROUNDDOWN((2 + FILTSCL + SCL_RISETIME) / (2^PRESCALE)) and SCL_RISETIME = t_r(SCL)/t_LPI2C. Also t_LOW = (CLKLO+1) x 2^PRESCALE, t_HIGH = (CLKHI+1+SCL_LATENCY) x 2^PRESCALE, t_HD;STA = (SETHOLD+1) x 2^PRESCALE, t_SU;STA = t_SU;STO = (SETHOLD+1+SCL_LATENCY) x 2^PRESCALE. | certain | i.MX RT1060 Processor Reference Manual Rev. 3, 07/2021, section 47.3.1.4, Table 47-2 (p.2798) and Table 47-3 Synchronization Latency (p.2799) |

With rise time <41.7 ns: SCL_LATENCY = 4, t_SCL = 26+28+2+4 = 60 cycles = 2.5000 us -> f_SCL = 400.000 kHz EXACTLY. t_LOW = 29c = 1208.3 ns, t_HIGH = 31c = 1291.7 ns, t_HD;STA = 19c = 791.7 ns, t_SU;STA = t_SU;STO = 23c = 958.3 ns. | certain | WireIMXRT.cpp lines 495-502 + IMXRT1060RM Rev.3 Tables 47-2/47-3 |

Note t_LOW is measured on the wire from SCL falling through 0.3VDD to rising through 0.3VDD, so a real fall time plus the sub-0.3VDD part of the rise adds roughly 0.357 x RC (~93 ns at Rp_eff 2.0k / Cb 130 pF), which is what pulls it back over the line in practice. | certain | NXP UM10204 Rev. 7.0 (2021-10-01) Table 11 (t_LOW Fast-mode min 1.3 us); Microchip MCP23017/MCP23S17 DS20001952D Table 1-3 param 101; WireIMXRT.cpp CL |

The Arduino-core timeout API added in Arduino 1.8.13 is absent. | certain | PaulStoffregen/Wire WireIMXRT.h lines 43-90 |

The i.MX RT1060 datasheet specs RPU_22K at max 212 uA with Vin = 0 V, i.e. R >= 3.3 V / 212 uA = 15.6 kOhm; no minimum current is given, so the true value lies somewhere at or above 15.6 kOhm with 22 kOhm nominal. | certain | WireIMXRT.cpp line 8 (PINCONFIG); IMXRT1060RM Rev.3 SW_PAD_CTL PUS/DSE field encodings; i.MX RT1060 Industrial datasheet IMXRT1060IEC Rev.4 04/2024, R |

### Implementation guidance

- Bus assignment: put the 8-chip MCP23017 bank on Wire (SDA 18 / SCL 19) — top-side headers, adjacent, no alternates to get confused about. Keep Wire2 (SDA 25 / SCL 24) free; remember its SCL is the LOWER pin number, the opposite convention to Wire and Wire1. Do not use pins 44/45 (Wire1 alternates) — they are back-side SMT pads shared with the SD socket.
- Init order matters: Wire.begin(); then Wire.setClock(400000); — in that order, because begin() internally calls setClock(100000) after resetting MCR. Re-apply setClock after ANY call to begin(). Wire.end() is a no-op on this core, so it is not a valid way to release the pins.
- Do not pass a value below 400000 to setClock() expecting anything between: setClock(400000) is the ONLY way to get the 400 kHz profile. setClock(399999) silently gives you ~100 kHz. Consider a compile-time constant and a static_assert-style guard.
- Read GPIOA+GPIOB in one transaction with the built-in internal-address form, which emits exactly S/addrW/0x12/Sr/addrR/data/data/P:   uint8_t n = Wire.requestFrom((uint8_t)addr, (uint8_t)2, (uint32_t)0x12, (uint8_t)1, (uint8_t)1);   if (n == 2) { a = Wire.read(); b = Wire.read(); } The equivalent long form is: Wire.beginTransmission(addr); Wire.write(0x12); if (Wire.endTransmission(false) != 0) -> error; Wire.requestFrom(addr, 2, true);
- Verify IOCON before trusting the 2-byte read: the single-transaction GPIOA+GPIOB read only works with IOCON.BANK = 0 and IOCON.SEQOP = 0 (both POR defaults). If your init writes IOCON, write it as BANK=0, MIRROR=1, SEQOP=0, ODR=1 — note ODR=1 makes INT open-drain and overrides the INTPOL bit. Read IOCON back and assert it, because a glitched write to IOCON at 0x0A/0x0B during a BANK transition is a known way to end up in an unexpected bank.
- Add an application-level reentrancy guard around every Wire access, because Wire is not reentrant and endTransmission()/requestFrom() call yield() on every poll iteration:   static volatile bool i2c_busy = false;   // enter: if (i2c_busy) return I2C_BUSY; i2c_busy = true;  ... exit: i2c_busy = false; Enter/exit with interrupts briefly masked (__disable_irq()/__enable_irq()) around the test-and-set.
- Alternative, stronger fix if you do not need serialEvent dispatch during I2C: define your own yield in the sketch. It is declared weak, so this compiles and fully overrides the core version:   extern "C" void yield(void) { } The cost is that serialEvent()/serialEventN() are then never dispatched anywhere, so you must poll Serial1.available() in loop() yourself. At 921600 baud (~11 us/byte) a 132 us I2C transaction accumulates ~12 bytes, well inside the default HardwareSerial RX buffer.
- Never call Wire from an ISR on this core. yield() has an unimplemented '// TODO: do nothing if called from interrupt' and will dispatch serialEvent handlers from interrupt context. (EventResponder handlers are safe — runFromYield() checks IPSR — but the serial path is not.) Have the MCP23017 INT ISR set a flag only, and do the I2C read from loop().
- Write your own public bus-recovery routine — the library's force_clock() is private, never emits a STOP, and only runs after a 16 ms stall. For Wire on pins 18/19:  bool i2cRecover(uint8_t sdaPin, uint8_t sclPin) {   pinMode(sclPin, OUTPUT_OPENDRAIN); digitalWrite(sclPin, HIGH);   pinMode(sdaPin, OUTPUT_OPENDRAIN); digitalWrite(sdaPin, HIGH);   delayMicroseconds(10);   for (int i = 0; i < 9; i++) {     if (digitalRead(sdaPin) && digitalRead(sclPin)) break;   // released early     digitalWrite(sclPin, LOW);  delayMicroseconds(5);     digitalWrite(sclPin, HIGH); delayMicroseconds(5);   }   // manual STOP: SDA low while SCL high, then SDA high   digitalWrite(sdaPin, LOW);  delayMicroseconds(5);   digitalWrite(sclPin, HIGH); delayMicroseconds(5);   digitalWrite(sdaPin, HIGH); delayMicroseconds(5);   bool ok = digitalRead(sdaPin) && digitalRead(sclPin);   Wire.begin(); Wire.setClock(400000);   // re-mux pins and restore speed   return ok; }  Note pinMode(pin, OUTPUT_OPENDRAIN) writes pad = DSE(7)|ODE (drive ~21 Ohm, NO internal pull-up, no hysteresis) and sets mux = ALT5|SION, so recovery relies entirely on your external pull-ups. Then Wire.begin() restores the LPI2C mux via configSDApin()/configSCLpin() — and the setClock() after it is mandatory.
- Distinguish the endTransmission() return codes on this core when logging: 0 = success; 2 = NACK (the library cannot tell an address NACK from a data NACK — it returns 2 for both); 4 = arbitration lost, OR pin-low/clock-stretch timeout (MSR[PLTF]), OR the 50 ms generic timeout, OR wait_idle() failed after 16 ms + failed recovery, OR an empty tx buffer; 5 = LPI2C FIFO error (MSR[FEF]) — note the library deliberately does NOT try to send a STOP on FEF ('empirical evidence suggests NOT trying to send a STOP condition'). Treat 5 as 'bus state unknown, run i2cRecover()'.
- Budget the scan loop for blocking: a wedged bus costs up to 16 ms (wait_idle) + 50 ms (transaction) = 66 ms per chip. Across 8 chips that is >0.5 s of blocked loop(). Add your own elapsedMicros watchdog around each chip read, mark a failing chip 'offline', skip it for N cycles, and only run i2cRecover() once per scan pass rather than per chip.
- Optional: to disable the Teensy's internal 22k pull-ups on SDA/SCL after Wire.begin() (only do this if you have measured that they are causing a t_LOW violation), clear PKE/PUE in the pad register:   CORE_PIN18_PADCONFIG &= ~(IOMUXC_PAD_PKE | IOMUXC_PAD_PUE);   // SDA, GPIO_AD_B1_01   CORE_PIN19_PADCONFIG &= ~(IOMUXC_PAD_PKE | IOMUXC_PAD_PUE);   // SCL, GPIO_AD_B1_00 Do NOT clear IOMUXC_PAD_ODE — that would turn the pins into push-pull drivers and short the bus.
- Use one MCP23017 read helper and reuse it for all 8 chips; do not re-order the address pointer between chips. Do not enable IOCON.SEQOP=1 (byte mode) — it breaks the 2-byte read. Do not enable IOCON.DISSLW; leaving slew-rate control at its default is what keeps SDA fall time inside the 300 ns Fast-mode limit.

### Pitfalls

- The '~7% fast' claim in the brief is wrong, and I can name the exact wrong formula: 24 MHz / (CLKHI + CLKLO + 2) = 24e6/56 = 428.571 kHz is +7.14%. The RM's real formula divides by (CLKHI + CLKLO + 2 + SCL_LATENCY) = 60, giving exactly 400.000 kHz. Because SCL_LATENCY grows with the actual SCL rise time, a real bus runs BELOW 400 kHz, never above. Anyone who sized a timing budget assuming 428 kHz has the sign of the error backwards.
- Wire calls yield() inside endTransmission() and requestFrom(). If your firmware defines serialEvent1() for the ESP32-S3 link (very likely at 921600), that handler runs thousands of times per I2C transaction. If it ever touches Wire, the nested call runs wait_idle(), which breaks immediately on MBF (we already own the bus), then executes 'port->MSR = 0x00007F00' — wiping the OUTER transaction's SDF/NDF/PLTF status — and pushes its own START and address into the same TX FIFO. The two transactions interleave on the wire and the outer loop reports success on the inner transaction's STOP. Completely silent, and it will look like random MCP23017 data corruption. yield()'s 'running' flag prevents infinite recursion, NOT this first-level corruption.
- Wire::end() on IMXRT is an empty function. Code that does Wire.end() expecting the pins to be released, the clock gate to be turned off, or the peripheral to be reset gets none of those things — the LPI2C keeps driving pins 18/19.
- Wire.begin() calls setClock(100000) internally after resetting MCR. Any re-init (including inside a recovery routine) silently drops you from ~360 kHz to ~98 kHz unless you re-apply setClock(400000). A 4x scan-rate regression with no error and no log line.
- The Teensy 400 kHz profile's nominal t_LOW is 1208.3 ns against an I2C Fast-mode and MCP23017 minimum of 1300 ns — it is 92 ns short on paper. In practice the SCL fall time plus the sub-0.3VDD portion of the rise (~93 ns at Rp_eff 2.0k / 130 pF) pushes the measured value back over the line. That means making the pull-ups stronger or the bus shorter can push t_LOW BACK into violation. Counter-intuitive: do not 'improve' the bus by dropping to 1k.
- MCFGR3[PINLOW] gives a 15.0 ms clock-stretch timeout, but Wire's response to MSR[PLTF] is to clear the FIFOs and push a STOP command — not the RM-prescribed recovery of clearing MCR[MEN] or setting MCR[RST]. If the bus is genuinely held low by a stuck slave, the STOP cannot be issued either, and the peripheral can be left with MEN set and a jammed state machine. After any return code of 4 or 5, run your own recovery; do not just retry.
- The 16 ms wait_idle timeout is the ONLY path that reaches force_clock(). Nothing calls the recovery on a NACK, a FIFO error, or a PLTF — only on a busy-bus stall. So the common wedge (a slave holding SDA low mid-byte after a reset) is not auto-recovered if BBF happens to be clear.
- IOCON.BANK: if BANK is ever set to 1, GPIOA moves to 0x09 and GPIOB to 0x19 — no longer adjacent — and the 2-byte sequential read returns GPIOA followed by whatever is at 0x0A (IOCON). Since the IOCON register itself lives at a different address in each bank (0x0A/0x0B in BANK=0, 0x05/0x15 in BANK=1), a partially-completed BANK write is a well-known way to get an expander into a state your init code cannot address. Always read IOCON back and assert its value.
- IOCON.SEQOP=0 with BANK=0 has a special documented behaviour that looks like the sequential read but is not: 'Byte mode with IOCON.BANK = 0 causes the address pointer to toggle between associated A/B register pairs.' If SEQOP is set to 1 (byte mode) your 2-byte read still returns GPIOA then GPIOB by the pair-toggle path — so the bug will not show up on a 2-byte read, but a 3-byte read would return GPIOA/GPIOB/GPIOA instead of GPIOA/GPIOB/OLATA. Do not extend the read length without re-checking SEQOP.
- 1.7 MHz mode on the MCP23017 requires VDD 4.5-5.5 V. At your 3.3 V rail it is unavailable, and the Teensy '1 MHz' profile is out of spec anyway (its t_LOW is 458.3 ns against the Fast-mode Plus 500 ns minimum). 400 kHz is the ceiling for this bank; do not chase 1 MHz.
- MCP23017 A0/A1/A2 'must be externally biased' — there are no internal pulls. Floating address pins give an address that changes with hand capacitance. Eight chips means eight sets of three straps; a single missed strap creates a duplicate address that shows up as one chip mirroring another's data rather than as a NACK.
- The Wire library's internal 22 kOhm pull-ups are on and invisible. Anyone computing rise time from the external 2.2k alone gets 242 ns instead of the true 220 ns, and anyone who removes the external resistors entirely will find the bus 'works' at 100 kHz on 22k alone and then fails intermittently at 400 kHz. Measure with the parallel value.
- endTransmission() returns 2 for both address NACK and data NACK — the source has the explicit comment 'TODO: how to tell address from data'. A bus scan that treats 2 as 'device absent' will misreport a present-but-wedged device.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Measure the real f_SCL (this is the master check for everything else). Capture SCL on one LA channel at the full 24 MS/s. Measure across a whole 45-pulse transaction rather than one period: time from the 1st SCL falling edge to the 45th SCL falling edge, divide by 44. Expected 2.50 us/period if rise time is negligible, 2.79 us if rise is ~313 ns. Anything ABOVE 400.0 kHz falsifies the RM formula; anything at 428 kHz would vindicate the '7% fast' folklore. My prediction: 355-378 kHz.
- Back out the SCL rise time and hence Cb from that measurement, since an 8-channel digital LA cannot see an analogue edge. Compute period_cycles = round(measured_period_ns / 41.6667). Then SCL_LATENCY = period_cycles - 56, and SCL_RISETIME = SCL_LATENCY - 4 cycles, so t_r(to the pad's trip point) = (period_cycles - 60) x 41.67 ns. Then Cb = t_r / (1.204 x Rp_eff) if you assume a 0.7VDD trip, or t_r / (0.693 x Rp_eff) for a 0.5VDD trip — quote both as a range. Cross-check by repeating the whole measurement with the external resistor changed from 2.2k to 1.5k: Rp_eff goes 2.00k -> 1.404k, so t_r should scale by 0.70 and period_cycles should drop by about 3.
- Measure t_LOW directly against the 1.3 us Fast-mode / MCP23017 minimum. At 24 MS/s each sample is 41.67 ns, so 1.3 us is 31.2 samples — resolvable to about +/-3%. Count samples from an SCL falling edge to the next rising edge, on a mid-transaction bit (not one adjacent to a START or STOP). Nominal is 29 cycles = 1208 ns = 29 samples; measured should read a few samples longer because of fall time and the sub-threshold part of the rise. If you measure fewer than 31 samples you are out of spec and should NOT reduce the pull-ups further.
- Settle the t_BUF / BUSIDLE question — do this one first, it is worth 2x on scan rate. Put SCL on ch0 and SDA on ch1, run a tight back-to-back loop reading two different chips, and measure from the STOP (SDA rising while SCL high) to the next START (SDA falling while SCL high). Two possible answers: ~1.2 us (RM 47.3.1.2, (CLKLO+1) = 29 cycles) or ~152 us (MCFGR2[BUSIDLE] = 3600, per the empirical teensy4_i2c finding). There is no middle ground, so the reading is unambiguous.
- Verify the frame shape and the repeated START. Decode SDA+SCL with the LA's I2C decoder and confirm exactly: START, 0x4n W, ACK, 0x12, ACK, repeated START (no STOP between), 0x4n R, ACK, GPIOA, ACK from master, GPIOB, NACK from master, STOP. Count 45 SCL pulses between START and STOP. If you see a STOP before the second address byte, endTransmission(false) is not being used and you are paying for two full transactions.
- Time the whole 8-chip scan. Toggle a spare Teensy GPIO high at the top of the scan loop and low at the bottom, capture it on ch2 alongside SCL. Expected 0.96-1.07 ms of pure bus time; anything materially over that is firmware overhead (most likely yield() dispatching serialEvent handlers). Then rebuild with 'extern "C" void yield(void) { }' and re-measure the same signal — the difference is exactly the yield cost per scan.
- Prove or disprove the yield() reentrancy hazard empirically. Toggle a second GPIO inside serialEvent1() and capture it alongside SCL. If that pin toggles while SCL is actively clocking, yield() is dispatching your serial handler mid-transaction and the reentrancy guard is mandatory. If it only toggles between transactions, the risk is lower but still present.
- Confirm the setClock quantisation on the wire. Build three times with setClock(200000), setClock(399999) and setClock(400000) and measure the period each time. Prediction: the first two are byte-identical at 238 x 41.67 ns = 9.92 us (about 100.8 kHz, or ~97.6 kHz with real rise time), and only the third jumps to ~2.5-2.8 us. This directly demonstrates the cliff.
- Verify the recovery routine. Deliberately wedge the bus by holding SDA low with a jumper to GND while a transaction runs, then release it, and capture the recovery: you should see exactly up to 9 SCL pulses at ~100 kHz (5 us low, 5 us high) followed by your explicit manual STOP (SDA low, SCL high, SDA high). The stock library's private force_clock() emits the 9 pulses but NO STOP — that missing STOP is precisely what you are checking your own routine adds.
- Check DC with an INA219 rather than the LA: with the bus idle (both lines high) the pull-ups draw nothing; with SCL held low the current through the effective pull-up should read 3.3 V / Rp_eff = 1.65 mA for a 2.2k external in parallel with the Teensy's internal 22k. If you measure closer to 1.5 mA, the internal pull-up is weaker than 22k; if closer to 2.6 mA, there are extra pull-ups on the MCP23017 breakouts that you have not accounted for.

### Open questions for the builder

- Does the 8-chip bank sit on the same physical breadboard as the Teensy, or on a separate board with a ribbon between? A ribbon adds roughly 40-70 pF/m per conductor and changes the pull-up answer materially — the 2.2k value is only good to Cb = 177 pF total.
- Do the MCP23017 modules you have (bare DIP chips, or breakout boards?) already carry pull-ups on SDA/SCL? Many Chinese MCP23017 breakouts ship with 10k on-board. Eight of those in parallel is 1.25k, which combined with your 2.2k and the Teensy's 22k gives ~780 Ohm — below the 967 Ohm Rp(min) and out of spec. If they are breakouts, please read the silkscreen or ohm it out.
- Does your existing Teensy1 firmware define serialEvent1() (or any serialEventN / serialEvent) for the ESP32-S3 UART link? This determines whether Wire's internal yield() actually dispatches anything, and therefore whether the reentrancy hazard is live or dormant.
- Is any Wire access currently made from inside an ISR, an IntervalTimer callback, or an EventResponder yield handler? Specifically: does the MCP23017 INT handler do the register read itself, or just set a flag?
- What is the actual physical topology of SDA and SCL across the 8 chips — a single daisy chain down one breadboard rail, or a star from the Teensy? A star with 8 stubs behaves worse than a chain at the same total wire length.
- Is IOCON written during your MCP23017 init, and if so with what byte and to which address (0x0A or 0x0B)? I need this to confirm BANK=0/SEQOP=0 survive.
- What is the required scan rate for the input bank? If ~1 kHz is enough, 400 kHz polling is fine as-is. If you need more, the answer is not a faster clock but MIRROR'd interrupts plus reading only the chips that flagged.
- 2.2 kOhm at what tolerance and what do you actually have in the passive assortment — 2.2k, 2.4k, or only 1k/4.7k/10k? The usable window here is roughly 1.0k to 2.7k effective, and 4.7k external (3.87k effective) is too weak: t_r would be 427 ns at 130 pF, over the 300 ns limit.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

None. All content I read came from primary sources fetched directly: raw.githubusercontent.com files from PaulStoffregen/Wire and PaulStoffregen/cores, PDFs from pjrc.com (IMXRT1060RM_rev3.pdf, card11a/card11b pinout cards), nxp.com (UM10204.pdf, IMXRT1060IEC.pdf), ww1.microchip.com (DS20001952 MCP23017 datasheet), and two markdown docs from Richard-Gemmell/teensy4_i2c. I found no instruction-shaped text, no fake system-reminders, and nothing attempting to alter commit attribution or any other behaviour. One thing worth naming so it is not mistaken for an injection later: the WebSearch tool's own result envelope appends a line reading "REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks." That text is emitted by the search tool wrapper itself, not by any fetched web page, and I treated it as tool framing rather than as page content or as an instruction from the pages.

---

## Teensy 4.1 (i.MX RT1062) complete pin budget: 8 UARTs, ADC, and every conflict — allocation for the Bench One stack-1 hub

### Bottom line

All eight LPUARTs, the analog map, the SPI/I2C maps and the XBAR list were taken from PJRC's own source of truth (cores/teensy4 + the SPI and Wire libraries + the rev-4 pinout cards), not from third-party pinout blogs, and they cross-check each other exactly (e.g. digitalPinHasPWM() matches the PWM marks on the card for all 55 pins). The headline result for this build: Serial2 (RX 7 / TX 8), Serial7 (RX 28 / TX 29) and Serial8 (RX 34 / TX 35) are the only three UART pairs that cost zero analog pins, zero I2C buses, zero SPI pins, no LED, no SD socket and no bottom-pad soldering — so Link A = Serial2, Link C = Serial7, Link D = Serial8, and all 16 remaining analog inputs plus Wire1 and Wire2 stay free for the second stack. Ethernet is a genuine non-conflict: it lives on GPIO_B1_04..B1_11, B1_14/15 and B0_14/15, none of which are Teensy pin numbers, and USB device/host are dedicated RT1062 USB pads. The two real traps are (a) Serial1's pins 0/1 are also SPI1's default CS and MISO, so SPI1 is only usable via setMISO(39)/CS 38, and (b) the 64-byte default RX buffer holds only 0.69 ms of 921600-baud traffic against a 1024-byte max frame, so addMemoryForRead() is mandatory on every link. On the ADC: Teensyduino runs ADCK at roughly 10 MHz (ADACK ~20 MHz with ADHSC=1, ADIV=/2), giving ~2.4 µs acquisition and ~5.0 µs per 12-bit conversion, and the binding limit on source impedance is not RC settling (~120 kΩ) but the ±1 µA pad leakage spec, which caps you at ~800 Ω for 1 LSB at 12 bits.

Scaling to Teensyduino's 12-bit Tsamp of 2.4 us gives (RAS + 7k) <= 2.4us / (9.4 x 2pF) = 128 kohm, i.e. RAS <= ~120 kohm. LEAKAGE is the real limit: pad input current IIN = +/-1 uA max, so the DC error is 1 uA x RAS. For <= 1 LSB at 12 bits (3.3 V / 4096 = 0.806 mV) RAS <= ~806 ohm; for <= 1 LSB at 10 bits (3.22 mV) RAS <= ~3.2 kohm. | 921600 baud is not exactly 921600 on Teensy 4.1 | UART_CLOCK is hard-coded to 24000000 Hz. begin() searches OSR 4..32 for the smallest fractional error using err <= besterr (so the LARGEST OSR wins ties). For 921600 it selects OSR = 26, SBR = 1, giving an actual 24e6 / 26 = 923076.9 baud, +0.160 %. BOTHEDGE is only set when OSR <= 8, so it is off here. For reference 115200 also lands at +0.160 % (OSR 26, SBR 8), while 1000000 and 2000000 baud are exact (0.000 %). | certain | cores/teensy4/HardwareSerial.cpp begin() lines 110-167 (#define UART_CLOCK 24000000, the OSR search loop, LPUART_BAUD_OSR/SBR/BOTHEDGE) |
| UART FIFO and software buffer sizes, and the RTS watermarks | Hardware: all LPUARTs have a fixed 4-byte TX FIFO and 4-byte RX FIFO; the core sets RXWATER = 2 and TXWATER = 2. Software buffers: Serial1 TX 64 / RX 64; Serial2..Serial8 TX 40 / RX 64 bytes. RTS watermarks are computed as rts_low = total - 38 and rts_high = total - 24; with the default 64-byte RX buffer RTS de-asserts (goes HIGH = pause) once 40 bytes are buffered and re-asserts (LOW = ready) when <= 26 remain. RTS is active LOW. At 921600 baud one byte is 10.85 us, so 64 bytes = 0.69 ms of slack and the 24-byte headroom above the high watermark gives the peer 260 us to stop. | certain | cores/teensy4/HardwareSerial1..8.cpp (SERIALn_TX/RX_BUFFER_SIZE defines); HardwareSerial.cpp begin() (rx_water/tx_water, FIFO comment citing IMXRT1060 |
| addMemoryForRead / addMemoryForWrite limits | "The buffer array must be a global or static variable. Maximum total buffer size is 64K." addMemoryForRead() sets rx_buffer_total_size_ = rx_buffer_size_ + length and recomputes both RTS watermarks, so hardware flow control scales with the enlarged buffer automatically. | certain | https://www.pjrc.com/teensy/td_uart.html; cores/teensy4/HardwareSerial.cpp addMemoryForRead() lines 454-468 |
| Interrupt priorities that matter for this firmware | startup.c sets EVERY NVIC interrupt to priority 128 at boot; SysTick and PendSV are set to 32 (SCB_SHPR3 = 0x20200000). HardwareSerial::begin() then raises each LPUART IRQ to priority 64. attachInterrupt() does NOT change priority, so all GPIO interrupts stay at 128 and share a single vector, IRQ_GPIO6789. Lower number = higher priority. | certain | cores/teensy4/startup.c line 136 and 239; cores/teensy4/HardwareSerial1..8.cpp (#define IRQ_PRIORITY 64); cores/teensy4/interrupt.c attachInterrupt()/ |
| Wire / Wire1 / Wire2 pin map (and what does not exist) | Wire = LPI2C1, SDA 18, SCL 19 (no alternates). Wire1 = LPI2C3, SDA 17 or 44, SCL 16 or 45 (44/45 are inside the SD socket). Wire2 = LPI2C4, SDA 25, SCL 24 (no alternates). There is NO Wire3 on Teensy 4.1 - the LPI2C2 (pins 40/41) definition is compiled only for ARDUINO_TEENSY_MICROMOD. WIRE_INTERFACES_COUNT = 3. | certain | PaulStoffregen/Wire WireIMXRT.cpp i2c1_hardware/i2c3_hardware/i2c4_hardware and the #if defined(ARDUINO_TEENSY_MICROMOD) guard on Wire3; cores/teensy4 |

NOT PWM: 16, 17, 20, 21, 26, 27, 30, 31, 32, 34, 35, 38, 39, 40, 41, 48, 49, 50, 52, 53. | certain | cores/teensy4/pins_arduino.h digitalPinHasPWM() macro for ARDUINO_TEENSY41; independently confirmed by the PWM marks on PJRC card11a/card11b rev4 (eve |

Separately, indices 0-13 of the table are NOT digital pins 0-13: they duplicate A0..A13 for Arduino compatibility, so analogRead(0) reads A0 (pin 14), analogRead(10) reads A10 (pin 24), and so on. Digital pins 0-13 have no ADC connection at all. Pins 28-37 map to 255 and return 0. | certain | cores/teensy4/analog.c, pin_to_channel[] and analogRead() |

### Implementation guidance

- FINAL ALLOCATION - Link A (Luckfox Pico Mini B) = Serial2, RX pin 7, TX pin 8. Justification against the matrix: pins 7/8 are GPIO_B1_01 and GPIO_B1_00, which touch nothing - no analog channel, no I2C bus, no SPI signal, not the LED, not the SD socket, not an Ethernet pad, not a bottom pad. Their only other functions are I2S1 (OUT1A/IN1) and XBAR inputs IN15/IN14, neither of which you use. They are adjacent on the left edge immediately below Serial1's 0/1, so the two 'upstream' links leave the board side by side.
- FINAL ALLOCATION - Link C (Teensy2 worker) = Serial7, RX pin 28, TX pin 29. Justification: pins 28/29 are GPIO_EMC_32 and GPIO_EMC_31; the only alternate function either has is FlexPWM. No analog, no I2C, no SPI, no CAN (CAN3 is on 30/31, not 28/29), no SD, no Ethernet, no bottom pad. Adjacent on the left edge.
- FINAL ALLOCATION - Link D (E32R40T display node) = Serial8, RX pin 34, TX pin 35. Justification: pins 34/35 are GPIO_B1_13 and GPIO_B1_12, adjacent on the right edge. No analog, no I2C, no SPI, no PWM, no LED, and they are NOT Ethernet RMII pads (Ethernet uses B1_04..B1_11 and B1_14/15). Serial8's alternates (RX 48, CTS 50) are QSPI bottom pads you will never solder, so nothing is lost by ignoring them. Note Serial8 TX has no alternate at all - pin 35 is fixed.
- REJECTED and why, so the choice is auditable: Serial3 (15/14) costs A0 and A1. Serial4 (16/17) costs A2, A3 AND the whole Wire1 bus. Serial5 (21/20) costs A6, A7 and its alternates live in the SD socket. Serial6 (25/24) costs A10, A11 AND the whole Wire2 bus. Choosing Serial2 + Serial7 + Serial8 instead costs zero analog pins and zero I2C buses - all 16 remaining analog inputs (A0-A3, A6-A17) and both Wire1 and Wire2 stay available for stack 2 and for the 74HC fabric.
- Bring the links up in this order, and never call attachCts() before begin(): Serial2.begin(921600, SERIAL_8N1); then, only if you want hardware flow control, Serial2.attachRts(<any free pin>); Serial2.attachCts(2). attachCts() returns false if the LPUART clock gate is still off, which is exactly the state before begin().
- Enlarge every link's RX buffer before begin(). Declare static uint8_t rxA[2048], rxC[2048], rxD[1024]; then Serial2.addMemoryForRead(rxA, sizeof(rxA)); Serial7.addMemoryForRead(rxC, sizeof(rxC)); Serial8.addMemoryForRead(rxD, sizeof(rxD)); before the matching begin(). The buffers must be global or static and total no more than 64 KB. Your protocol's 1024-byte max frame is 11.1 ms of wire time at 921600 baud, and the stock 64-byte buffer holds only 0.69 ms.
- If you add hardware flow control, assign CTS to XBAR pins with DIFFERENT XBAR inputs, one per port: Serial1 CTS = pin 2 (XBAR IN06), Serial2 CTS = pin 3 (IN07), Serial7 CTS = pin 4 (IN08), Serial8 CTS = pin 33 (IN09). Never pick two of {0, 5, 37} (all IN17), or {1, 36} (IN16), or {2, 43} (IN06), or {3, 42} (IN07), or {4, 47} (IN08), or {33, 46} (IN09) at the same time. RTS is plain GPIO, so use any free pins (e.g. 5, 6, 9, 10) with attachRts().
- Invert every XBAR CTS input. The native CTS pins (19, 43, 50) follow the normal convention - high means pause. An XBAR CTS is backwards: the Teensy transmits while the pin is HIGH and pauses while it is LOW. If the peer drives a conventional active-low /CTS, put one gate of a 74HC04 (VCC = 3.3 V) between the peer and the Teensy pin. Do not try to fix this in software; the LPUART TXCTSE gate is hardware.
- Do not use SPI1 as-is. Its defaults are CS = pin 0 and MISO = pin 1, which are the tested ESP32-S3 Serial1 pins. If you ever need a second SPI bus, call SPI1.setMISO(39) and use pin 38 (or any GPIO) as CS - MOSI 26 and SCK 27 are unaffected. SPI2 (42-45 / 49-54) is inside the SD socket and on the bottom pads; treat it as nonexistent.
- Never use LED_BUILTIN (pin 13) for status while SPI0 is active - pin 13 IS the SPI0 SCK line. Put your heartbeat LED on a free pin (e.g. 6, 9 or 10 if 10 is not a chip select) with its own series resistor.
- Analog scan with a 74HC4051: write S0/S1/S2 (and hold E low), delayMicroseconds(2), then analogRead(). Two microseconds covers the 4051's turn-on (interpolated <= ~350 ns at 3.3 V) plus about 470 ns of RC settling on the ~40 pF common node. Use the E (enable) pin as a global deselect and take it high before changing S0-S2, exactly as you already do with the 74HC138.
- Do not put a filter capacitor larger than about 100 pF on the 4051 common (Z) pin. Anti-alias filtering belongs on each individual Y channel, before the switch. A 100 nF on the common node gives a 130 us time constant and about 1.2 ms to settle, which shows up as one channel bleeding into the next - a plausible-looking wrong reading, not an obvious fault.
- Keep total analog source impedance (sensor output + 4051 RON + wiring) under 1 kohm if you use analogReadRes(12), or under 3 kohm at the default 10 bits. The limit is the +/-1 uA pad leakage, not RC settling.
- Call analogReadAveraging() only with 4, 8, 16 or 32. Anything below 4 disables averaging entirely rather than averaging that count, and anything above 32 clamps to 32. Call analogReadRes() only with 8, 10 or 12 - every other value silently takes the 12-bit branch.
- Never call analogRead() with a bare small integer. analogRead(7) reads A7 (pin 21), not digital pin 7. Always write analogRead(A7) or analogRead(21). Also never call analogRead(42): the bounds check is off by one and it reads past the end of the channel table.
- For any fabric input with slow edges - a 74HC165 QH on a long run, an open-drain MCP23017 /INT with an external pull-up - do not use pinMode(pin, INPUT). That mode leaves the pad's Schmitt trigger off. Use INPUT_PULLUP if you want the internal 22 kohm pull-up as well, or INPUT_DISABLE if you already have the external pull-up and only want the hysteresis.
- Leave pinMode()'s default slow slew rate alone on the 74HC fabric. pinMode() sets DSE(7) (4 mA guaranteed at 0.45 V) with SRE clear and SPEED = 0, which is the low-ringing choice for long parallel runs. Only reach for IOMUXC_PAD_SRE if a measured edge is genuinely too slow for a 165/595 setup time.
- Do not power the 74HC/MCP23017 fabric from the Teensy's 3.3V pin. That rail is rated 250 mA max. Give the fabric its own 3.3 V regulator, tie the grounds at one point, and do not back-feed the Teensy 3.3V pin.
- Budget for a second-stack expansion using the ports you have left free: Serial3 (15/14), Serial4 (16/17, gives up Wire1), Serial5 (21/20) and Serial6 (25/24, gives up Wire2). Prefer Serial3 and Serial5 first, since they only cost analog pins and no bus.

### Pitfalls

- analogRead(n) for n in 0..13 does NOT read digital pin n - it reads An. analogRead(7) returns A7 (pin 21). Once pin 7 is Link A's RX, a call to analogRead(7) still returns a perfectly plausible number from an entirely different pin.
- analogReadAveraging(2) and (3) silently DISABLE hardware averaging instead of averaging 2 or 3 samples. Noise roughly doubles versus the boot default of 4, and nothing reports an error.
- analogReadRes(14) or analogReadRes(16) silently configures 12-bit mode. The code has no validation - anything that is not 8 or 10 falls into the 12-bit branch.
- The ADC conversion clock is a free-running RC oscillator (ADACK, typ 20 MHz with ADHSC=1, divided by 2), not a crystal-derived clock. NXP gives only a typical value, no min/max. Any firmware that times its ADC schedule by counting analogRead() calls will drift with temperature and between chips. Gate scans on IntervalTimer or micros().
- SPI1's factory defaults are CS = pin 0 and MISO = pin 1 - the exact pins the tested ESP32-S3 link occupies. Calling SPI1.begin() without setMISO(39) will re-mux pin 1 away from Serial1 TX and kill the radio link, with no error message.
- Serial4 and Wire1 are the same two pins (16/17), and Serial6 and Wire2 are the same two pins (25/24). Enabling one silently re-muxes the pads out from under the other; the I2C bus just stops acknowledging.
- The onboard LED is pin 13, which is SPI0 SCK. digitalWrite(13, HIGH) for a status blink while SPI is active fights the SPI peripheral for the pad, and the LED's load sits on the clock line permanently.
- pinMode(pin, INPUT) leaves the pad's 250 mV Schmitt hysteresis DISABLED on Teensy 4. A slow edge from a 74HC165 at the end of a long ribbon can produce multiple counted transitions. INPUT_PULLUP, INPUT_PULLDOWN and INPUT_DISABLE all enable hysteresis; plain INPUT does not.
- A filter capacitor on a 74HC4051 common pin turns channel-to-channel crosstalk into readings that look like real, slightly-wrong data. With 100 nF and 1.3 kohm of source you need 1.2 ms to settle; at a 2 us settle delay every channel reads as a weighted average of itself and its predecessor.
- 921600 baud is actually 923077 baud on Teensy 4.1 (OSR 26, SBR 1 from a 24.000 MHz UART clock, +0.160 %). Harmless for the link, but a logic-analyser capture will measure 10.833 us frames and 1.0833 us bit cells - do not chase that as a fault.
- The default 64-byte RX buffer holds 0.69 ms of 921600-baud traffic against a protocol whose maximum frame is 11.1 ms of wire time. An SD write or a display refresh in loop() will drop bytes mid-frame; the CRC catches it, so it presents as an intermittent frame-error rate rather than an obvious failure.
- attachCts() returns false, silently, if it is called before begin() - it tests the LPUART clock gate. Flow control then just never happens.
- XBAR CTS runs backwards from the native CTS pins. Wire a conventional active-low /CTS to an XBAR pin and the Teensy transmitter stays gated off permanently, which looks exactly like a dead UART.
- Each serial port can make only ONE XBAR connection. Assign an XBAR pin to a port's CTS and you can no longer use an XBAR pin for that port's RX, and vice versa. Two ports assigned XBAR pins that share the same XBAR input (0/5/37, 1/36, 2/43, 3/42, 4/47, 33/46) will silently fight.
- analogRead(42) reads one byte past the end of pin_to_channel[] - the bounds check is `pin > sizeof(...)` where it should be `>=`. It will return whatever byte follows the table, interpreted as an ADC channel.
- Serial5's alternate pins (46/47) and CTS (43) are inside the SD card socket, and Serial8's alternate RX (48) and CTS (50) are QSPI memory pads. Selecting them via setRX()/attachCts() will appear to succeed in software while nothing is physically connected.
- pinMode(An, INPUT_PULLUP) followed by analogRead(An) leaves the 22 kohm pull-up engaged - analogRead() only clears the keeper, and deliberately preserves a user-set pull. Every reading gets dragged toward 3.3 V.
- The 3.3V pin is rated 250 mA. A fabric of 12 MCP23017s plus 20 shift registers with loaded outputs will brown out the regulator; the symptom is ADC gain error and random resets, not an obvious short.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Prove the UART pin map: with the 8-channel analyser at 24 MSa/s, put probes on pins 8, 29 and 35 (the three TX lines) plus a common ground, run Serial2/7/8.begin(921600) and print a known byte (0x55) on each. Decode as async 8N1 at 921600 - you should see 10.833 us frames (923077 baud, +0.16 %), not 10.851 us. Any port that shows nothing was mis-selected.
- Prove 923077 baud rather than 921600: capture 200 consecutive bit cells of a 0x55 pattern at 24 MSa/s (26 samples per bit) and measure the total span. 200 bits should take 216.67 us, not 217.01 us. The 0.16 % difference is 0.34 us over 200 bits - resolvable at 41.7 ns per sample.
- Prove Serial1 is untouched by the new links: probe pin 1 (Serial1 TX) while bringing up Serial2, Serial7 and Serial8, and confirm no glitch or re-mux transient. Then deliberately call SPI1.begin() and watch pin 1 go dead - that is the SPI1 MISO conflict, and it is the reason to call SPI1.setMISO(39) first.
- Prove the RTS watermarks: probe the RTS pin and the RX line. Blast 64+ bytes at 921600 into a link whose loop() is stalled, and confirm RTS goes HIGH after the 40th byte enters the buffer and returns LOW once the backlog falls to 26 bytes. With the stock 64-byte buffer the peer has 24 bytes = 260 us to react; time the interval from the RTS edge to the last byte the peer sends.
- Prove XBAR CTS polarity: attachCts() an XBAR pin, drive it LOW from a bench source and confirm the TX line stops mid-stream after the byte in flight; drive it HIGH and confirm transmission resumes. If the behaviour is reversed you are on a native CTS pin, not an XBAR one.
- Measure real analogRead() duration: set a spare GPIO high immediately before analogRead() and low immediately after, and capture at 24 MSa/s (41.7 ns resolution). Expect roughly 3.7 us per conversion at the default 10 bits with averaging off, about 5.0 us at 12 bits, and roughly 4x those with analogReadAveraging(4). A measurement well above these indicates the asynchronous ADC clock start-up penalty (ADACKEN is not set in analog_init).
- Measure the ADC acquisition window indirectly: run analogReadRes(8) then (10) then (12) with averaging off and compare the GPIO-bracketed durations. The ratios should be about 25 : 37 : 50 ADCK cycles, i.e. 2.5 us : 3.7 us : 5.0 us.
- Find the true 4051 settle time: wire Y0 to GND and Y1 to 3.3 V. Toggle the select lines between them with a GPIO marker on the analyser, sweep the delay before analogRead() from 0 to 5 us in 250 ns steps, and plot the reading. The delay at which the value stops changing is your real settle time; set the firmware constant to twice it.
- Measure 4051 RON at 3.3 V (the datasheet has no 3.3 V column): put a known 10.0 kohm resistor from 3.3 V into a channel and read the divider against a known load, or force a 1 mA current through one switch with a bench supply and measure the drop with a DMM. Expect somewhere between 150 and 400 ohm; whatever you get goes straight into your source-impedance budget.
- Quantify the leakage-driven source-impedance limit empirically: read the same 3.3 V/2 source through 0 ohm, 1 k, 10 k and 100 k series resistors at 12-bit with 32x averaging. The 1 uA spec predicts about 0, 1.2, 12 and 124 LSB of offset. If the 10 k point moves by ~12 LSB the model is confirmed and your 1 kohm design rule is justified.
- Confirm the 3.3 V rail holds under fabric switching: put an INA219 on the fabric supply and simultaneously probe a 74HC595 SRCLK burst on the analyser. Correlate rail droop with the burst. If ADC readings shift with fabric activity, that is ratiometric gain error, not ADC noise.
- Prove pins 2 and 3 really are free before committing them to RTS/CTS: toggle each at 1 MHz with the analyser attached and confirm clean 3.3 V edges with no contention from any peripheral you have enabled.
- Prove Ethernet is a non-conflict, if you ever fit the magjack: capture pins 7, 8, 28, 29, 34 and 35 while the Ethernet driver initialises. Nothing on those six pins should move - the RMII signals are on GPIO_B1_04..B1_11, which carry no Teensy pin number.

### Open questions for the builder

- How many analog inputs does stack 1 actually need, and at what source impedance? The allocation above leaves all 16 free analog pins untouched, but if the answer is 'none' then Serial3 or Serial5 become viable alternates and the whole matrix loosens.
- Do Link A, C and D need hardware RTS/CTS at all, or is the CRC-framed protocol plus a 2 KB software buffer sufficient? Flow control costs up to 6 more pins and forces a 74HC04 inverter on every XBAR CTS line.
- Is the Luckfox link 3.3 V CMOS on both ends with a shared ground and a direct wire, exactly like the tested ESP32-S3 link? If any level shifter is contemplated for it, confirm the rule (push-pull UART -> TXB0108, never TXS0108) still applies with the Luckfox's specific IO voltage.
- What is the maximum time loop() can block on stack 1 (SD write, display refresh, fabric scan)? That number, times 92160 bytes/second, is the required RX buffer size per link. Until it is known, 2048 bytes is a guess.
- Is the 2.4 inch display on Teensy1 an ST7789 SPI TFT or something else, and does it hang off SPI0? If it does, it needs CS + DC + RST = 3 more GPIO on top of the fabric's chip selects, and the pin budget above should reserve them explicitly.
- Will either QSPI memory footprint ever be populated with PSRAM? If yes, pins 48-54 are permanently gone, which removes Serial1's alternate RX/TX (52/53) and Serial8's alternate RX (48) and CTS (50). The recommendation above does not depend on them, but it should be stated as a fixed constraint.
- Will the SD socket be used? If yes, pins 42-47, SPI2 and Serial5's alternates are all permanently gone. Again the recommendation does not depend on them.
- How many 74HC138 chip-select outputs, 74HC595 latch lines and 74HC165 load lines does the fabric need from the Teensy directly (as opposed to through an MCP23017)? That determines whether the free set {4, 5, 6, 9, 10, 14-17, 20-27, 30-33, 36-41} is comfortable or tight.
- What is the fabric's total 3.3 V current draw with all outputs loaded? It has to be measured or estimated before deciding whether it can share any rail with the Teensy.
- Does the second stack's Teensy1 need to talk to stack 1's Teensy1 over a fifth UART? If so, reserve Serial3 (15/14) now rather than discovering later that A0/A1 were spent.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

No prompt injection was found in any fetched content this pass. I read: pjrc.com (pinout.html, teensy41.html, teensy41_pins.html, td_uart.html, card11a_rev4_web.pdf, card11b_rev4_web.pdf), raw.githubusercontent.com source files from PaulStoffregen/cores, PaulStoffregen/SPI, PaulStoffregen/Wire and ssilverman/QNEthernet, the NXP IMXRT1060IEC Rev. 4 datasheet PDF, and the Nexperia 74HC4051 Rev. 12 datasheet PDF. None contained instruction-shaped text, fake system-reminders, or any attempt to alter commit attribution or configuration. One thing worth naming so it is not mistaken for one: the WebSearch tool's own result blocks end with a line reading "REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks." That text comes from the search tool's output template, not from any fetched web page, and I treated it as tool formatting rather than as an instruction from page content. I also did not fetch any PJRC forum thread, precisely because forum posts are the most likely place for user-authored injected text; every claim above comes from a manufacturer datasheet, an official PJRC page, or PJRC's own published source code.

---

## 74HC138 addressable chip-select decode, 74HC373 transparent latch, 74HC04 polarity inversion, bus-contention interlock, and why the 74LS245 cannot be used at 3.3 V

### Bottom line

The 74HC138 has eight select codes and all eight assert an output, so there is no "nothing selected" address — deselection must come from an enable pin, and E3 (G1, pin 6) is the right one to wire to a Teensy GPIO. Neither TI (SCLS107G) nor Nexperia (Rev 10, 26 Feb 2024) tabulates any AC parameter at 3.3 V: the columns are 2.0 V, 4.5 V and 6.0 V only, so the only *guaranteed* bound at 3.3 V is the 2 V column (225 ns address→Y, 195 ns enable→Y over −40 to +85 °C, CL = 50 pF), and the realistic value sits near the 4.5 V column (45 ns / 39 ns). Budget 500 ns of settle per step until measured, then tighten. The 74HC373 is a level-sensitive transparent latch that captures on the LE falling edge, which means the '04 inverter sitting between the '138's active-low Y and the '373's active-high LE delays the capture instant — so firmware must hold the data bus valid for tpd(138 enable→Y) + tpd(04) + th(373) *after* the deselect write, not before it. Shoot-through is made impossible in hardware by deriving every /OE from one '138 (or one '138 tree), because the function table guarantees mutual exclusivity by construction; add 100–220 Ω series resistors to survive the unavoidable ns-scale overlap. The 74LS245 is unusable: it is only specified from 4.75 V to 5.25 V, and at 3.3 V its Darlington totem-pole output cannot reach the 2.31 V (0.7 × 3.3 V) VIH that both 74HC and the i.MX RT1062 require, while at 5 V its output high (typ 3.4 V, no maximum specified) sits at or above the Teensy's 3.6 V absolute maximum. Buy SN74HC245N, or SN74LVC245AN if any 5 V signal will ever touch the bus.

### Facts

| Claim | Value | Confidence | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| 74HC138 DIP-16 pinout (D, DB, J, N, NS, PW, W packages) — TI names G2A/G2B/G1, Nexperia names the same pins E1/E2/E3 | pin 1 = A (select LSB); pin 2 = B; pin 3 = C (select MSB); pin 4 = G2A / E1 (active-LOW enable); pin 5 = G2B / E2 (active-LOW enable); pin 6 = G1 / E3 (active-HIGH enable); pin 7 = Y7; pin 8 = GND; pin 9 = Y6; pin 10 = Y5; pin 11 = Y4; pin 12 = Y3; pin 13 = Y2; pin 14 = Y1; pin 15 = Y0; pin 16 = VCC | certain | TI SN54HC138/SN74HC138, SCLS107G (Oct 2021), §5 Pin Functions, p.3; cross-checked against Nexperia 74HC/HCT138 Rev 10 (26 Feb 2024) §5.1 Pinning, p.3 |
| 74HC138 complete enable truth table — all three enables must be simultaneously active or every output is HIGH | G1(E3)=X, G2A(E1)=H, G2B(E2)=X → all Y HIGH. G1=X, G2A=X, G2B=H → all Y HIGH. G1=L, G2A=X, G2B=X → all Y HIGH. Only G1=H AND G2A=L AND G2B=L enables decoding. | certain | TI SCLS107G Table 8-1 Function Table, p.10; Nexperia Rev 10 Table 3 Function table, p.4 |
| There is no 'all deselected' select code on a 74HC138 | 3 select inputs = 8 codes (CBA = 000..111), and the function table shows all 8 codes driving exactly one output LOW when enabled. No code is reserved for 'none'. | certain | TI SCLS107G Table 8-1, p.10 — the last 8 rows each contain exactly one 'L' in the output columns |
| 74HC138 outputs are actively driven HIGH when deselected — they are not high-impedance | Deselected state = VOH, min 3.84 V at VCC = 4.5 V, IOH = −4 mA (SN74HC138 over −40 to +85 °C); min 4.4 V at IOH = −20 µA | certain | TI SCLS107G §6.7 Electrical Characteristics: SN74HC138, p.6 |
| NEITHER TI NOR NEXPERIA PUBLISHES ANY 3.3 V AC COLUMN FOR THE 74HC138 | TI switching-characteristics tables have exactly three VCC columns: 2 V, 4.5 V, 6 V. Nexperia adds one extra typical-only entry at VCC = 5 V, CL = 15 pF. There is no 3.3 V row anywhere in either document. | certain | TI SCLS107G §6.9/§6.10, pp.6–7; Nexperia 74HC/HCT138 Rev 10 Table 7 Dynamic characteristics, p.7 |
| 74HC138 guaranteed tpd, address (A/B/C) → any Y, SN74HC138 over −40 to +85 °C, CL = 50 pF | VCC = 2 V: 225 ns max. VCC = 4.5 V: 45 ns max. VCC = 6 V: 38 ns max. (25 °C typicals: 67 / 18 / 15 ns) | certain | TI SCLS107G §6.10 Switching Characteristics: SN74HC138, p.7 (over-temperature) and §6.9, p.6 (25 °C typ) |
| 74HC138 guaranteed tpd, enable → any Y, SN74HC138 over −40 to +85 °C, CL = 50 pF | VCC = 2 V: 195 ns max. VCC = 4.5 V: 39 ns max. VCC = 6 V: 33 ns max. (25 °C typicals: 66 / 18 / 15 ns) | certain | TI SCLS107G §6.10, p.7; §6.9, p.6 |

ENGINEERING estimate: TI's Figure 6-1 (typical tpd vs VCC) reads approximately 25–30 ns typical at 3.3 V, CL = 50 pF, 25 °C. | Boot hazard: what a floating enable does at power-up | Before firmware configures GPIO, the Teensy pin driving G1/E3 is an input (high-Z), so the '138's G1 pin is floating. If it resolves HIGH while G2A/G2B are already low, the decoder ENABLES and asserts whichever output the (also floating or garbage) address selects — a real, driven, milliamp-capable LOW on a random /CS or /OE for the entire duration of the MCU boot. | certain | Threshold values: TI SCLS107G §6.3 (VIH/VIL scale as 0.7/0.3 × VCC). On every 74HC373: 10 kΩ from LE (pin 11) to GND, and 10 kΩ from /OE (pin 1) to +3.3 V. Justification: '138 input leakage is ±1 µA max over temperature → 10 kΩ gives a 10 mV worst-case offset, utterly safe against a 0.99 V VIL. When firmware later drives the pin, it fights only 3.3 V / 10 kΩ = 330 µA. RC settling with ~30 pF of input plus stray capacitance = 300 ns. | certain | Leakage: TI SCLS107G §6.7, p.6 (II ±1000 nA). DESELECTING drives Yn HIGH → LE LOW → THE CAPTURE HAPPENS HERE, delayed from the firmware's deselect write by tpd('138 enable→Y) + tpd('04). Required bus hold after the deselect write = tpd(138) + tpd(04) + th(373) = 39 + 24 + 13 = 76 ns at 4.5 V; = 195 + 120 + 26 = 341 ns at the 2 V guaranteed bound. Required setup before the select write = tsu(373) = 15 ns @ 4.5 V / 75 ns @ 2 V, plus the same '138+'04 chain. No firmware bug, no stuck bit, no half-written GPIO register can produce two simultaneous asserted /OE, because the decoder physically cannot emit two. | certain | TI SCLS107G Table 8-1, p.10 (every enabled row has exactly one 'L'); Nexperia Rev 10 Table 3, p.4, and §1 'Active LOW mutually exclusive outputs' |

Therefore across the whole 32- or 64-wide field, exactly one output is LOW at any instant when the master is enabled, and zero are LOW when it is not. | certain | Composition of TI SCLS107G Table 8-1 applied twice |
