### Implementation guidance

- WIRING, 74HC138 as a single-tier 1-of-8 chip-select decoder on Teensy1: pin 1 (A) -> Teensy CS_A; pin 2 (B) -> CS_B; pin 3 (C) -> CS_C; pin 4 (G2A/E1) -> GND directly (hard tie, no resistor); pin 5 (G2B/E2) -> GND directly; pin 6 (G1/E3) -> Teensy CS_EN, WITH a 10 kOhm resistor from pin 6 to GND; pin 8 -> GND; pin 16 -> +3.3 V with a 0.1 uF X7R ceramic within 5 mm of the pin. Outputs Y0..Y7 on pins 15,14,13,12,11,10,9,7 -> the eight /CS destinations. Add a 10 kOhm pull-up to +3.3 V on each downstream /CS at the slave end.
- PIN ALLOCATION RULE: put CS_A, CS_B and CS_C on Teensy 4.1 pins belonging to the SAME fast-GPIO port so all three address bits can be written in one store to the port's DR register. That collapses inter-bit skew from three digitalWriteFast calls to zero. It does NOT remove the need for break-before-make (the '138's internal decode skew remains), but it removes the largest term. Check the Teensy 4.1 pin-to-GPIO6/7/8/9 map before allocating.
- GPIO WRITE ORDERING: the Cortex-M7 store buffer posts GPIO writes. If you time anything from the instant of a pin change, insert a barrier so the write has actually retired before you start counting: __asm__ volatile("dsb" ::: "memory"); A read-back of the same GPIO DR register works equally well and is cheaper to reason about.
- THE SETTLE WAIT. Use Teensy's delayNanoseconds(). At 600 MHz one core cycle is 1.667 ns. Start at 500 ns per step -- that comfortably covers the 2 V guaranteed bound of 195-225 ns for a single '138 stage, plus the 100-220 Ohm series-resistor RC, plus slave /CS recovery. Do NOT tighten it below the measured value plus 3x margin. #define CS_SETTLE_NS 500
- BREAK-BEFORE-MAKE, exact sequence:  static inline void cs_barrier(void) { __asm__ volatile("dsb" ::: "memory"); }  void cs_deselect_all(void) {     digitalWriteFast(CS_EN, LOW);       // G1/E3 low -> ALL Y0..Y7 driven HIGH     cs_barrier();     delayNanoseconds(CS_SETTLE_NS);     // >= tpd(enable->Y) + slave /CS recovery }  void cs_select(uint8_t ch) {            // ch = 0..7     // 1. BREAK. Deselect BEFORE touching the address. This is the whole point.     digitalWriteFast(CS_EN, LOW);     cs_barrier();     delayNanoseconds(CS_SETTLE_NS);     // >= tpd(enable->Y): 195 ns bound / 39 ns @4.5V      // 2. Change the address only while globally deselected.     //    Decode glitches here are invisible: all outputs are held HIGH by the enable.     digitalWriteFast(CS_A, (ch >> 0) & 1u);     digitalWriteFast(CS_B, (ch >> 1) & 1u);     digitalWriteFast(CS_C, (ch >> 2) & 1u);     cs_barrier();     delayNanoseconds(CS_SETTLE_NS);     // >= tpd(A,B,C->Y): 225 ns bound / 45 ns @4.5V      // 3. MAKE.     digitalWriteFast(CS_EN, HIGH);     cs_barrier();     delayNanoseconds(CS_SETTLE_NS);     // >= tpd(enable->Y) before the first SCK edge }  NEVER write the address while CS_EN is HIGH. There is no legal ordering that avoids glitches other than this one.
- LATCH WRITE through '138 -> '04 -> '373 LE. The capture happens on the DESELECT, delayed by the '138 and the '04. Hold the bus across it:  void latch_write(uint8_t slot, uint8_t data) {     cs_deselect_all();                  // every LE is LOW: all latches opaque     bus_drive(data);                    // Teensy or '595 drives the shared 8-bit bus     delayNanoseconds(CS_SETTLE_NS);     // >= tsu(373) + tpd(138) + tpd(04)      cs_select(slot);                    // Yn LOW -> '04 -> LE HIGH: this latch transparent     delayNanoseconds(CS_SETTLE_NS);     // >= tw(LE high): 120 ns bound / 24 ns @4.5V      cs_deselect_all();                  // Yn HIGH -> '04 -> LE FALLS: CAPTURE HAPPENS HERE     delayNanoseconds(CS_SETTLE_NS);     // MUST hold the bus: >= tpd(138)+tpd(04)+th(373)                                         //   = 195+120+26 = 341 ns bound, 76 ns @4.5V     bus_release();                      // ONLY NOW may the bus change }  The single most common bug is calling bus_release() before that last delay. The capture has not occurred yet at that point.
- 1-of-32 MASTER TREE WIRING (recommended over the flat 4-chip version). Master '138: pin 1 (A) <- ADDR3, pin 2 (B) <- ADDR4, pin 3 (C) -> GND, pin 4 -> GND, pin 5 -> GND, pin 6 (G1) <- CS_EN GPIO with 10 kOhm to GND, pin 8 -> GND, pin 16 -> 3V3. Master Y0..Y3 (pins 15,14,13,12) -> leaf0..leaf3 pin 4 (G2A). Each leaf: pin 1/2/3 <- ADDR0/1/2 (bussed to all leaves), pin 5 -> GND, pin 6 -> +3.3 V (hard tie, this is a fixed enable and must not float), pin 8 -> GND, pin 16 -> 3V3 + 0.1 uF. Master Y4..Y7 unused: leave them open (they are outputs, not inputs -- they may float safely) but do NOT leave any leaf input open.
- SOFTWARE FOR THE TREE: the settle wait doubles because there are two '138 stages in series. Use CS_SETTLE_NS = 1000 for a two-tier tree until measured. Otherwise cs_select() is identical, with ch = 0..31 and the address split ADDR0..2 = ch & 7, ADDR3..4 = (ch >> 3) & 3.
- BUS-CONTENTION INTERLOCK, hardware: every /OE on the shared 8-bit bus comes from a Y output of ONE '138 (or one tree). Nothing between the '138 pin and the '373 /OE pin except a 0 Ohm trace. No AND gate, no '04, no MCP23017, no second GPIO. Add 220 Ohm 1% in series with each '373 Q output on the shared bus: this bounds an 8-bit contention transient to about 44 mA through VCC/GND, inside the ±70 mA absolute maximum, at a cost of ~25 ns of extra settling into 50 pF.
- BUS-CONTENTION INTERLOCK, firmware: there is nothing to do, and that is the point. Do not write a software mutex for /OE. If you find yourself writing one, the hardware is wrong -- go back and route the /OE lines from a single decoder.
- '04 POLARITY: use ONE '04 gate per '373 LE line. Do not chain two gates to 'restore' polarity somewhere else, and do not use two different gates of the same package for two edges that must align -- TI publishes no gate-to-gate skew for the '04 and no separate tPLH/tPHL.
- WHAT THE '04 MUST NOT DO: (1) Do not parallel '04 gates to increase drive. IOH is only -4 mA at 4.5 V and the parts are not characterised in parallel; unequal delays cause cross-conduction between the paralleled outputs. (2) Do not drive LEDs, relays, MOSFET gates, or long cables from an '04. (3) Do not build an RC or crystal oscillator with it -- the 74HC04 is BUFFERED (three stages) and has no usable linear region; a crystal oscillator wants the unbuffered 74HCU04, which he does not own. (4) Do not feed an '04 any signal with an edge slower than the Delta-t/Delta-v limit (500 ns at 4.5 V; no 3.3 V spec) -- it has no hysteresis and will oscillate. For every one of cases 3 and 4, use the 74HC14 from the Minidodoca kit: min hysteresis 0.4 V at 4.5 V, typ 0.9 V.
- 74LS245: do not fit it. There is no configuration in which it belongs in a 3.3 V fabric. Order SN74HC245N (DIP-20) as the direct replacement -- same pinout, VCC 2-6 V, tpd 26 ns max at 4.5 V. Order SN74LVC245AN instead only if a 5 V module will physically touch that bus (VI up to 5.5 V with 3.3 V VCC, tpd 6.3 ns max at 3.3 V, IOH -24 mA) -- and be aware its ~2 ns edges will ring on breadboard and your 24 MHz analyser cannot see the ringing. Do NOT substitute a TXB0108 or TXS0108 for a '245; they are translators, not bus transceivers, and have no DIR pin.

### Pitfalls

- THE 3.3 V TIMING GAP IS THE BIGGEST SILENT RISK. There is no 3.3 V AC column in the TI or Nexperia datasheet for the '138, '373, '04 or '14. Anyone who hands you a '3.3 V propagation delay' for these parts is interpolating. If firmware is tuned to a delay that came from a blog, it will pass on your bench and fail at 0 degC or on a different manufacturer's die.
- UNBRANDED HC PARTS. The 74HC parts in a Minidodoca-style kit, and cheap marketplace tubes generally, are frequently unmarked re-badges that do not meet the TI/Nexperia 3.3 V corner even though the datasheet you are citing says they should. Test with the part you will actually fit, in the socket it will actually sit in.
- RELEASING THE DATA BUS TOO EARLY. The '373 captures on the LE FALLING edge, which is delayed from your deselect write by tpd('138 enable->Y) + tpd('04). Releasing the bus immediately after the deselect write latches whatever the bus decays to. This fails silently and intermittently -- it will look like a flaky wire.
- CHANGING THE '138 ADDRESS WHILE ENABLED. The three select bits do not change simultaneously and the internal decode paths differ, so an address change from 011 to 100 can transiently assert Y0, Y1, Y2 or Y7 as a real, driven, sub-100 ns LOW. On a /CS that is a phantom chip select; on an inverted LE it is a phantom latch capture. This is the entire reason for break-before-make and it is invisible without a fast analyser.
- USING Y7 AS A 'NULL CHANNEL'. It does not work: reaching Y7 requires an address change, which glitches. Only an enable pin gives a glitch-free deselect, because it is a single input with a single decode path.
- FLOATING ENABLE AT POWER-UP. Before setup() runs, the Teensy pin driving G1/E3 is high-Z, the '138 input sits between VIL (0.99 V) and VIH (2.31 V), and its resolution is arbitrary. If it resolves high, a random /CS or /OE is asserted, hard, for the whole boot window. A 10 kOhm pull-down on G1/E3 and 10 kOhm pull-ups on any MCU-driven G2A/G2B fix this for the cost of 330 uA.
- FORGETTING THE '373's OWN BOOT STATE. TI states outright that at startup Q0 is unknown, and that if LE is held low during startup the outputs stay unknown until LE is driven high with valid data. Without a 10 kOhm pull-up on /OE (pin 1) that unknown state is driven onto the shared bus at power-up, against whatever else is there.
- PUTTING ANY GATE BETWEEN THE '138 AND A /OE. The mutual-exclusion guarantee comes from the decoder's function table. Inserting an '04, an AND gate, or an MCP23017 output between the decoder and /OE destroys it -- gate skew reopens the overlap window, and a second control input can now assert independently. The '04 belongs in the LE path only.
- DRIVING /OE FROM AN MCP23017. Its POR state is all pins configured as inputs, so every /OE it drives would float at power-up. Even with pull-ups this puts a slow, I2C-latency-limited path in the safety-critical position. Keep /OE on the decoder.
- THE ±70 mA VCC/GND RATING IS WHAT YOU VIOLATE FIRST, NOT THE ±35 mA PIN RATING. Eight bits of contention on a '373 is roughly 165 mA through the package rails, about 3.4x over. This is why a double-/OE bug kills parts rather than merely corrupting data.
- A 24 MHz LOGIC ANALYSER CANNOT MEASURE THESE DELAYS DIRECTLY. One sample is 41.67 ns; you need two samples to see an edge separation, so the practical floor is ~83 ns. A 25-45 ns '138 delay is below the noise floor of the instrument. Do not conclude 'the delay is zero' from a capture that shows both edges in the same sample -- see the ring-oscillator method below.
- SERIES RESISTORS ON /CS OR ENABLE LINES CAN VIOLATE THE INPUT SLEW SPEC. Delta-t/Delta-v is 500 ns max at 4.5 V (139 ns/V, Nexperia) with no 3.3 V figure. Series resistance belongs on the DATA bus outputs for contention limiting, not on control inputs.
- 74HC04 USED AS A CRYSTAL OSCILLATOR. It is buffered (three internal stages); the part intended for that job is the unbuffered 74HCU04, which he does not own. A buffered '04 in a Pierce topology either will not start or will run on an overtone.
- MIXING THE FLAT 4-CHIP 1-of-32 WITH A GLOBAL DESELECT. The four chips end up with spare enables of mixed polarity, so one global line needs an inverter for half of them -- and the two polarities then arrive skewed by tpd('04), opening a window where half the chips are enabled and half are not. Use the master tree instead.
- ASSUMING 'IT WORKED ON THE BENCH' MEANS THE LS245 IS FINE AT 3.3 V. Its input side (VIH 2.0 V) genuinely does accept a 3.3 V CMOS high, which is why the mistake survives a first test. The failure is on the output side, is temperature- and lot-dependent, and in the 5 V-powered variant is a slow-kill of the Teensy through its ESD clamp rather than an immediate fault.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- EXCLUSIVITY CHECK (the important one, and fully within the instrument's ability). Put all 8 channels of the analyser on Y0..Y7 of one '138. Run a firmware loop that walks all 8 channels plus deselect, thousands of times, at the slowest rate you will ever use and then at the fastest. Trigger the analyser on 'any two channels low simultaneously'. If your capture software cannot express that, capture and post-process: assert that the popcount of the inverted 8-bit sample is <= 1 for every sample in the file. Any sample with two lows means either your wiring bypasses the decoder or a channel is shorted. This test proves the hardware interlock, and it does not need sub-sample timing resolution.
- BREAK-BEFORE-MAKE CHECK. Channels: CS_EN, CS_A, CS_B, CS_C on 4 channels, and 4 of the Y outputs on the rest. Verify in every transition that CS_EN goes LOW at least one sample before any of CS_A/B/C changes, and that no Y goes low between the address change and the CS_EN rising edge. With CS_SETTLE_NS = 500 the gaps are ~12 samples wide and trivially visible. Then reduce CS_SETTLE_NS and watch the gap shrink -- that is how you find the point where the analyser can no longer confirm ordering, and that is your floor for this instrument.
- MEASURE THE 74HC04 tpd AT YOUR ACTUAL 3.3 V USING A RING OSCILLATOR -- this is the measurement that closes the datasheet's 3.3 V gap, and it is fully within a 24 MHz analyser's reach. Chain 13 inverters (you have 20 packages x 6 gates) output-to-input in a ring, close the loop, power at your real 3.3 V rail. Put one analyser channel on any node. The ring oscillates at f = 1 / (2 x N x tpd). If tpd is ~13 ns, f = 1/(2 x 13 x 13 ns) = 2.96 MHz -- well inside 24 MHz sampling. Then tpd = 1 / (2 x N x f). Repeat with N = 21 and N = 31 to confirm linearity (f should scale as 1/N). CAVEAT: the ring loads each stage with only one gate input (~3.5 pF Ci, plus a few pF of stray), not the 50 pF of the datasheet, so this yields an UNLOADED tpd -- a lower bound. Scale to your real load using the datasheet slope: the '138 shows ~12 ns typ at CL = 15 pF vs ~15 ns typ at CL = 50 pF, i.e. roughly 0.086 ns/pF. Add ~4 ns for a 50 pF load.
- MEASURE THE 74HC138 ENABLE->Y DELAY AT 3.3 V using the same trick with a mixed ring. Wire Y0 of a '138 into three cascaded '04 gates and feed the third inverter's output back into G1/E3 of that same '138 (odd total inversion count, so it oscillates). Tie the address to 000 so Y0 is the selected output. Loop delay = tpd('138 enable->Y) + 3 x tpd('04). Measure the frequency, compute the loop delay as 1/(2f), and subtract 3 x tpd('04) from the previous experiment. That gives you a real, measured, 3.3 V '138 enable delay for your specific parts -- the number the datasheet refuses to give you. Repeat at 3.0 V and 3.6 V with a bench supply to see the slope.
- MEASURE THE '373 CAPTURE INSTANT relative to the deselect. Channels: CS_EN, the relevant '138 Yn, the '04 output (= LE), one data-bus bit D, and the corresponding Q output. Drive a pattern where the bus bit changes shortly after the deselect write. With CS_SETTLE_NS = 500 the LE fall and the bus change are ~12 samples apart and you can confirm Q holds the pre-change value. Then deliberately break it: release the bus 100 ns after the deselect and confirm Q latches garbage. Seeing the failure once is worth more than trusting the arithmetic.
- CONFIRM THE LE PULSE WIDTH. On the '04 output (LE), measure the high time in samples. It must exceed tw(LE high) with margin -- 24 ns at 4.5 V, 120 ns at the 2 V guaranteed bound. At CS_SETTLE_NS = 500 you will read ~12 samples (~500 ns), which is 4x the worst-case bound. Good.
- INA219: FLOATING-INPUT / CONTENTION DETECTOR. Put one INA219 in the +3.3 V feed to the logic-fabric board only (not the Teensy). Quiescent expectation with everything static and every input properly tied: 80 uA max per '138 plus 160 uA max per '373 (both at the 6 V, over-temperature worst case, so at 3.3 V expect far less -- single-digit uA per package). If you read hundreds of uA or milliamps with nothing switching, an input is floating or two outputs are contending. Unplug packages one at a time to bisect. CAVEAT: the stock 0.1 Ohm shunt on a typical INA219 breakout with the common default calibration gives roughly 100 uA per LSB, which is barely enough resolution -- reconfigure to the low-current calibration (setCalibration_16V_400mA() or equivalent) before trusting sub-milliamp readings. UNVERIFIED: check your library's actual current-LSB for the calibration you select.
- INA219: PROVE THE SERIES RESISTORS WORK, ONCE, DELIBERATELY. On a scrap '373 pair with 220 Ohm series resistors fitted, force both /OE low simultaneously with a jumper (not from firmware) with opposite data on the two latches, and read the rail current. Expect roughly 44 mA for 8 bits of contention. Then remove the resistors and repeat with ONE bit only -- expect roughly 21 mA for that single bit. Do not run the 8-bit unresisted case; that is the 165 mA condition that destroys the part. This calibrates your estimate of the HC output impedance, which is currently a derived number rather than a datasheet one.
- VERIFY THE PULL RESISTORS ACTUALLY HOLD AT POWER-UP. Put an analyser channel on CS_EN and one on a Y output, arm the trigger on the Y output going low, then power-cycle the board. With the 10 kOhm pull-down fitted you should capture nothing. Remove the pull-down and repeat several times -- you will eventually catch the phantom assert. That is the boot hazard, on your own bench, in a screenshot.

### Open questions for the builder

- What is the actual measured voltage on the fabric rail under load -- 3.30 V, or 3.15 V after a regulator and some trace drop? Every propagation-delay estimate scales steeply with VCC in the 3 V region, and the difference between 3.0 V and 3.3 V is meaningful on a curve whose 2 V point is 5x slower than its 4.5 V point.
- How many 74HC373 D inputs, plus how much trace and breadboard, will sit on the shared 8-bit data bus? That decides whether the CL = 50 pF or the CL = 150 pF switching table applies -- the difference is 45 ns vs 60 ns (D->Q) at 4.5 V, and TI explicitly says not to exceed 50 pF. Count the loads and estimate the capacitance before fixing the settle wait.
- Who manufactured the 74HC138, 74HC373 and 74HC04 parts you own -- TI, Nexperia, Toshiba, or unmarked? Everything above is cited from TI and Nexperia. Unmarked marketplace HC parts routinely miss the 3.3 V corner. If they are unmarked, the ring-oscillator measurement is not optional.
- Do the 74HC373 outputs actually need to drive a SHARED bus, or are they point-to-point to dedicated loads (LEDs, individual peripheral inputs)? If every '373's Q outputs go to their own private destination, there is no shared bus, no /OE arbitration problem, no shoot-through hazard, and no need for series resistors -- you can tie every /OE to GND permanently and the whole contention analysis evaporates. This single answer changes half the design.
- Is the 2.4 inch display on Teensy1 sharing the same SPI bus whose /CS lines the '138 will decode? If so, what is its controller's minimum /CS-high recovery time between transactions? That figure, not the '138's propagation delay, may well be the binding constraint on CS_SETTLE_NS.
- Which Teensy 4.1 pins will carry CS_A, CS_B, CS_C? If all three land on the same fast-GPIO port (GPIO6/7/8/9) you can write them in a single register store and eliminate inter-bit skew entirely. If they are scattered across ports you take three separate stores and three skew intervals. Worth deciding before wiring anything.
- How many independently addressable chip-selects do you actually need in stack 1 -- 8, 16, 32, or 64? 1-of-32 costs 5 '138s per stack (10 across both stacks, leaving 10 spare of your 20). 1-of-64 costs 9 per stack (18 of 20, leaving 2 spare) for the same two-stage delay. If 32 is enough, take 32 and keep the spares.
- Does anything in the planned stack genuinely run at 5 V? That is the only thing that decides between buying SN74HC245N (cheaper, matches the fabric, HC thresholds throughout) and SN74LVC245AN (5 V-tolerant inputs, 6.3 ns, but fast edges that will ring on breadboard and that your 24 MHz analyser cannot see). If the answer is 'nothing is 5 V', buy the HC245 and stop thinking about it.
- Do you want the '373s to be readable back onto the bus (i.e. is anything ever reading FROM a '373's Q outputs into the Teensy), or are they write-only output expanders? A '373 is not a transceiver -- if you need bidirectional traffic on that bus you need the '245 you do not currently own, and the whole /OE arbitration question gets larger.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

None. Every source in this pass was a manufacturer PDF fetched directly (ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/*.pdf for SN74HC138 SCLS107G, SN74HC373 SCLS140G, SN74HC573A SCLS147G, SN74HC374 SCLS141G, SN74HC04 SCLS078H, SN74HC14, SN74HC245, SN74LVC245A, SN74LS245 SDLS146B; assets.nexperia.com for 74HC/HCT138 Rev 10 and 74HC/HCT374; nxp.com for IMXRT1060CEC Rev 4) plus one vendor product page (pjrc.com/store/teensy41.html). The PDFs were extracted locally with pypdf rather than being interpreted by a summarising model, so the text I read was the raw datasheet content. I found no instruction-shaped text, no fake system-reminder blocks, no attribution-change directives, and nothing purporting to grant authority or urgency in any fetched document. One WebSearch was run (i.MX RT1060 absolute maximum ratings); its result summary contained no embedded instructions, and I verified its claim independently against the NXP PDF rather than relying on the summary. Note for completeness: two early WebFetch calls returned a model-written summary saying it could not read the compressed PDF and suggesting I supply a different document -- that is the fetch tool's own helper model failing on binary content, not injected content, and I ignored its suggestion and extracted the PDFs locally instead.

---

## ESP32-WROOM-32E (E32R40T 4.0" display board) as a second protocol node: UART peripherals, GPIO matrix routing, strapping-pin hazards, S3→classic porting, ESP-NOW interop, and power

### Bottom line

The classic ESP32 in the WROOM-32E has 3 UARTs (UART0/1/2), each with a 128-byte hardware FIFO and a hard max of 5,000,000 baud, so 921600 8N1 is comfortably safe — the ESP32's fractional divider off the 80 MHz APB clock lands on 922,190.2 bps, +0.064% error. The catch is pins: every IO_MUX UART pin except UART0's is unusable on this board (UART1's IO_MUX pins are GPIO9/10 = SPI flash, not led out on WROOM-32E; UART2's are GPIO16/17 = the onboard RGB LED), and UART0 is hardwired to the CH340C. You must route UART1 or UART2 through the GPIO matrix to explicit pins, and on the E32R40T only GPIO21, 25, 32 (output-capable) and GPIO35, 39 (input-only) are free. Recommended link: TX=GPIO25, RX=GPIO35, optional RTS=GPIO32 / CTS=GPIO21; avoid GPIO39 because of erratum GPIO-3.11 (80 ns spurious low pulse when ADC1 powers on, and this board reads the battery on GPIO34 = ADC1_CH6). The GPIO12 hazard is real and specific: MTDI high at reset switches VDD_SDIO to 1.8 V and brownouts the 3.3 V flash, and this board wires GPIO12 to the shared LCD/touch MISO — never add a pull-up there. Porting from S3: the classic ESP32 has no USB peripheral at all (no SOC_USB_* in soc_caps.h), so ARDUINO_USB_CDC_ON_BOOT is forced 0 and `Serial` becomes UART0 on GPIO1/3 — any `Serial.print()` debug that was harmlessly going to native USB on the S3 now goes out a real UART pin. ESP-NOW interoperates fine across families (it is a plain 802.11 vendor-specific action frame), provided both nodes sit on the same 2.4 GHz channel and the payload stays ≤250 bytes if either side is running an ESP-NOW v1.0 stack.

### Implementation guidance

- Use UART2 for the protocol link and leave UART0 alone for the CH340C console. Concretely: `HardwareSerial &Link = Serial2;` then in setup(), BEFORE begin(): `Link.setRxBufferSize(4096); Link.setTxBufferSize(2048);` then `Link.begin(921600, SERIAL_8N1, /*rx=*/35, /*tx=*/25);`. Argument order is (baud, config, RX, TX) — RX first.
- Never call `Serial1.begin(...)` or `Serial2.begin(...)` without explicit rx/tx pins on this board. On arduino-esp32 3.x the classic defaults are RX1=26/TX1=27 and RX2=4/TX2=25; GPIO27 is the LCD backlight and GPIO4 is AUDIO_EN. On 2.x they are RX1=9/TX1=10 (SPI flash, unreachable) and RX2=16/TX2=17 (RGB LED).
- Pin choice, in priority order. TX must be output-capable: GPIO25 (first choice), GPIO32 (second), GPIO21 (third). RX must be input-capable: GPIO35 (only sane choice). Do NOT use GPIO39 for RX — erratum GPIO-3.11 puts an 80 ns low pulse on it whenever ADC1 powers on, and this board reads the battery on GPIO34 = ADC1_CH6.
- Add an external 10 kohm pull-up to 3.3 V on GPIO35 (the RX line). GPIO34-39 have no software-enabled internal pull-up, so if the Teensy is in reset or unplugged the ESP32 RX pin floats and will latch random frames.
- Add an external 10 kohm pull-up to 3.3 V on the ESP32 TX line (GPIO25) as well. GPIO25 is high-Z during ESP32 reset and the whole ROM bootloader window, so the Teensy's RX would otherwise float for hundreds of milliseconds on every ESP32 reset.
- Optional but recommended given Wi-Fi ISR jitter: use hardware flow control. `Link.setPins(35, 25, /*cts=*/21, /*rts=*/32); Link.setHwFlowCtrlMode(UART_HW_FLOWCTRL_CTS_RTS, 100);` — RTS on GPIO32 (ESP32 output, wire to Teensy CTS), CTS on GPIO21 (ESP32 input, wire from Teensy RTS). Threshold 100 leaves 28 bytes of the 128-byte FIFO as margin. This consumes every remaining free pin on the board.
- Do not touch the CPU frequency. `setCpuFrequencyMhz()` below 80 MHz makes APB_CLK follow the CPU clock instead of staying at 80 MHz, which silently changes the UART divisor and breaks 921600. Leave the CPU at 240 MHz (or 160/80, all of which keep APB at 80 MHz) and leave the UART clock source at its APB default — never select UART_CLK_SRC_REF_TICK.
- Never write a 1 for GPIO12 and never leave it as a driven output at reset. If you add any device to the LCD/touch SPI bus, verify its data-out pin is high-Z at power-up and fit a 10 kohm pull-DOWN from GPIO12 to GND to guarantee MTDI reads 0. If you want the hazard gone permanently, run `espefuse.py --port <COM> set-flash-voltage 3.3V` once — this is an irreversible eFuse burn, and it is only correct because WROOM-32E's embedded flash is a 3.3 V part.
- Port the console object as `Serial`, not `Serial0`. `Serial` compiles on both arduino-esp32 2.x and 3.x for the classic; `Serial0` only exists on 3.x for this chip. Delete every `USBSerial`, `HWCDCSerial`, `USB.begin()`, `USBCDC`, and `Serial.setTxTimeoutMs()` reference from the S3 sketch — none of those symbols exist on a chip with no USB peripheral.
- Audit every `Serial.print()` inherited from the S3 firmware. On the S3 with USB CDC On Boot those went to native USB; on the classic they go out physical GPIO1 to the CH340C. That is harmless as long as the protocol link stays on UART2 — but if you ever move the link to UART0 to save pins, those prints will inject bytes into the binary framing. Guard them with a `#define LINK_UART Serial2` / `#define LOG Serial` split and never let LOG and LINK_UART be the same object.
- `while (!Serial);` is safe to leave in: on the classic, `HardwareSerial::operator bool()` returns `uartIsDriverInstalled(_uart)`, so it returns true immediately after begin() rather than waiting for a USB host to enumerate.
- For ESP-NOW peering with the S3 fleet: `WiFi.mode(WIFI_STA); esp_wifi_set_channel(<same channel as the S3 nodes>, WIFI_SECOND_CHAN_NONE); esp_now_init();` and when adding the peer set `peer.channel = 0` so it transmits on the current channel. If any S3 in the fleet associates to an AP, that AP's channel forces the whole mesh — read it with `WiFi.channel()` on the S3 and hard-code or distribute it.
- Keep every mesh (channel 0x04) payload at or below 250 bytes. That keeps the link valid whether either end is running an ESP-NOW v1.0 or v2.0 stack. Since the protocol max frame is 1024 bytes, add a fragmentation layer inside CHAN 0x04 rather than relying on ESP-NOW v2's 1470-byte frames.
- Build the display node and the S3 radio node on the SAME arduino-esp32 major version. The ESP-NOW receive callback changed signature at core 3.x: `void onRecv(const esp_now_recv_info_t *info, const uint8_t *data, int len)` versus core 2.x's `void onRecv(const uint8_t *mac, const uint8_t *data, int len)`.
- LVGL/display buffers: the -32E has 520 KB SRAM and no PSRAM. A full 480x320 RGB565 framebuffer is 307,200 bytes and will not leave room for the Wi-Fi stack. Use a partial buffer of 480 x 40 x 2 = 38,400 bytes (or two of them for double-buffered striping) and keep the S3's PSRAM-sized buffer configuration out of this build.
- The LCD reset is tied to the module EN pin, not a GPIO. There is no `LCD_RST` to toggle. To re-init the panel without a chip reset, send the ST7796 software reset command 0x01 and wait the datasheet-specified 120 ms before further commands.
- Service the link UART aggressively. At 921600 the 128-byte FIFO gives 1.389 ms before overflow. Either register `Link.onReceive(cb)` (runs in the UART event task) or poll from a dedicated FreeRTOS task pinned with `xTaskCreatePinnedToCore(..., 1)` so LVGL rendering on core 0 cannot starve it. Do not parse frames from inside `loop()` if `loop()` also does a blocking LVGL flush.

### Pitfalls

- GPIO12 pull-up = dead board, no error message. MTDI high at reset switches VDD_SDIO to the internal 1.8 V LDO while the WROOM-32E's flash is a 3.3 V part; the flash brownouts and the chip neither boots nor flashes. GPIO12 is the shared LCD/touch MISO on this board, so a level-shifter's internal pull-up, a bus pull-up 'for signal integrity', or a third SPI device that drives DO high at power-up all produce the same symptom: a board that was working and now does nothing.
- Serial1.begin(921600) with no pin arguments on arduino-esp32 3.x drives GPIO27 (LCD backlight) as UART TX. The screen flickers or dims in a data-dependent pattern and the link half-works — a classic plausible-but-wrong result, because RX on GPIO26 will still occasionally frame correctly from crosstalk.
- UART RX on GPIO39 will show rare, unreproducible framing errors correlated with battery-voltage reads. Erratum GPIO-3.11 drops GPIO36/GPIO39 low for ~80 ns each time ADC1 is powered on. 80 ns is only 7.4% of a bit time so it never corrupts a byte in flight, but on an idle line it is a valid falling edge and the UART will start sampling a phantom frame. On a logic analyser this looks like a single glitch byte every few seconds — easy to blame on the Teensy.
- The same erratum already fires phantom touch interrupts: TOUCH_IRQ is GPIO36. If the firmware treats a falling edge on GPIO36 as 'pen down' without confirming the level, battery reads will generate ghost touches.
- `Serial` means different physical hardware on the two chips. On the S3 with CDCOnBoot it is native USB; on the classic it is UART0 on GPIO1/3. Code that assumed `Serial` was a free debug channel now shares pins with the CH340C, and if UART0 is ever repurposed for the protocol, log lines are injected mid-frame — the CRC catches it, but the failure looks like line noise rather than a software bug.
- Setting the UART clock source to REF_TICK (1 MHz) makes 921600 unachievable. The divisor would be ~1.085 and the LL driver truncates to 1, giving 1 Mbps. The S3 firmware cannot make this mistake because the S3 has no REF_TICK, so the reviewer's instinct that 'this worked on the S3' does not transfer.
- `setCpuFrequencyMhz(40)` or lower silently retunes every UART. On classic ESP32, APB_CLK is fixed at 80 MHz for CPU frequencies of 80/160/240 MHz but follows the CPU below that. The UART divisor is not recomputed, so the baud drops proportionally and every frame fails CRC.
- setRxBufferSize()/setTxBufferSize() called after begin() are silently ineffective — they log an error and return 0. The default 256-byte RX ring cannot hold a 1024-byte protocol frame, so frames get chopped whenever the reader task is late, and the CRC failures look like an electrical problem.
- With the default _txBufferSize = 0 there is no TX ring buffer at all; Serial.write() of a 1024-byte frame blocks for ~11.1 ms waiting on the 128-byte FIFO. On a node also running LVGL, that is a visible frame hitch and can cascade into watchdog resets.
- ESP-NOW that fails only because of a channel mismatch reports success. esp_now_send() returns ESP_OK and the send callback fires with ESP_NOW_SEND_SUCCESS if the MAC layer got the frame out, regardless of whether any peer was listening on that channel. Only the absence of application-level ACKs reveals it.
- A mixed arduino-esp32 2.x / 3.x fleet fails at compile time on the ESP-NOW recv callback signature — but if someone 'fixes' it with a cast, the first argument is a struct pointer, not a MAC array, and the peer address read will be garbage.
- Assuming GPIO16/17 are free because the -32E has no PSRAM. They are electrically free at the module level but wired to the green and blue RGB LED channels on this board — using them as UART pins turns the status LED into a data-activity strobe and back-feeds current through the LED.
- LVGL configuration carried over from the S3-N16R8 assumes 8 MB PSRAM. A full 480x320 RGB565 framebuffer is 307,200 bytes against 520 KB total SRAM; the allocation may succeed at boot and then the Wi-Fi stack fails to initialise later, which presents as an ESP-NOW bug rather than an out-of-memory one.
- The 4-pin JST connector pin ORDER is not documented on the vendor wiki — only which GPIOs reach which connector. Building a harness from an assumed pin order risks shorting 5 V or 3.3 V into GPIO35. Buzz every connector out before soldering.
- The 2-pin expansion-input JST carries GPIO35 and GPIO39 only — there is no ground on it. The UART return path must come from the I2C or serial JST, and a link with no shared ground reference will appear to work at short lengths and fail intermittently.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Baud accuracy: capture ESP32 TX (GPIO25) on the 24 MHz analyser at full rate. One bit is 1.0844 us, which is 26.0 samples at 24 MHz — comfortably above the 4-samples-per-bit minimum. Measure the width of a known 0x55 pattern (10 alternating edges): expect 10 x 1.0844 us = 10.844 us +/- one sample. If you measure 10.851 us you are reading nominal 921600; the ESP32's real +0.064% offset (922,190.2 bps) is 7 ns over 10 bits and is below the analyser's 41.7 ns resolution — so treat 'indistinguishable from nominal' as the pass criterion, not a defect.
- Confirm the UART actually landed on the pins you asked for: probe GPIO25 and GPIO35 and simultaneously probe GPIO27 (backlight) and GPIO26 (audio DAC). If GPIO27 shows serial activity you fell into the arduino-esp32 3.x default-pin trap. GPIO27 should be either steady DC or a clean PWM at your backlight frequency, never a 921.6 kbps bitstream.
- Prove the GPIO12 strap state at reset: put a probe on GPIO12 and one on EN, trigger on EN's rising edge, and confirm GPIO12 is low from the EN edge until at least the first LCD CS assertion. Any high level on GPIO12 in that window is the brownout condition. Do this once after every change to the LCD SPI bus.
- Reproduce the GPIO-3.11 glitch: probe GPIO39 (and GPIO36) with the analyser set to capture a single glitch, then run a loop that repeatedly calls analogRead(34). Set the trigger to a falling edge on GPIO39 with the line otherwise pulled high. You should catch an ~80 ns low pulse coincident with each ADC1 power-on. Sampling at 24 MHz gives 41.7 ns resolution, so an 80 ns pulse appears as 2 samples — it is right at the edge of detectability, so use edge-triggered single-shot capture rather than eyeballing a long trace.
- Frame timing: a maximum 1024-byte frame at 921600 8N1 occupies 1024 x 10 / 921600 = 11.11 ms. Trigger on the 0xAA START byte and confirm no inter-byte gap exceeds one bit time within a frame. Gaps of hundreds of microseconds mid-frame mean the TX ring buffer is absent (_txBufferSize = 0) and the writer is blocking on the FIFO.
- FIFO overrun hunt: run the link at full rate while forcing Wi-Fi activity (an ESP-NOW burst or an AP scan) and capture 200 ms windows. Any lost byte will show as a valid RX waveform on the wire with no corresponding application-level ACK. Correlate with a GPIO you toggle inside the UART receive callback — if the toggle gap ever exceeds 1.389 ms while data is flowing, you have exceeded the 128-byte FIFO slack and need flow control or a pinned task.
- Idle-state proof: with the Teensy powered off, confirm GPIO35 sits at 3.3 V (your external pull-up working) rather than floating. With the ESP32 held in reset, confirm the Teensy's RX line sits at 3.3 V (the pull-up on GPIO25). A floating line reads as an ambiguous mid-rail trace on the analyser's analogue view or as random edges on the digital view.
- Power: put an INA219 in series with the board's 5 V input. Expect ~40 mA during the reset window, ~230 mA once the display is up, and transient excursions during Wi-Fi TX. Log the peak over a 60 s window that includes an ESP-NOW burst and an SD write; if you see sustained draw above 500 mA the charger is active and you need a supply rated well beyond the USB default.
- Backlight isolation: measure the 5 V input current with the backlight PWM at 0% and at 100%. The delta should be close to the vendor's 142 mA backlight figure (allow for LDO efficiency, since the LEDs are driven from the 3.3 V rail through the LDO: the 5 V-side delta will be roughly 142 mA x 3.3/5 / efficiency, so expect ~95-142 mA). A much smaller delta means your PWM is not reaching GPIO27.
- ESP-NOW channel check without RF gear: have both the S3 node and the WROOM node print WiFi.channel() over their respective UARTs and capture both simultaneously on separate analyser channels via their TX lines. Mismatched channels are the failure mode that esp_now_send() will not report.

### Open questions for the builder

- Which physical connector do you intend to run the Teensy link through, and have you buzzed out its pin order? The vendor wiki names the GPIOs per connector but not the pin sequence or the power-pin voltage (5 V vs 3.3 V) of the 4-pin serial, SPI and I2C JSTs.
- Do you need the I2C JST (GPIO25/GPIO32) for anything else? If Teensy1 remains the sole I2C master, those two pins are the natural UART TX + RTS, but that permanently forecloses local I2C on the display node.
- Do you want hardware flow control on this link? Adding RTS/CTS consumes GPIO32 and GPIO21, i.e. every remaining free pin on the board, and it is a departure from the existing 3-wire tested link. Given Wi-Fi ISR jitter on a single-antenna classic ESP32, is 1.389 ms of FIFO slack enough for your frame cadence, or do you want the extra two wires?
- Which arduino-esp32 version is the existing tested S3 firmware built against? That decides both the Serial0 availability and the ESP-NOW callback signature, and the display node must match.
- Is the ESP-NOW fleet channel fixed, or does an S3 associate to an AP and inherit the AP's channel at runtime? If the latter, the display node needs a channel-discovery or channel-distribution step, because it cannot scan and peer simultaneously.
- Are you powering this board from the stack's 5 V rail or from its own USB-C? If from the stack, the rail needs ~600 mA of headroom for this node alone and the grounds must be bonded at a single point with the Teensy link ground.
- Is the LiPo charger circuit populated on your unit, and will a battery be fitted? The 580 mA figure only applies with charging active; without a battery the ceiling is nearer 300 mA.
- Do you intend to burn the flash-voltage eFuse to neutralise the GPIO12 hazard permanently? It is irreversible and I would not do it without you explicitly deciding.
- Has the 2.4-inch display on Teensy1 been resolved as ST7789 SPI TFT vs OLED? It does not affect this node, but it does affect whether the two display nodes can share a driver abstraction.
- Does the existing protocol define a display channel (the used list stops at 0x04 mesh, with 0x03 reserved)? A new channel ID needs allocating for the display node before firmware is written.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

none — no instruction-shaped text, fake system-reminders, or attempts to alter commit attribution appeared in any fetched page. Sources used were Espressif documentation.espressif.com and docs.espressif.com pages, raw.githubusercontent.com files from espressif/esp-idf (release/v5.5) and espressif/arduino-esp32 (master and tag 2.0.17), the LCDWIKI vendor wiki page for the 4.0inch ESP32-32E Display, and a MICRONE ME6217 part listing. Two Espressif PDF URLs returned 301 redirects to documentation.espressif.com, which I re-fetched explicitly rather than following blindly; one PDF (esp32_datasheet_en.pdf) would not parse as text so I used the HTML edition of the same document instead. Two pages (device.report, documentation.espressif.com HTML datasheet on one attempt) returned HTTP 403 and were not used.

## 74HC4051 8×8 analog mux tree (one 74HC138 enabling eight leaves) into one Teensy 4.1 12-bit ADC pin

### Bottom line

The topology is sound and I confirm it over a master-mux tree, but three of the numbers you need do not exist in any primary datasheet and must be measured. (1) Neither Nexperia nor TI characterises the 74HC4051 at 3.3 V at all — Ron, ton and toff are specified only at VCC−VEE = 2.0 / 4.5 / 6.0 / 9.0 V, so every 3.3 V Ron figure in circulation is somebody's measurement, not a spec. Design against Ron ≈ 300 Ω and ton ≈ 350 ns (the 2.0 V bound) until you measure your own parts. (2) Charge injection is not specified by either vendor; the closest published proxy is Nexperia's control-to-switch crosstalk Vct = 110 mV p-p at VCC = 4.5 V into 600 Ω ‖ 50 pF. (3) Break-before-make is real but qualitative — TI says "a break-before-make system eliminates channel overlap", Nexperia lists "typical 'break before make' built-in"; neither gives a t_BBM number, and neither guarantees it between two different packages, which is exactly the overlap the '138 must prevent. The dominant electrical fact of this design is that bussing eight Z pins puts ~200 pF (8 × 25 pF typ Ccom) on the ADC node — 100× the RT1060's 2 pF sample cap. That is good news (charge-sharing error is only 2/202 = 0.99 % of the step, and one discarded read kills it) and bad news (the bus, not the ADC, sets your settling time). Teensy's stock 12-bit config gives a 2.4 µs sample aperture, which is 18× more than the ADC itself needs, so all your timing budget is mux-bus settling. Do not skip the discarded read: with a source above ~1 kΩ the un-discarded first read after a channel change is wrong by tens of LSB and looks completely plausible.

| What goes wrong when VEE floats (derived mechanism, three distinct failures). | (a) Gate drive loses its reference. The internal level converter generates the n-/p-channel gate voltages between VCC and VEE. A floating VEE drifts (leakage, ESD structures, coupling) typically toward VCC, collapsing the n-channel gate overdrive. Ron rises from hundreds of ohms to kΩ–MΩ, non-monotonically with signal level. (b) Substrate diodes become live. Every Yn and Z pin has a clamp diode to VEE (abs-max ISK is specified for VSW < VEE − 0.5 V). With VEE floating, any channel signal near 0 V can forward-bias into the floating node and drag VEE around, so VEE follows your lowest active signal. (c) Latch-up path opens. Current injected into a floating substrate rail is exactly the trigger condition JESD78 latch-up testing exercises. | likely | Derived from Nexperia Rev.12 Fig. 1/Fig. 4 (level conversion block, switch schematic) and Table 4 (ISK switch clamping current, VSW < −0.5 V, ±20 mA) |

If the loop gain exceeds 1, the SCR latches and conducts VCC→VEE independently of the input; the mux stops switching and the die heats. Output transition time tt: 7 ns typ / 15 ns max at 4.5 V; 19 ns typ / 75 ns max at 2.0 V. No 3.3 V column exists. | certain | Nexperia 74HC138/74HCT138 datasheet, Table 7 (Dynamic characteristics) |
| The '138 does not give you break-before-make between leaves for free during an address change. | A 3-to-8 decoder generates each output from the address bits and their inverted copies. During an A0..A2 transition the inverted copies are delayed by one inverter stage, so two outputs can be asserted LOW simultaneously for a few nanoseconds, or all outputs can go inactive. Nexperia specifies no glitch-free decode guarantee. | likely | Derived from standard 3-to-8 decoder topology; absence of any glitch or one-hot guarantee in Nexperia 74HC138 datasheet |
| The '6 GPIO' claim needs a seventh pin, or a shift register. | 3 GPIO (S0/S1/S2, shared to all eight '4051) + 3 GPIO ('138 A0/A1/A2) = 6 GPIO for addressing. A 7th GPIO on '138 E3 is required for guaranteed break-before-make. Alternative: drive all 7 lines from one 74HC595 (you own 20) — bits [S0,S1,S2,A0,A1,A2,E3,spare] — costing 3 shared SPI pins + 1 dedicated RCLK, and giving atomic two-phase updates. | certain | Derived from the '138 glitch analysis above and your stated inventory |
| Inventory constraint on the second stack. | You own 10× 74HC4051. The eight-leaf topology consumes 8 per stack. Two identical 64-channel stacks need 16. | certain | Your stated inventory |
| Teensy 4.1 analog pin map, and which pins are ADC2-only. | A0–A9 = digital pins 14–23 (also aliased on 0–9 in the table). A10 = pin 24, A11 = pin 25. A12 = pin 26 and A13 = pin 27 are ADC2-ONLY. A14 = pin 38 and A15 = pin 39 are ADC2-ONLY. A16 = pin 40, A17 = pin 41. Channel codes with bit 7 set (128+n) denote ADC2-only. | certain | PaulStoffregen/cores, teensy4/analog.c, pin_to_channel[] table with per-pin comments |
| INA219 measurement resolution, for your instrumentation plan. | Shunt voltage LSB = 10 µV. PGA full-scale ranges ±40 / ±80 / ±160 / ±320 mV. Bus voltage LSB = 4 mV, range 0–26 V. 9/10/11/12-bit modes, conversion times ~84 µs to 8.244 ms. | certain | TI INA219 datasheet (ti.com/lit/ds/symlink/ina219.pdf) |
| ESD ratings differ substantially between the two vendors' parts — relevant to bench handling. | Nexperia 74HC4051: HBM exceeds 2000 V (JS-001 class 2), CDM exceeds 1000 V (JS-002 class C3). TI CD74HC4051 (SCHS122O, Jan 2026): HBM ±500 V, CDM ±200 V, all pins. | certain | Nexperia Rev.12 Section 2; TI SCHS122O Section 5.2 ESD Ratings |

### Implementation guidance

- Wire the bus. All eight '4051 pin 3 (Z) to one node -> one Teensy 4.1 ADC1 pin (recommend A0 = digital pin 14, or A16 = pin 40). Keep this node physically short; every centimetre of breadboard rail adds capacitance to the 200 pF you already have.
- Wire the shared select. All eight '4051 pin 11 (S0), pin 10 (S1), pin 9 (S2) bussed to three Teensy GPIO. These are CMOS gate inputs and must never float.
- Wire the enable. 74HC138 outputs Y0..Y7 (pins 15,14,13,12,11,10,9,8) each to one '4051 pin 6 (E). '138 A0/A1/A2 (pins 1,2,3) to three Teensy GPIO. '138 E1 (pin 4) and E2 (pin 5) hard-wired to GND. '138 E3 (pin 6) to a SEVENTH Teensy GPIO — this is the global deselect and it is not optional.
- Wire the rails. Every '4051: pin 16 = 3.3 V, pin 8 = GND, pin 7 (VEE) = GND. Put a 100 nF X7R from pin 16 to pin 8 within 10 mm of each package (TI Section 8.3 recommends 0.1 uF per VCC pin). Add one 1 uF bulk cap per group of four packages.
- Terminate unused channels. A floating Yn on a SELECTED channel leaves the bus floating and the ADC returns the previous channel's decaying value — plausible and wrong. Tie every unpopulated Yn to GND through 100 kohm. (Unlike S0..S2 and E, the Yn pins are switch terminals, not gate inputs, so a floating Yn on a DESELECTED channel is harmless — but you cannot guarantee you never address it.)
- Series-protect any channel whose source is not hard-bounded to 0..3.3 V: 1 kohm in series with that Yn. This limits clamp-diode current to 3.3 mA for a full-rail overdrive against the +/-20 mA ISK abs-max, and Nexperia explicitly endorses this ('Inputs include clamp diodes. This enables the use of current limiting resistors'). It costs you 313 ns of extra tau1 which your settle budget already covers.
- Init once: analogReadResolution(12); analogReadAveraging(4);  This selects ADC_CFG_MODE(2) | ADC_CFG_ADSTS(3) | ADC_CFG_ADLSMP | ADC_CFG_ADIV(1) | ADC_CFG_ADICLK(3) | ADC_CFG_ADHSC, i.e. fADCK ~10 MHz, 24-cycle (2.4 us) sample aperture, 50-cycle (5.0 us) conversion. Do NOT reduce the sample time; it is free margin. Do not reach for the pedvide ADC library for this — the stock core already picks the longest aperture.
- Per-channel read sequence, in this exact order. (1) digitalWriteFast(PIN_138_E3, LOW) — global deselect, all eight leaves off. (2) Write S0,S1,S2 and '138 A0,A1,A2 for the target channel. (3) delayNanoseconds(500) — dead time; covers '138 tpd max 150 ns (2.0 V bound) plus '4051 toff max 300 ns. (4) digitalWriteFast(PIN_138_E3, HIGH) — enable exactly one leaf. (5) delayMicroseconds(t_settle). (6) (void)analogRead(pin);  // DISCARD — flushes the previous channel out of the SAR sample cap. (7) v = analogRead(pin);  // USE.
- Compute t_settle, do not guess it: t_settle_us = ceil( ton_us + 9.011 * (R_source + Ron) * C_bus ). With ton = 0.35 us (2.0 V bound), Ron = 250 ohm (estimate — replace with your measured value), C_bus = 250 pF: R_source = 0 -> 0.92 us, use 2 us. R_source = 1 kohm -> 3.2 us, use 5 us. R_source = 10 kohm -> 23.4 us, use 30 us. Encode this as a per-channel constant in your channel descriptor table, not one global delay — a 100 kohm thermistor channel and a 100 ohm divider channel should not pay the same price.
- Never change S0..S2 or the '138 address while E3 is HIGH. Making that a single inlined function with the deselect built in is the cheapest way to guarantee it survives future edits.
- Scan budget: 0.5 us dead + 2 us settle + 5 us discard (call analogReadAveraging(1) for the discard if you want it faster) + 20 us real read (avg 4) = ~27.5 us per channel. 64 channels = ~1.8 ms, ~560 full scans/s. If you need faster, drop averaging to 1 (5 us/read) for a ~10 us/channel, ~640 us full scan — at the cost of ~1 extra LSB of noise.
- Protocol integration: this is a new per-channel namespace, so add it as a NEW CHAN byte on the existing frame (START 0xAA | LEN_H | LEN_L | SEQ | FLAGS | CHAN | CMD | PAYLOAD | CRC_H | CRC_L, CRC-16/MCRF4XX poly 0x8408 init 0xFFFF). Channels 0x00-0x04 are taken; use 0x05 for 'analog'. Suggested CMDs: 0x01 READ_ONE (payload: u8 channel 0..63) -> u16 raw; 0x02 READ_BLOCK (payload: u8 first, u8 count) -> count x u16; 0x03 SET_SETTLE (payload: u8 channel, u16 settle_us); 0x04 SCAN_ALL -> 64 x u16 = 128 bytes payload, total frame 134 bytes, comfortably inside the 1024 max. Reserve SEQ bit7 for an unsolicited threshold-crossing report.
- Do not reimplement the frame. Extend the existing encoder/decoder with the new CHAN constant only; the ESP32-S3 <-> Teensy1 link at 921600 8N1 is already tested and must not be touched.
- Calibrate rather than trust: at boot, address a channel hard-wired to GND and one hard-wired to 3.3 V and store the two raw codes. Every other reading becomes ratiometric against those. This cancels the '4051 Ron drop, the leakage offset, the ADC's -0.03 %FSV offset error and 0.01 %FSV gain error in one step, and it costs you two of the 64 channels.

### Pitfalls

- Wiring Y0..Y7 to pins 1..8 in order. The channel-to-pin map is 13,14,15,12,1,5,2,4 — non-monotonic and non-obvious. The mux works perfectly; your channel numbers are scrambled. Symptom looks like a firmware indexing bug and you will look there first.
- Leaving VEE (pin 7) unconnected. The part still appears to work at first — the level shifter limps along on leakage — then Ron drifts into the kilohms and readings become a slowly-moving mid-scale number that tracks whichever channel you read previously. It never rails, never zeroes, never throws an error. Check pin 7 to pin 8 continuity with a meter before writing a line of code.
- Skipping the discarded read. With R_source >= 1 kohm the SAR sample cap arrives holding the previous channel's voltage and charge-shares with the bus: 0.79 % of the step, up to ~32 LSB at 12-bit, and the error is PROPORTIONAL TO THE PREVIOUS CHANNEL. Every reading is a small function of its neighbour in the scan order. Reorder the scan and the numbers change. This is the most convincingly-wrong failure in the whole design.
- Assuming break-before-make protects you between packages. Both vendors' BBM claim is about channels within ONE device. During a '138 address transition two decoder outputs can be LOW simultaneously for a few nanoseconds, shorting two analog sources through ~500 ohm. Nothing in either datasheet prevents this. Only the E3 global deselect does.
- Trusting any 3.3 V number for Ron, ton or toff. Not one of them is specified. If a number in your design notes says '70 ohm at 3.3 V', it came from the 4.5 V rail spec and is roughly 3-4x optimistic.
- Believing the -3 dB bandwidth. The datasheet's 170-180 MHz is measured into 50 ohm. Your bussed node is ~250 ohm into ~250 pF, giving ~2.5 MHz — 70x lower. If you ever use a leaf for anything AC, use the RC, not the datasheet figure.
- Using the 74LS245 (or anything LS) anywhere near this. You own exactly one '245 type and it is LS: TTL thresholds and wrong VOH at 3.3 V. Also do not substitute 74HCT4051 for 74HC4051 — HCT needs a 4.5-5.5 V VCC and its VIH of 2.0 V is not what a 3.3 V Teensy GPIO guarantees under load.
- Tying VEE to a negative rail 'because the datasheet mentions -5 V'. With VEE below GND, the analog range extends below your ADC's 0 V floor and any negative excursion forward-biases the ADC pin's own protection into the Teensy. VEE = GND, always, for this design.
- Reading a floating Yn. A selected-but-unconnected channel leaves the 250 pF bus floating; the ADC returns the last value slowly decaying through leakage. It looks like a real, drifting sensor. Tie unused channels to GND through 100 kohm.
- Expecting the INA219 with its stock 0.1 ohm shunt to show you mux quiescent current. 10 uV shunt LSB / 0.1 ohm = 100 uA per count; a healthy '4051 draws under 8 uA. You will read zero and conclude the chip is unpowered. It will show latch-up (tens of mA) fine.
- Expecting the 8-channel 24 MHz logic analyser to measure the '138's 15-30 ns propagation delay. One sample period is 41.67 ns; you are below Nyquist for that edge and will get aliased garbage that looks like a plausible measurement. Measure firmware-generated windows (hundreds of ns to us), not gate delays.
- Assuming 12 real bits at the end of all this. NXP's own ENOB for this ADC is 10.1 bits minimum / 10.7 typical WITH 32x hardware averaging and no mux in front of it, and PJRC says 10 bits is what is normally usable. Settling to 1/2 LSB at 12-bit is the right target because it makes the mux a non-contributor — it does not create bits that the ADC does not have.
- Building eight leaves per stack and then discovering you cannot build the second stack. Ten '4051 in inventory, eight per 64-channel tree.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Prove VEE is grounded, before anything else. Multimeter continuity, pin 7 to pin 8, on every one of the eight packages. Expect < 1 ohm. This is 30 seconds and it eliminates the worst silent failure in the design.
- Measure Ron at 3.3 V (the missing datasheet number). Fit a 1.00 kohm 1 % resistor from Z (pin 3) to GND. Drive one Yn from 3.3 V through nothing. Enable that channel. Measure V at Z with a multimeter. Ron = 1000 * (3.3 - Vz) / Vz. Repeat with Yn driven from a 3.3 V/2 divider (mid-rail, ~1.65 V) to get the peak Ron, and at Yn = 0.3 V and 3.0 V to get the rail values. Repeat for all eight channels of one package to get your real delta-Ron. Substitute the mid-rail figure into every t_settle calculation. Expect somewhere in 180-400 ohm; if you get < 100 ohm your part is not running at 3.3 V, and if you get > 1 kohm you have a CD4051B or a floating VEE.
- Measure C_bus (the other estimated number). Disconnect the ADC pin. Drive the Z bus through a known 10.0 kohm from a Teensy GPIO toggling at 10 kHz, all leaves disabled. Capture the Z node rise on the logic analyser using its own input threshold as the trip point — the time from edge to trip gives you tau within about 20 %. Better: if you have a scope, measure the RC directly. C_bus = tau / 10 kohm. Expect 200-300 pF. If you measure much more, your breadboard layout is the problem, not the chips.
- Prove the break-before-make window actually exists in firmware. Deliberately stretch the dead time to 5 us for this test. LA channels: [0]=S0, [1]=S1, [2]=S2, [3]='138 A0, [4]='138 A1, [5]='138 A2, [6]='138 E3, [7]= one '138 output (say Y0). At 24 MHz (41.67 ns/sample) a 5 us window is 120 samples — trivially resolvable. Confirm: E3 goes LOW before ANY of S0..S2 or A0..A2 moves, and E3 goes HIGH only after all six have been stable for the full dead time. Then shrink the dead time to your production 500 ns (12 samples) and confirm the ordering still holds. Do NOT try to measure the '138's own 15-30 ns propagation delay with this instrument — one sample period is 41.67 ns and you will get aliased nonsense.
- Prove the settle window with a marker pin. Add an 8th LA channel on a spare GPIO that the firmware sets HIGH at the start of t_settle and LOW when analogRead() is entered. Confirm the measured width matches your computed t_settle to within one sample period. This is the only way to catch a delayMicroseconds() that got optimised or interrupted.
- Prove the discarded read matters, empirically. Hard-wire channel 0 to GND and channel 1 to 3.3 V. Scan 0,1,0,1,... at 12-bit. With the discard read in place, channel 0 should read 0-3 counts and channel 1 should read 4092-4095. Comment out the discard and re-run: channel 0 should jump to roughly 32 counts and channel 1 drop to roughly 4063 (0.79 % of a full-scale step). Seeing that ~32-count shift appear and disappear is direct confirmation of the charge-sharing model and of your C_bus estimate. If the shift is bigger than 32 counts, C_bus is smaller than you think; if smaller, larger.
- Prove leakage-limited offset. Connect one channel to GND through 10.0 kohm and read it with all other channels floating, then again with all other channels tied to 3.3 V. The difference, divided by 10 kohm, is your real total off-channel leakage at 3.3 V (the number no datasheet gives at this voltage). Expect tens of nA, i.e. a difference of only a few counts. If you see hundreds of counts of shift you have a real leakage or feedthrough problem.
- Latch-up alarm with the INA219. Put one INA219 in the 3.3 V feed to all eight '4051 packages, stock 0.1 ohm shunt, PGA at +/-40 mV. Normal reading will be 0 counts (quiescent current is under 8 uA per package, far below the 100 uA current LSB — this is expected, not a fault). Any reading above a few counts (i.e. above ~0.5 mA) during a channel scan means a channel is being driven into a rail or a device has latched. Poll it once per full scan and log it; the whole point is that it reads zero until something is badly wrong.
- Prove the one-hot invariant over a full scan. Free-run a 64-channel scan and capture 8 of the '138 outputs is impossible with 8 channels once you include the address lines — so do it in two passes: pass A captures '138 Y0..Y7 only (all 8 LA channels), and you assert that at no sample are two of them LOW simultaneously. Pass B captures the address and enable lines as above. Pass A is the one that actually proves no two leaves are ever enabled together.

### Open questions for the builder

- What is the actual measured Ron of YOUR 74HC4051 parts at VCC = 3.3 V, VEE = 0, measured at Vis = 1.65 V (mid-rail, the worst case)? Every timing number I gave scales linearly with it, and no datasheet contains it.
- Are your 74HC4051s Nexperia, TI, or an unbranded Chinese part? The ESD rating differs 4x (2000 V vs 500 V HBM) and unbranded parts frequently turn out to be CD4051B (not HC), which has roughly 2-4x the Ron at 3.3 V and completely different timing. Read the part marking.
- What is the actual source impedance of each of the 64 things you intend to connect? The answer changes t_settle by a factor of 25 between a 100 ohm divider and a 10 kohm thermistor, and it determines whether you need buffers at all.
- Can you spare a seventh GPIO on Teensy1 for the '138 E3 global deselect, or do you want the 74HC595 approach (1 dedicated RCLK + the shared SPI bus)? This is the only real design decision left.
- Do you want ONE 64-channel tree (8 of your 10 '4051) or TWO 32-channel trees (4 leaves each, 8 chips, one per stack)? You cannot have two 64-channel trees without buying six more '4051.
- Is the mux bus going on a breadboard or on a soldered protoboard? Breadboard adds roughly 2-5 pF per contact column plus significant coupling between adjacent rails; it can push C_bus from 250 pF toward 350 pF and it will make the crosstalk numbers meaningless.
- Do you have any way to measure resistance and DC voltage at all (a multimeter)? The Ron measurement and the VEE continuity check both need one; neither the logic analyser nor the INA219 can do it.
- Are any of the 64 intended signals capable of going below 0 V or above 3.3 V, even transiently at power-up? If yes, those channels need the 1 kohm series resistor and I need to know so the settle time for them is budgeted.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

none. All content in this pass came from manufacturer PDFs (Nexperia 74HC_HCT4051 Rev.12, TI SCHS122O, TI SBOS513F, TI INA219, Microchip DS20001733L, NXP IMXRT1060CEC Rev.4) which I downloaded and converted to text locally with pdftotext/pymupdf rather than trusting a summariser, plus source files from github.com/PaulStoffregen/cores. No instruction-shaped text, no fake system-reminders, no attribution-changing directives appeared in any of it. One item worth noting but NOT an injection: a Microchip URL returned an HTTP 301 whose Location header the fetch tool explicitly labelled 'server-supplied, not verified'. I did not follow it, so the MCP6021/22/23/24 specs remain unverified in this report — that is a gap, not a compromise.

---

## Honest performance envelope: Rockchip RV1103 (Luckfox Pico Mini B) as "the CPU" vs Raspberry Pi 5 (BCM2712), and what the heterogeneous stack genuinely wins at

### Bottom line

The RV1103 is not a CPU, it is a camera SoC with a CPU attached. Rockchip's own Rev 1.5 datasheet contains no display controller, no HDMI, no MIPI-DSI, no GPU, and no video decoder — only a video ENCODER and MIPI-CSI input. That is silicon, not software: the Luckfox physically cannot render a desktop under any firmware, so "Pi 5 replacement" is off the table before benchmarks start. On general-purpose integer work one RV1103 core is roughly 4x slower than one Pi 5 core, and it takes an estimated 14-20 Luckfoxes to match a single Pi 5's whole-machine 7-zip throughput; that number is a fantasy because the only interconnect is a 921600-baud UART moving 92,160 B/s, which is about 57,000x slower than a Pi 5's own measured 5,270 MB/s memcpy, and because a real Luckfox has 34,316 kB of MemTotal against a Pi 5's 8 GB. The Pi 5 also wins on performance-per-watt at general compute (~1135 vs ~630 7-zip MIPS/W), so there is no efficiency consolation prize either. What the stack genuinely wins is the time domain, not the throughput domain: NXP measured 11 core cycles (18.33 ns) from GPIO edge to ISR instruction on a 600 MHz Cortex-M7, against Linux GPIO paths that show ~2 us average with 150 us spikes and sub-millisecond hard-real-time floors, a determinism advantage of roughly 8,000x that a Pi 5 cannot buy at any price. Two further real wins: the RV1103 has a hardware H.264/H.265 encoder (4 MP@30fps, 60 Mbps) and a 0.5 TOPS NPU, and the Pi 5 has neither an encoder nor an NPU.
