### Implementation guidance

- FRAME ARITHMETIC - hard-code these as compile-time constants and assert against them: at 921600 8N1, BIT_PERIOD_NS = 1085, BYTE_PERIOD_NS = 10851, WIRE_BYTES_PER_SEC = 92160. Frame total bytes = LEN + 6 (START, LEN_H, LEN_L, SEQ, CRC_H, CRC_L are outside LEN). Minimum frame = 9 bytes = 97.66 us. Max 1024-byte frame = 11.111 ms. Compute expected wire time in the sender and log actual-vs-expected; a mismatch is your first sign of a baud or FIFO problem.
- TEENSY UART - THE HIGHEST-PRIORITY FIX: the RT1062 LPUART has only a 4-entry FIFO, giving 43.4 us of tolerance at 921600. Move both protocol UARTs to eDMA RX with a circular buffer in RAM2 (DMA-optimised) and a half/full-complete interrupt, rather than relying on the byte-interrupt path. Until you do, audit every noInterrupts()/critical section and every blocking SPI transfer for windows longer than 43.4 us. Instrument this: keep a per-link counter of framing errors, overrun errors and CRC failures, exposed over channel 0x00 (transport) as a new unsolicited status command.
- TEENSY ISR PLACEMENT: put every hard-real-time ISR and its data in tightly-coupled memory (RAM1) using FASTRUN for code and DMAMEM only for buffers the DMA touches. NXP's 18.33 ns figure is explicitly 'code runs from the TCM with no wait states' (AN12078 section 5). An ISR that runs from flash-backed ITCM-miss or hits a D-cache miss will not reproduce it.
- TEENSY TIMESTAMPING: use the ARM DWT cycle counter (32-bit at 600 MHz, 1.667 ns/tick) as the primary clock for all latency measurement. It wraps every 7.158 s, so store a 64-bit extended counter by accumulating wraps in a 1 kHz tick. Never use micros() for anything you intend to publish - it quantises to 1 us, which is 600 cycles.
- REPRODUCE AN12078 EXACTLY on the RT1062: configure a GPIO to interrupt on a falling edge; in the ISR, toggle a second GPIO twice with the minimal instruction sequence (LDR address, STR set, MOVS clear, STR clear). Measure t1 = stimulus falling edge to indicator rising edge, t2 = indicator rising to falling. Then latency = t1 - (t2 + 2 cycles). This subtraction cancels the instruction overhead and is the only defensible way to get a real number. It needs a scope, not the 24 MS/s LA.
- LUCKFOX UART - enable DMA on ttyS3/ttyS4 if the Rockchip 8250 driver supports it on this SoC (check dmesg for 'DMA' on the serial probe and check the device tree for dmas/dma-names on the uart nodes). Without DMA, the 64-byte RX FIFO gives 694 us of scheduling slack. Verify empirically before trusting it.
- LUCKFOX PROCESS TUNING for the link handler: set SCHED_FIFO with sched_setscheduler() at a priority below the kernel's IRQ threads, call mlockall(MCL_CURRENT|MCL_FUTURE) to avoid page-fault stalls (critical with only 33.5 MB), and pin IRQ affinity for the UART to the single A7 core. Measure dropped-frame count before and after - that delta is a publishable result.
- LUCKFOX MEMORY - reduce CMA from 24576 kB to 1024 kB in the kernel command line if you are not using the camera/ISP. Measured effect: MemTotal rises from 34,316 kB to 57,372 kB, a 67% increase. Verify with /proc/meminfo and record both MemTotal and CmaTotal in any published result, because a benchmark run at the wrong CMA setting is not comparable.
- LUCKFOX RAM GUARD: with a 33.5 MB ceiling, add an explicit RSS watchdog to the link daemon. Read /proc/self/statm every second; if RSS exceeds a configured fraction (say 60% of MemTotal), emit an unsolicited transport-channel warning frame and shed load rather than letting the OOM killer take the process. An OOM kill on 'the CPU' node looks identical to a link failure from the Teensy's point of view - make it distinguishable.
- MCP23017 - clamp the I2C bus to 400 kHz in firmware and add a compile-time assertion that the configured rate is <= 400000, with a comment citing Microchip DS20001952C Table 1-3 (1.7 MHz mode requires 4.5-5.5 V). Do not let a future edit raise it to 1 MHz on a 3.3 V bus.
- MCP23017 ISR (confirms and sharpens your existing rule): with MIRROR=1 and ODR=1, the shared INT line is open-drain and wire-ORed, so the ISR must read BOTH INTCAPA and INTCAPB (or GPIOA and GPIOB) on every expander in the chain before it can safely clear. Reading only one port leaves the other port's interrupt latched and the shared line asserted forever - a silent hang, not a crash. Use sequential addressing to read both ports in one transaction (~140 us at 400 kHz).
- 74HC138 - the datasheet gives no 3.3 V timing. Do not assume any number. Set the inter-transaction guard time from your own measurement of An-to-Yn delay at 3.3 V, then add 100% margin. Until measured, use a conservative 100 ns guard (which is the 2.0 V max of 150 ns scaled down, not a guarantee). Keep using the enable pin for break-before-make global deselect - that is correct and it makes the guard time a non-issue for the CS lines themselves.
- PROTOCOL EXTENSION for measurement (stay inside the existing frame format - do not reimplement it): add commands on channel 0x00 (transport) for ECHO (payload returned verbatim, for RTT), TIMESTAMP_REQ/TIMESTAMP_RSP (peer returns its own cycle counter, for clock-offset estimation), and LINK_STATS (frames TX, frames RX, CRC errors, framing errors, overruns, SEQ gaps, max observed inter-frame gap). Use the SEQ bit7 unsolicited class for periodic LINK_STATS pushes so the measurement traffic does not perturb the request/response timing you are trying to measure.
- MEASUREMENT MARKER PINS: dedicate one GPIO per node purely as an LA marker, driven high on entry to the event of interest and low on exit. On the WROOM-32E display node the free pins he identified are 35 and 39 (input-only, so unusable as markers) and 25 - use GPIO25 as the display node's marker. Do not repurpose any of the known onboard pins.
- INA219 CONFIGURATION for power-per-operation: set 12-bit with 128-sample averaging (68.10 ms conversion) for the steady-state mean, and a separate 9-bit / 84 us configuration only if you want to see coarse transients. Read the bus voltage and shunt voltage registers, not the calibrated current register, and do the maths in float on the host - this removes the Calibration-register rounding error from your published figure. Record the shunt resistance you actually measured with a 4-wire method, not the nominal 0.1 ohm.

### Pitfalls

- Quoting '64MB RAM' for the Luckfox. The real, measured figure is MemTotal 34,316 kB with the stock 24 MB CMA reservation. Any capacity planning done against 64 MB is wrong by a factor of nearly two, and the failure mode is an OOM kill that looks exactly like a link dropout.
- Quoting '0.5 TOPS' as achieved NPU throughput. It is a peak MAC-count figure. With a 16-bit DDR2 bus (2.1 GB/s optimistic peak) and 33.5 MB of RAM, the memory system cannot feed it. Publishing 0.5 TOPS without a measured inferences/second on a named model would be the single most embarrassing claim in the whole project.
- Believing the Luckfox can ever drive a display. The RV1103 datasheet Rev 1.5 has no VOP, no HDMI, no DSI, no LCD controller and no GPU. Framebuffer benchmarks people publish for this board are running over VNC to another computer's screen. Do not design any part of the system on the assumption that a display could later be attached to the Luckfox.
- Assuming the RV1103 can decode video. It encodes only. If any part of the design involves the Luckfox playing back or transcoding an incoming stream, that work lands on the single A7 core in software and will not keep up.
- Assuming 528 MHz means what you think it means. Rockchip's ddrbin guide lists 'DDR2: 528MHz' without units stated. If it is the DRAM clock, peak is 2.112 GB/s; if it is the data rate, 1.056 GB/s. A 2x error in the bandwidth figure will silently propagate into every bandwidth-ratio claim you publish. Read it off the running kernel before quoting it.
- The Teensy's 4-entry LPUART FIFO. This is the least obvious and most dangerous number in the stack: 43.4 us of slack at 921600, 16x less than the Luckfox's and 32x less than the ESP32-S3's. Any interrupt-disabled window longer than that - a blocking SPI blit to the 2.4in display, a long critical section, a flash write - drops bytes mid-frame. The CRC will catch it, so it presents as an unexplained error rate rather than a crash, which is exactly the kind of bug that gets blamed on wiring for weeks.
- Trying to measure 18.33 ns of interrupt latency with a 24 MS/s logic analyser. One sample is 41.667 ns. A single-shot capture of the M7 IRQ latency is pure quantisation noise. The LA can measure the DISTRIBUTION of latency over many events (jitter width in samples) but never the absolute value. Publishing an LA-derived absolute interrupt latency would be a confident wrong number.
- Trying to measure per-transaction energy with an INA219. The fastest 12-bit conversion is 532 us, which is 5.4x longer than a 97.66 us minimum protocol frame. The INA219 measures average power over milliseconds. Energy-per-operation is only obtainable by dividing an averaged power delta by a known sustained operation rate.
- Assuming 1.7 MHz I2C is available. The MCP23017's 1.7 MHz mode is specified only for VDD 4.5-5.5 V. On a 3.3 V fabric the part is a 400 kHz device, full stop, and that caps the entire IO transaction rate at roughly 7,140 two-port reads per second per bus.
- Assuming a 74HC138 propagation delay at 3.3 V. Neither Nexperia nor TI publishes a 3.3 V switching table for this part. Interpolation between the 2.0 V and 4.5 V columns is an engineering guess, not a guaranteed limit, and CMOS propagation delay is strongly non-linear with supply voltage near the lower end.
- Only two UARTs are free on the Luckfox (UART3/ttyS3 and UART4/ttyS4); UART2 is the debug console. If you consume both for protocol links you lose your console, which on a headless board with 33 MB of RAM is the difference between debugging a hang and power-cycling blind.
- Expecting a perf/watt win. The Pi 5 is roughly 1.8x better at MIPS per watt on integer work. The Luckfox's power advantage is in absolute draw and in fixed-function silicon (ISP/encoder/NPU), not in efficiency of general-purpose computation. Claiming otherwise is checkable and wrong.
- Cascading the ratio argument. '16 Luckfoxes equal a Pi 5' is arithmetically defensible on 7-zip and completely misleading in practice: 16 Luckfoxes have 536 MiB of RAM split across 16 unshared address spaces, connected by links running at 1/57,000th of a Pi 5's memory bandwidth. There is no workload where that substitution works.
- Attaching the AR9271 USB WiFi to the Luckfox Pico Mini B. The board has one USB 2.0 port which is also the power, console and RNDIS path. The AR9271 draws several hundred milliamps and would need an OTG splitter plus separate supply. UNVERIFIED whether the stock Luckfox kernel even has the ath9k_htc driver and firmware - check before planning around it.
- Thermal throttling. The RV1103's 32.1 C/W junction-to-ambient is quoted against a JEDEC 4-layer 114.3 x 76.2 mm board. The Pico Mini's 28.14 x 21.00 mm PCB is dramatically worse, so sustained-load benchmarks will drift downward. Any published throughput number needs a die-temperature trace alongside it or it is not reproducible.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- LINK ROUND-TRIP LATENCY. Probe TX and RX of one link on two LA channels. Teensy1 sends a 9-byte ECHO command; peer returns a 9-byte response. Measure from the falling edge of the request's first START bit to the falling edge of the response's first START bit. Wire floor is 195.32 us (two 97.66 us frames); anything above that is peer turnaround. GOOD RESULT: median RTT under 300 us (i.e. peer turnaround under 105 us), p99.9 under 400 us, over 100,000 trials. Report the full histogram, not the mean. LA resolution 41.67 ns is 4,700x finer than the quantity measured - this test is well within instrument capability.
- SUSTAINED FRAME THROUGHPUT PER LINK. Stream 1,000,000 frames of fixed 64-byte payload (73 bytes on the wire, 792.1 us each) for 13 minutes. Count frames on the LA's UART decoder and independently in firmware; compare. GOOD RESULT: >= 95% of the 1,262 frames/s wire ceiling sustained (i.e. >= 1,199 frames/s), zero CRC failures, zero SEQ gaps, and LA-decoded count matching firmware count exactly. Any divergence between the two counts localises the loss to a specific node's FIFO.
- FIFO OVERRUN THRESHOLD (the predicted failure). Run the throughput test above while a second thread on the peer deliberately blocks interrupts for a swept duration: 10, 20, 40, 43.4, 60, 100, 200, 500, 694, 1000 us. Plot CRC-error rate against blocked duration. PREDICTION: the Teensy link breaks sharply above ~43.4 us (4-entry LPUART FIFO) and the Luckfox link above ~694 us (64-byte FIFO). GOOD RESULT: the measured knee lands within 20% of the predicted value, which validates the FIFO model - and if the Teensy knee is at 43 us, that is a publishable finding that most Teensy users do not know.
- INTERRUPT-TO-ACTION JITTER, TEENSY. Wire a stimulus pin to an interrupt pin. Toggle the stimulus at a pseudo-random interval, and in the ISR toggle a response pin. Capture 1,000,000 events on the LA. Report max minus min in SAMPLES, not the absolute latency. GOOD RESULT: max - min <= 2 samples (<= 83.3 ns) with all ISR code in FASTRUN and buffers in DTCM, running concurrently with full UART, SPI and I2C traffic. A spread of 3+ samples means you have a priority inversion, a cache miss on the ISR path, or code executing from non-TCM memory. IMPORTANT: this test measures jitter, not latency - the LA cannot resolve the 18.33 ns absolute figure.
- INTERRUPT-TO-ACTION JITTER, LUCKFOX. Identical wiring, using libgpiod with an edge-triggered line request from userspace, process at SCHED_FIFO with mlockall. Capture for 1 hour under `stress-ng --cpu 1 --vm 1 --vm-bytes 16M` load. GOOD RESULT: whatever it is, publish it honestly next to the Teensy histogram on the same axes. EXPECTATION from the literature: median in the tens of microseconds with outliers into the hundreds of microseconds or milliseconds. The side-by-side histogram is the single most persuasive artefact this project can produce.
- IO TRANSACTION RATE, MCP23017 FABRIC. Probe SCL and SDA. Teensy1 loops reading GPIOA+GPIOB from one expander with sequential addressing at 400 kHz. Count complete transactions per second on the LA and measure the inter-transaction gap (that gap is pure Teensy software overhead). GOOD RESULT: >= 6,500 two-port reads/s against the ~7,140/s bus ceiling (>= 91% bus utilisation), with inter-transaction gap < 15 us. LA gives 60 samples per SCL period - ample.
- MCP23017 INTERRUPT SERVICE LATENCY. Probe the shared open-drain INT line and the Teensy's I2C SCL. Toggle an expander input; measure from the INT line falling edge to the first SCL edge of the servicing read, and to the STOP of the final read that clears both ports. GOOD RESULT: INT-to-first-SCL under 20 us; INT-to-fully-cleared under 200 us (one ~140 us two-port read plus overhead). VERIFY the INT line actually returns high after the ISR - if it stays low, your ISR is not reading both ports, which is the classic MIRROR+ODR bug.
- 74HC138 PROPAGATION DELAY AT 3.3 V (a measurement nobody publishes). Probe A0, A1, A2 and one Yn output. Drive an address change and measure An-to-Yn at the 1.65 V crossing on both edges, over 10,000 transitions, with the real fabric capacitance loaded. Note: at 41.67 ns/sample this is a 1-sample-resolution measurement of a ~20 ns quantity, so it is only usable as an upper bound - report it as '<= 42 ns at 3.3 V with CL = X pF' rather than a precise figure, or use a scope for the real number. GOOD RESULT: an upper bound you can then double for your firmware guard time.
- BREAK-BEFORE-MAKE VERIFICATION. Probe the '138 enable pin and any two Yn outputs. Confirm on every chip-select change that both Yn lines are simultaneously deasserted for at least one full sample period. GOOD RESULT: zero instances in 100,000 selection changes where two CS lines are simultaneously active. This is a pass/fail safety test, and the LA is perfectly suited to it.
- END-TO-END LATENCY, RADIO TO PIXEL. Five LA channels: (1) an ESP32-S3 GPIO toggled inside the ESP-NOW receive callback, (2) the S3-to-Teensy1 UART TX line, (3) a Teensy1 GPIO toggled on successful CRC validation, (4) the UART line to the display node, (5) the display node's GPIO25 toggled after the final ST7796 write completes. Measure (1) to (5). BUDGET: S3 callback-to-UART-start < 200 us, frame on wire 97.66 us, Teensy decode < 50 us, second frame 97.66 us, display draw = measure it. GOOD RESULT: total under 5 ms with p99 under 10 ms. Publish the per-stage breakdown - the stage that dominates is your optimisation target and you will not guess it correctly in advance.
- POWER PER OPERATION (INA219, one per node). Configure 12-bit with 128-sample averaging (68.10 ms). Measure (a) 60 s idle baseline, (b) 60 s at a known sustained frame rate R. Energy per frame = (P_load - P_idle) / R. GOOD RESULT: report microjoules per frame for each node with +/-5% repeatability across three independent runs, plus the idle baseline separately. EXPECT the Teensy to be dramatically better per-frame than the Luckfox because it does not carry a Linux kernel's idle overhead - that is a real and defensible win for the architecture.
- THE HONEST 'IS THE LUCKFOX ACTUALLY THE CPU' TEST. Run the workload you genuinely intend it to do (NPU inference, or JSON/compression handling of fleet data) while logging /proc/self/statm RSS every second and the die temperature from the TSADC. GOOD RESULT: peak RSS under 25 MB against the 34,316 kB MemTotal, no OOM kill over a 1-hour soak, and the workload completing within a latency budget that the Teensys could not have met themselves. FAILURE IS ALSO A RESULT: if the answer is that the Teensys could have done it faster with less power, that is the scientific truth and it is worth knowing before the second stack is built.

### Open questions for the builder

- What does /sys/kernel/debug/clk/clk_summary (or the dmc node) report for the DDR clock on your actual Luckfox? This settles the 1.056 vs 2.112 GB/s question and I could not resolve it from documentation.
- What does `7z b` report for single-thread MIPS on your Luckfox, and what does tinymembench report for memcpy/memset? These two runs replace my entire estimated range (550-790 MIPS, bandwidth unknown) with measured numbers directly comparable to sbc-bench's Pi 5 figures.
- Does `dmesg | grep -i -E 'ttyS3|ttyS4|dma'` show DMA attached to the Luckfox's user UARTs, or is it running in PIO with only the 64-byte FIFO? This determines whether 694 us or something much shorter is your real scheduling-stall budget.
- Is the 2.4in display on Teensy1 an ST7789 SPI TFT (per the BOM) or something else? The controller determines the SPI clock, which determines whether your logic analyser can decode that bus at all (>6 MHz is not decodable at 24 MS/s) and whether the blit blocks interrupts long enough to overrun the Teensy's 43.4 us LPUART FIFO window.
- What SPI clock are you actually running to the display, and does the driver disable interrupts across the transfer? This is the specific mechanism by which I expect your link error rate to be non-zero.
- Is the RISC-V MCU in the RV1103 usable from your image, and at what clock? The datasheet describes an MCU with 16 KB cache and a mailbox to the A7 but never names the ISA or the frequency; the 400 MHz RISC-V figure is third-party. If it is usable, it is a second deterministic core you already own and are not using.
- Do you intend to attach a MIPI-CSI camera to either Luckfox? If not, the ISP, the video encoder and most of the NPU's reason to exist are idle, and the honest framing changes from 'camera SoC' to '33 MB Linux box with two spare UARTs'.
- What shunt resistance do your INA219 boards actually have, measured 4-wire? The 0.1 ohm nominal has a tolerance that directly sets the accuracy floor of every power number you publish.
- Is your logic analyser sustaining a true 24 MS/s on all 8 channels for the capture durations you need, or is it dropping samples? 8 ch x 24 MS/s = 24 MB/s over USB 2.0 bulk, which is marginal. Capture a known-frequency square wave for 60 s and check for missing edges before trusting any long capture.
- What is the Luckfox's measured idle and loaded current on an INA219, and what die temperature does it reach under sustained load on the Pico Mini's small PCB? Both of my power figures for this board are soft third-party numbers.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

None. I fetched the Rockchip RV1103 Datasheet Rev 1.5 PDF, NXP AN12078 PDF, TI INA219 and SN74HC138 PDFs, the Nexperia 74HC138 PDF, the Microchip MCP23017 PDF, plus HTML/markdown from raspberrypi.com, pjrc.com, nxp.com, wiki.luckfox.com, github.com (ThomasKaiser/sbc-bench, geerlingguy/sbc-reviews, rockchip-linux/rkbin, themrleon/luckfox-pico-mini-b), docs.espressif.com and cnx-software.com. No instruction-shaped text, no fake system-reminder blocks, and no attempts to alter commit attribution or any other behaviour appeared in any of it. I treated all fetched content as data throughout. One note on source hygiene rather than injection: several search-result summaries paraphrased datasheets rather than quoting them, so wherever a number mattered I extracted the PDF text locally with pdftotext and read the actual table - the MCP23017 3.3V/400kHz limit and the absence of any 3.3V timing column for the 74HC138 were both found that way and both contradict what a casual summary would have told you.

---

## Interconnecting two identical Luckfox/Teensy/ESP32 stacks: link options, addressing extension, and cross-stack time sync

### Bottom line

The best stack-to-stack link is Teensy1<->Teensy1 over the Teensy 4.1's own DP83825I PHY (two PJRC Ethernet Kits, straight CAT5 - the PHY has Auto-MDIX on by default): 94 Mbit/s measured with lwIP, ~650 us RTT, and it doubles as the PTP transport with a published Teensy-to-Teensy accuracy of sigma = 40.7 ns. The Luckfox Pico Mini B does have Ethernet - the RV1103 contains a 10/100 MAC with an embedded FEPHY and the Pico Mini breaks ETH_T_P/ETH_T_N/ETH_R_P/ETH_R_N + GND out to a 5-pad "Header 5" - but it needs external magnetics and gmac enabled in the DTS, and no one has published a throughput number, so treat it as unmeasured. USB gadget between two Luckfoxes is the worst option: both USB-C ports are hardwired as sinks (5.1k pulldowns on CC1/CC2, VBUS feeds VCC5V0_SYS), so two boards cannot enumerate over a C-to-C cable at all without a butchered cable and external VBUS. ESP-NOW is fine as a low-rate out-of-band channel (5.6 ms median RTT for a 12-byte payload, 250 B/frame in v1.0) but must never carry timing or bulk. For addressing, add ONE new channel (0x05) carrying a routed envelope rather than smuggling a node-id into existing payloads - that keeps every existing per-channel handler byte-identical and makes an old node fail loudly instead of silently mis-parsing. Critically, the RV1103's GMAC appears to be synthesised WITHOUT the IEEE 1588 timestamp block (MAC_HW_Feature0 = 0x400103E1, TSSEL bit 12 = 0, PPSOUTNUM = 0), so the Luckfox cannot be the time reference - a Teensy must be grandmaster.

### Implementation guidance

- INTERCONNECT - recommended primary: Teensy1(stack1) <-> Teensy1(stack2) direct Ethernet. Buy 2x PJRC Ethernet Kit. Solder the 0.1 uF cap, the 3x2 2mm headers and the magjack per the kit board; the Teensy pad group is RX+ / RX- on one row, LED / GND on the middle, TX- / TX+ on the third (PJRC card11b_rev4). Use a straight CAT5e patch cable - DP83825I Auto-MDIX is on by default, so no crossover and no switch.
- Use QNEthernet (not NativeEthernet). Before expecting more than 68 Mbit/s, set TCP_SND_BUF and TCP_WND to 8*TCP_MSS (11680 bytes) in lwipopts.h - that is the difference between the measured 68 and 94 Mbit/s.
- For the inter-stack data plane prefer UDP over TCP: the existing frame format already has SEQ, CRC and length, so TCP's reliability is redundant and its window control is what costs the RTT. Send one project frame per UDP datagram, max 1024 bytes, well under the 1472-byte non-fragmenting payload of a 1500 MTU link.
- ADDRESSING - add exactly one new channel, 0x05 = ROUTE. Do not touch FLAGS, do not change any existing payload. Envelope, as the payload of a 0x05 frame: DST_NODE(1) | SRC_NODE(1) | ORIG_SEQ(1) | INNER_CHAN(1) | INNER_CMD(1) | INNER_PAYLOAD(0..1008) | INNER_CRC_H | INNER_CRC_L. Wire layout is therefore START|LEN_H|LEN_L|SEQ|FLAGS|0x05|CMD|<envelope>|CRC_H|CRC_L.
- Exact size budget for the envelope: max frame 1024, overhead 6 (START, LEN_H, LEN_L, SEQ, CRC_H, CRC_L), so max LEN = 1018; FLAGS+CHAN+CMD consume 3, leaving 1015 bytes of outer payload. The envelope header (DST, SRC, ORIG_SEQ, INNER_CHAN, INNER_CMD) is 5 bytes and INNER_CRC is 2, so INNER_PAYLOAD max = 1008 bytes. Enforce this with a compile-time assert.
- Node ID encoding: high nibble = stack, low nibble = node. 0x10 = stack1 Luckfox, 0x11 = stack1 Teensy1, 0x12 = stack1 Teensy2, 0x1A = stack1 ESP32-S3, 0x1D = stack1 display node; 0x20..0x2D likewise for stack 2. Reserve 0x00 = 'the peer on this link' (backwards-compatible no-op) and 0xFF = broadcast. Never assign 0x00 to a real node.
- Channel 0x05 CMD namespace: 0x01 = FORWARD (envelope as above), 0x02 = FORWARD_ACK, 0x03 = ROUTE_UNREACHABLE (payload = DST that failed), 0x04 = ROUTE_TABLE_GET, 0x05 = ROUTE_TABLE_SET. Keep it to these five - routing does not need more.
- INNER_CRC is CRC-16/MCRF4XX over DST..INNER_PAYLOAD, reusing the existing CRC routine unchanged (reflected poly 0x8408, init 0xFFFF, check 0x6F91). The outer CRC only protects one hop because the router recomputes it; INNER_CRC is what makes a two-hop path end-to-end safe and catches router bugs.
- SEQ handling across a hop: the router MUST rewrite the outer SEQ from its own per-link counter and preserve the origin's SEQ in ORIG_SEQ. Keep a small translation table (32 entries is plenty) mapping outgoing-SEQ -> (SRC_NODE, ORIG_SEQ) so the reply can be un-mapped. Preserve SEQ bit7 (unsolicited class) in both the outer and ORIG_SEQ bytes - if you drop it, unsolicited events from stack 2 will be mistaken for replies.
- Router placement: put the routing function in Teensy1 of each stack, not the Luckfox. Teensy1 is already the sole I2C/SPI master and already terminates every link in its stack, so it is the only node that can satisfy a cross-stack IO request without an extra hop. The Luckfox stays a leaf that speaks 0x05 to its own Teensy1.
- Rejection behaviour: a node that receives channel 0x05 and does not implement it must answer with the existing unknown-channel error. Verify this before shipping - the entire safety argument for choosing a new channel over a payload node-id rests on old firmware failing loudly.
- TIME SYNC - primary: run a hardware sync pulse on one wire between the two Teensy1s, timestamped by the ENET 1588 timer. On the master, configure ENETx_TCSR1 TMODE = 0b1111 (output compare, pulse output high for 1..32 1588-clock cycles per TPWC) and mux Teensy pin 24 to ALT6 (GPIO_AD_B0_12 -> ENET_1588_EVENT1_OUT). On the slave, configure ENETx_TCSR1 TMODE = 0b0001 (input capture on rising edge) and mux Teensy pin 25 to ALT6 (GPIO_AD_B0_13 -> ENET_1588_EVENT1_IN); read the captured counter from ENETx_TCCR1. Run the reverse direction on pins 14/15 (ALT4, EVENT2_OUT/EVENT2_IN) so you can measure and cancel the wire+logic delay by two-way exchange rather than assuming it.
- Discipline, do not step. A single captured edge gives you an offset, not a synchronised clock - the two crystals drift 12000 ns/s typical, 30000 ns/s worst case. Implement the published two-stage servo: coarse (write the offset directly into ENETx_ATVR / use ENETx_ATCOR) while |offset| > 1000 ns, then a PI controller on ENETx_ATINC[INC_CORR] + ENETx_ATCOR with KP = 1.5, KI = 0.3 at a 1 s update period. Expect ~3 s to converge.
- Subtract the PHY asymmetry constant. If you sync over Ethernet PTP rather than the sync wire, the DP83825I has ~200 ns of TX-vs-RX delay asymmetry; put it in one named constant with a comment citing the source, and calibrate it by looping a cable back rather than trusting the 200 ns figure.
- Do NOT let the Luckfox be the time reference. Its GMAC very likely has no IEEE 1588 block. Architecture: stack1 Teensy1 = grandmaster; stack2 Teensy1 = PTP/sync-wire slave; both Luckfoxes and both ESP32-S3s receive a monotonic epoch counter from their local Teensy1 over the existing UART and tag their own logs with it, never with their own free-running clock.
- ESP-NOW: pin the channel explicitly on both S3s with esp_now_peer_info_t.channel set to a fixed value (not 0) and call esp_wifi_set_channel() to match, on both, at boot. If either ever associates to an AP, ESP-NOW is forced onto the AP's channel and the fixed setting silently stops working.
- ESP-NOW frame sizing: keep every mesh-channel (0x04) frame at or below 250 bytes of ESP-NOW payload so it fits one v1.0 packet. If you need the full 1024-byte project frame over the radio, either move to ESP-IDF v5.4+ and ESP-NOW v2.0 (1470 B) on BOTH S3s, or accept the measured 24 ms for a 5-chunk transfer.
- ESP-NOW send pacing: do not call esp_now_send() again until the previous send callback has returned - Espressif explicitly warns that a too-short interval causes the send callbacks to be delivered out of order, which will corrupt any SEQ bookkeeping you layer on top.
- Teensy<->Teensy UART fallback (bring-up before the magjacks arrive): use a spare port (e.g. Serial5 on pins 20/21) so the existing S3 link on Teensy1 is untouched. Start at 921600 to match the rest of the fabric, then step to 2 Mbaud only after the logic analyser shows clean edges. Above ~1 Mbaud use a short twisted pair with a 33-100 ohm series resistor at each driver and a solid ground return in the same twist.
- If you do push the UART to 6 Mbaud, the 4-byte FIFO means an RX interrupt roughly every 6.7 us. Raise the Teensyduino serial buffer with SERIALn_RX_BUFFER_SIZE and measure ISR occupancy on a spare GPIO with the logic analyser before trusting it; the failure mode is dropped bytes mid-frame, which the CRC will catch but which will look like a link-quality problem.
- Luckfox Ethernet enablement: set `&gmac { status = "okay"; };` in <sdk_dir>/config/dts_config, rebuild, and confirm eth0 appears. Ring out the 5 castellated pads to the schematic nets ETH_T_P / ETH_T_N / ETH_R_P / ETH_R_N / GND with a continuity meter BEFORE soldering - the physical order on the silkscreen is not documented anywhere I could find.
- Luckfox Ethernet magnetics: the Pico Mini has no transformer on board (the schematic shows the FEPHY MDI pairs going straight to the header, and FEPHY_REXT terminated with 6.04K 1%). You must add a magjack. The Link-PP LPJ4012AHNL from the PJRC Ethernet Kit is a 10/100 magjack with integrated magnetics and is a suitable part - wire it following the PJRC kit board's MDI/centre-tap pattern.
- Do NOT plan on USB gadget mode between the two Luckfoxes. Both USB-C ports have 5.1k pulldowns on CC1 and CC2 (permanent sink) and VBUS is the board's power input, so two boards on a C-to-C cable will not even attach. If you insist on trying it: power both boards from the VCC5V0_SYS header pad, hand-build a cable carrying only D+/D-/GND, inject 5 V into the device-side VBUS, and set dr_mode = "host" on one via luckfox-config. Treat this as a research experiment, not a design.
- If you ever do run a Linux-to-Linux USB gadget link, use CDC-NCM or CDC-ECM, not RNDIS. RNDIS exists for Windows hosts and is deprecated/being removed in the Linux kernel; between two Linux boards it buys you nothing and costs you a fragile driver path.

### Pitfalls

- THE BIG ONE: the Luckfox wiki's board-variant table says the Pico Mini has no Ethernet, while the schematic and the RV1103 datasheet say it does. Both are 'right' - the table means 'no RJ45 fitted'. Believing the table costs you the best Luckfox-to-Luckfox option; believing it uncritically the other way (assuming a jack exists) costs you a magjack you didn't order.
- Two Luckfox USB-C ports connected together produce absolutely nothing - no error, no log line, no enumeration. Both are UFPs with 5.1k Rd. This looks exactly like 'the gadget driver isn't loaded' and will burn a day in dmesg.
- The RV1103 FEPHY pins are 100BASE-TX differential MDI, not RMII logic. Wiring them to a bare RJ45 with no magnetics may appear to link at short range and then fail intermittently, or fail entirely - either way you will blame the driver. It needs a transformer.
- A 1024-byte project frame does not fit in one ESP-NOW v1.0 packet (250 B limit). If firmware silently truncates rather than chunking, the CRC catches it and it looks like radio interference. If it chunks without waiting for each TX-done callback, Espressif warns the callbacks arrive out of order and your SEQ bookkeeping goes wrong in a way that only shows up under load.
- ESP-NOW's send callback is a transmit-completion notification, not a delivery receipt. Espressif states outright that application-layer reception is not guaranteed. Treating a successful callback as an ACK gives you a link that reports 100% success and loses frames.
- If either ESP32-S3 associates to an AP, ESP-NOW is forced onto that AP's channel. One stack joining WiFi (e.g. for the AR9271-adjacent debugging) silently kills the mesh channel between stacks. This produces zero errors on either side.
- SEQ collision after routing: SEQ is currently a per-link counter. The moment Teensy1 forwards frames from two different sources onto one link, two in-flight requests can carry the same SEQ and the replies get matched to the wrong requests. The result is a plausible-looking wrong answer, not an error. This is why the envelope carries ORIG_SEQ and the router rewrites the outer SEQ.
- Putting a node-id byte at the front of every existing command's payload (the option I am recommending against) is the worst failure mode available here: every payload shifts by one byte, the CRC is still valid, and a node running old firmware decodes garbage and acts on it. Nothing fails loudly.
- The outer CRC only covers one hop. If Teensy1 recomputes the CRC when forwarding - which it must, because it rewrites SEQ - then a bit flipped inside Teensy1's own RAM between receive and retransmit is re-blessed with a valid CRC. Only an inner end-to-end CRC catches it.
- Teensy 4.1 Ethernet defaults to 68 Mbit/s, not 94. If he measures 68 and concludes '100 Mbit Ethernet on a microcontroller is only ~68% efficient', that is a tuning artefact of TCP_SND_BUF = 4*TCP_MSS, not physics.
- Two free-running Teensy crystals drift 12000 ns/s typical (30000 ns/s at 30 ppm worst case). A one-shot sync pulse gives a beautiful sub-100 ns agreement that is 12 microseconds wrong one second later. The sync must be a disciplined loop, not an event.
- The 8-channel 24 MHz logic analyser quantises at 41.67 ns. It cannot verify a 40 ns time-sync claim on a single edge - it will show roughly +/-83 ns of apparent error that is entirely the instrument. Reporting that as measured sync jitter would be a confident wrong number.
- The DP83825I's ~200 ns TX/RX delay asymmetry appears as a constant ~100 ns offset in PTP. It looks like a real, physically plausible cable/propagation delay. It is not - it is a PHY artefact and must be subtracted explicitly.
- Enabling gmac on the Luckfox consumes GPIO1_C5_d and GPIO1_D0_d if he wires link/activity LEDs. On a board with only 17-25 exposed GPIO, check that against whatever else he has planned on those pins before soldering.
- The Luckfox's single USB port is simultaneously the console, the ADB path, the RNDIS network, and the only place the AR9271 WiFi adapter can live. Any USB-based inter-stack scheme takes all of those away at once. There is a known Luckfox issue thread about exactly this (disabling RNDIS to free the port for a USB WiFi adapter).
- No one has published a throughput number for Luckfox Ethernet or Luckfox USB gadget. If a number for either appears in a design doc without a measurement behind it, it was invented.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Prove the Teensy-to-Teensy sync wire: put stack1 Teensy1's ENET_1588_EVENT1_OUT (pin 24) on LA ch0 and stack2 Teensy1's own 1-second output-compare pulse on LA ch1. Capture 60+ seconds. The LA quantises at 41.67 ns, so a correctly disciplined pair should show edge separation within roughly +/-83 ns single-shot. Do NOT report the raw spread as sync jitter - export the edge times, take the mean over N edges, and quote mean +/- standard error (which shrinks as 1/sqrt(N)). To resolve below ~40 ns you need the oscilloscope, not the LA.
- Prove the sync wire's own delay: drive the pulse from stack1 pin 24 and capture it on LA ch0 at the transmitter and ch1 at the receiver end of the same wire. At 41.67 ns per sample you will almost certainly see 0 samples of delay over a bench-length wire - that is the correct answer and it tells you the wire delay is below your ability to measure, so a one-way (rather than two-way) sync exchange is acceptable at bench scale.
- Prove the router adds no silent latency: put the inter-stack link's TX line on LA ch0 and the intra-stack Teensy1<->Luckfox UART TX on ch1. Send a channel 0x05 FORWARD and measure first-byte-out minus last-byte-in at the router. At 921600 baud one byte is 10.85 us; a router that takes more than ~2 byte times has a buffering bug.
- Prove frame integrity end to end: capture both hops of a routed frame on two LA channels, decode as async 921600 8N1, and check that INNER_CRC is byte-identical on both hops while the outer CRC differs. If the inner CRC changes across a hop, the router is mutating the envelope - which is exactly the bug the inner CRC exists to catch.
- Prove the UART is actually clean at the baud you chose: capture the inter-stack UART TX line and look at bit-cell width, not just successful decodes. At 6 Mbaud one bit is 166.7 ns, which is only 4 samples at 24 MS/s - the LA is marginal there. Below 2 Mbaud (500 ns/bit, 12 samples) the LA is trustworthy. Above that, trust the CRC error rate over 10^6 frames instead of the LA's eye.
- Prove ISR headroom at high baud: toggle a spare GPIO high on entry and low on exit of the UART RX ISR, put it on an LA channel alongside the UART line, and measure duty cycle at full line rate. At 6 Mbaud with a 4-byte FIFO you expect an interrupt roughly every 6.7 us; if ISR occupancy exceeds ~30% you will drop bytes as soon as anything else in the system takes a lock.
- Measure ESP-NOW latency the way the published number was measured, so your figure is comparable: GPIO high immediately before esp_now_send() on stack1's S3, GPIO high in the receive callback on stack2's S3, both on LA channels. Expect ~5.6 ms median for a small payload. Your 24 MHz LA is ample here - milliseconds against a 41.67 ns sample period.
- Prove the ESP-NOW chunking behaviour: for a 1024-byte project frame, watch the TX GPIO across all five 250-byte chunks. You should see five distinct bursts and a total near 24 ms. If you see five bursts back-to-back with no gap, the firmware is not waiting for the TX-done callback and Espressif's out-of-order warning applies.
- Measure the real cost of each link with the INA219: put one INA219 in series with stack2's Teensy1 3.3V rail and log current with the inter-stack link idle, then saturated. Do the same on the Luckfox 5V rail with Ethernet idle vs iperf3 running. This is the honest way to answer 'what does the interconnect cost' and it is the only one of these that the LA cannot do. Sample at a fixed cadence and report mean +/- sd over at least 60 s, not a single reading.
- Prove the Luckfox Ethernet link before trusting any throughput number: `ethtool eth0` must report Speed: 100Mb/s, Duplex: Full, Link detected: yes. A link that negotiated 10 Mbit half-duplex will still pass traffic and will make every subsequent measurement wrong by 20x.

### Open questions for the builder

- Read the register and settle the Luckfox PTP question: on a booted Luckfox, what does `ethtool -T eth0` report for hardware-transmit-timestamp / hardware-receive-filter, and what is the actual 32-bit value at the GMAC base + 0x011C (MAC_HW_Feature0)? If bit 12 is 0, the Luckfox can never be the bench time reference and that decision is closed.
- With a meter, what is the actual left-to-right order of the five Ethernet castellated pads on your Pico Mini B, mapped to the schematic nets ETH_T_P, ETH_T_N, ETH_R_P, ETH_R_N and GND?
- Does the RV1103 FEPHY do Auto-MDIX? Concretely: with two Luckfoxes wired to magjacks and joined by a STRAIGHT patch cable, does eth0 link at 100 Mbit full duplex, or do you need a crossover?
- What does `iperf3 -c` actually give between two Luckfoxes over that link, TCP and UDP, and what is `top` showing for si (softirq) on the single A7 while it runs? Nobody has published this number.
- Are you willing to buy two PJRC Ethernet Kits (~magjack + PCB + ribbon each)? The entire recommendation hinges on this - with them, the inter-stack link and the time sync are the same cable and you get 94 Mbit/s and 40.7 ns. Without them you are on a 6 Mbaud UART and a hand-rolled sync wire.
- Is the FLAGS byte in the existing frame fully allocated, or are there spare bits? I am deliberately not touching it, but if there are two spare bits, a 'routed' flag is an alternative to channel 0x05 that I would want to weigh.
- How many bytes of the SEQ space are you currently using, and is SEQ per-link or global? The router's SEQ-rewrite table depends on the answer.
- What is the physical distance between the two stacks (same bench, same rack, different rooms)? Under ~30 cm a raw sync wire and a 6 Mbaud UART are easy; over 1 m both need attention, and Ethernet becomes strictly better.
- Does either ESP32-S3 need to be associated to a WiFi AP at the same time as running ESP-NOW? If yes, both must be locked to that AP's channel and the mesh link's availability is now coupled to your house network.
- Which ESP-IDF version are the S3s on? ESP-NOW v2.0 (1470-byte payload) needs v5.4+ on both ends, and a v1.0 device cannot receive a v2.0 packet larger than 250 bytes.
- On the second stack, is Teensy1 also the sole I2C/SPI master for its own fabric? The routing recommendation assumes the two stacks are structurally identical, as stated.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

None observed. I fetched Rockchip's RV1103 datasheet and RV1106 TRM, the NXP i.MX RT1060 reference manual, TI's DP83825I page, PJRC store/UART/pinout-card documents, Espressif's ESP-IDF and ESP-FAQ ESP-NOW pages, the Luckfox wiki, the Luckfox Pico Mini schematic PDF, a Hannover university PDF of the IEEE ICCE 2024 PTP paper, and several blog/GitHub pages (blog.dan.drown.org, stevep.xyz, electricui.com, themrleon/luckfox-pico-mini-b, IMS-AS-LUH/t41-ptp). No instruction-shaped text, fake system-reminder, claimed authority, or attempt to alter commit attribution or any other behaviour appeared in any of it. Caveat worth stating plainly: several of these were retrieved through a summarising fetch tool rather than as raw text, so an injection embedded in a page could in principle have been dropped during summarisation rather than surfaced to me. The three PDFs I consider most load-bearing (RV1103 datasheet, RV1106 TRM Part 1, i.MX RT1060 RM) I downloaded and extracted to text myself and read directly, so those are not subject to that caveat.

---

## Exact usable 3.3 V logic-fabric IC inventory audit: a job for every 74HC part, threshold arithmetic proving every 74LS part is dead, and a ranked short buy list

### Bottom line

Your usable 74HC inventory is larger than the bulk list suggests but bottlenecked in an unexpected place: the two Minidodoca kits give you only 4 each of 74HC00, 74HC08, 74HC14, 74HC32 and 74HC164, so the glue gates and the Schmitt trigger are your scarcest parts, not the shift registers (24x '595, 24x '138, 19x '165, 15x '373). Every 74LS part in the kits is unusable in a 3.3 V fabric, and the reason is the output side, not the input side: TI specifies 74LS00 VOH min 2.5 V at VCC(min) 4.75 V, a guaranteed 2.25 V drop below rail, so at VCC = 3.3 V the same emitter-follower stage yields roughly 1.05 V worst case — far under the 2.31 V (0.7 x 3.3 V) that both 74HC and the i.MX RT1062 require, and 74LS is not specified below 4.75 V at all. Powered correctly at 5 V, 74LS VOH typ is 3.4 V, which already exceeds the Teensy 4.1's NVCC and drives current into its ESD clamp (abs max Vin = OVDD + 0.3 V = 3.6 V). The one exception is the 74LS47: its outputs are open-collector rated to 15 V off-state and 24 mA on-state, so powered from 5 V it accepts your 3.3 V drive (VIH 2.0 V) and never presents a 5 V level back to the fabric — it is the only 74LS part in the kit with a legitimate job. Two findings will change firmware: the MCP23017's 1.7 MHz I2C mode is specified ONLY for 4.5–5.5 V, so at 3.3 V you are capped at 400 kHz; and the 74HC4051 has no ON-resistance specification at 3.3 V whatsoever, which makes it the wrong part for ADC muxing at your rail.

### Implementation guidance

- 74HC14 debounce, recipe A (good tactile switch, ~1.4 ms total dead time): R1 = 10 kOhm from the 74HC14 input to 3.3 V; R2 = 1 kOhm in series between that node and the switch; switch to GND; C = 100 nF from the node to GND. Release edge (rise) tau = (R1+R2) x C = 11 kOhm x 100 nF = 1.10 ms; time from the 0.30 V low level to worst-case VT+ 2.36 V is tau x ln(3.00/0.94) = 1.28 ms. Press edge (fall) tau = (R1 parallel R2) x C = 909 Ohm x 100 nF = 90.9 us; time from 3.3 V to worst-case VT- 0.61 V is tau x ln(9.68) = 206 us. Peak switch current = 3.3 V / R2 = 3.3 mA.
- 74HC14 debounce, recipe B (cheap tactile or toggle, bounce up to 10-20 ms): same R1 = 10 kOhm and R2 = 1 kOhm, change C to 1 uF. Release edge 12.8 ms, press edge 2.06 ms. Because the press edge is only masked 2 ms, either add a 5 ms firmware lockout after the falling edge, or wire the switch active-high (R1 to GND, switch to 3.3 V) so the slow RC edge lands on the press instead of the release.
- The hard constraint on R2 that people get wrong: the steady low level is VCC x R2/(R1+R2) and it MUST sit below the worst-case VT- of about 0.61 V at 3.3 V. That requires R2/(R1+R2) < 0.185, i.e. R2 < 0.227 x R1. With R1 = 10 kOhm anything above about 2.2 kOhm for R2 leaves the input stuck above VT- and the button never registers a press. R2 = 1 kOhm gives 0.30 V with comfortable margin.
- Keep R1 <= 47 kOhm. The 74HC14 input leakage is specified at +/-1.0 uA over -40..+125 C, so R1 = 10 kOhm costs 10 mV of threshold offset but R1 = 470 kOhm would cost 470 mV, which is more than half your interpolated hysteresis band.
- To clean a slow or ringing clock without inverting it, pass it through TWO 74HC14 stages in series. Do not use this in a delay-matched clock path: a Schmitt's propagation delay is a function of input slew rate, so the two branches of a matched pair will not stay matched if their edge rates differ.
- Every signal that has been through an RC, a long cable, an opto-coupler, a pull-up on a slow bus, or a mechanical contact must enter the fabric through a 74HC14 and never through a 74HC04. The 74HC04's own recommended operating conditions cap the input transition rate at 139 ns/V at 4.5 V; a 1 ms RC edge is roughly 3000x outside that.
- 74HC164 safe use #1 - the good one: pair it with a 74HC373 to build the '595 you would otherwise buy. Shift 8 bits into the '164 with CP (pin 8), wire Q0-Q7 (pins 3,4,5,6,10,11,12,13) to the '373's D0-D7, then pulse the '373's LE (pin 11) HIGH once after the last clock and take the data on LE's falling edge. The '373 supplies the output latch the '164 lacks, and you own 15 of them.
- 74HC164 safe use #2: LED banks where only the settled pattern matters. The 8-clock ripple is visible on a scope but invisible at any refresh rate above ~100 Hz.
- 74HC164 safe use #3: a one-hot scan ring for keypad row scanning, where you clock exactly ONCE per scan step. One clock = one intended state change, so there is no burst of intermediate states.
- 74HC164 forbidden uses, and state this in a comment in the driver: never a chip-select, never a latch-enable, never a reset line, never a bus-direction control, never an interrupt-mask line, never anything driving the E pins of a 74HC138. Shifting 0x01 into a cleared '164 raises Q0 on clock 1, Q1 on clock 2 and so on - up to 8 spurious device selects before the intended one lands.
- Build the write-enable from /CS and /STROBE with a 74HC32 (OR): nWE = nCS OR nSTROBE. The output is LOW only when both inputs are LOW, which is exactly 'this device is selected AND the strobe is asserted'. One 74HC32 package gives you four such gates. If you are working with active-high CS and STROBE instead, a 74HC08 gives WE = CS AND STROBE, and a 74HC00 gives you the active-low version nWE = NAND(CS, STROBE).
- Do NOT gate a clock with a 74HC08 AND gate on the fly. If the enable changes while SCK is high you emit a runt pulse. Two correct alternatives: (a) use the 74HC165's CE pin, whose function table shows CE and CP are interchangeable so you get a designed-in gated clock for free; (b) change the enable only while the clock is in its inactive state, and prove it on the logic analyser.
- 74HC138 break-before-make sequence, in this exact order: (1) drive E1 (pin 4) or E2 (pin 5) HIGH to deselect all outputs globally; (2) change A0/A1/A2 (pins 1,2,3); (3) wait at least the '138 propagation delay from its dynamic-characteristics table; (4) drive the E pin LOW to select. Never change A0-A2 while the part is enabled.
- 74HC138 cascade for 16 or 64 selects: drive one '138's E3 (pin 6, active HIGH) from address bit 3 and another '138's E2 (pin 5, active LOW) from the same bit to get 16 selects from 4 lines plus a global enable. For 64, use one '138 to drive the E2 pins of eight more.
- 74HC373 parallel write bus - this is the highest-value use of your 15 '373s: put a byte on 8 Teensy pins, decode a 3-bit destination address with a 74HC138, invert the selected active-LOW Yn through a 74HC04 to make an active-HIGH LE pulse, and strobe it. 12 Teensy pins (8 data + 3 address + 1 strobe) then drive 8 destinations x 8 bits = 64 latched outputs, updated in one bus cycle instead of a 64-clock serial shift.
- 74HC373 LE is level-sensitive, not edge-triggered. Q follows D the whole time LE is HIGH. Set up the data first, pulse LE HIGH for at least the datasheet tW, then take the latched value on LE's falling edge. Tie OE (pin 1) LOW if you never three-state it - never leave it floating.
- MCP23017 init sequence at 3.3 V: set the I2C clock to 400 kHz maximum (1.7 MHz is out of spec below 4.5 V). Write 0x44 to IOCON at register address 0x0A on a factory-fresh part - that is BANK=0, MIRROR=1, SEQOP=0 (auto-increment on), DISSLW=0, HAEN=0, ODR=1 (open-drain INT), INTPOL=0. With ODR=1 the INTPOL bit is ignored.
- MCP23017 ISR must read BOTH ports, every time, without exception. With MIRROR=1 an interrupt-on-change on either port pulls the shared open-drain line low. If the ISR reads only GPIOA/INTCAPA, PORTB's condition stays latched, the open-drain line stays asserted, and because it is level-not-edge you will never get another interrupt. Read GPIOA then GPIOB (or INTCAPA then INTCAPB) as one 2-byte auto-incrementing read with SEQOP=0.
- MCP23017 bus budget: a 2-byte GPIOA+GPIOB read is roughly 45 bit-times, roughly 113 us at 400 kHz. Eight expanders polled round-robin gives about 1.1 kHz per chip. Size your input-latency requirements against that number and use the interrupt path for anything faster.
- Split your 12 MCP23017s across the Teensy 4.1's three I2C buses - 8 on Wire (SDA 18 / SCL 19) and 4 on Wire1 (SDA 17 / SCL 16). Only 8 addresses (0x20-0x27) exist per bus, so all 12 on one bus is impossible. Apply your 2.2 kOhm pull-up-once-at-the-master rule independently to each bus.
- 74HC595 reset gotcha: pulsing MR (pin 10) LOW clears the shift register but leaves the storage register and therefore the output pins unchanged. To actually blank the outputs: pull MR LOW, release it HIGH, then pulse STCP (pin 12). Tie MR HIGH and OE (pin 13) LOW in hardware if you do not drive them.
- 74HC595 LED current budget: the binding limit is IGND = -70 mA per package, not the +/-35 mA per pin. Eight LEDs on one '595 share 70 mA absolute maximum. Design for 5 mA each with 220-330 Ohm at 3.3 V, and do not assume the 4 mA VOL figure applies - it is specified at 4.5 V only.
- 74HC165 scan sequence: hold PL (pin 1) LOW for at least tW to load the parallel inputs asynchronously, release PL HIGH, then clock CP (pin 2) eight times reading Q7 (pin 9). Never clock while PL is LOW. Cascade by wiring Q7 of chip N to DS (pin 10) of chip N+1 with PL and CP common. Tie CE (pin 15) LOW if you do not use the gated-clock trick.
- Cap every shift-register clock in the fabric at 10 MHz. None of the '164, '165 or '595 datasheets have a 3.3 V fmax column; the guaranteed minimum at 2.0 V is 6-9 MHz and at 4.5 V is 30 MHz, so 10 MHz at 3.3 V has margin without relying on a number that does not exist.
- Do NOT use the 74HC4051 to mux anything into an ADC at 3.3 V and absolutely not at the Luckfox's 1.8 V. Its RON has no 3.3 V specification and the datasheet warns it goes 'extremely non-linear' near 2.0 V. Use the 74HC4051 you own for digital routing instead - fanning one logic-analyser probe to 8 test points, or steering one debug UART to one of 8 headers - which is exactly what the datasheet note recommends for low supply voltages.
- Whenever you use a 74HC4051, tie VEE (pin 7) to GND for single-supply 3.3 V operation and drive or tie E (pin 6); E HIGH turns all switches off. Its break-before-make is described by Nexperia as 'typical', not guaranteed, so never rely on it to prevent two sources shorting together.
- 74LS47 wiring, if you use it: power the '47 from 5 V, drive A/B/C/D (pins 7,1,2,6) directly from a 3.3 V 74HC595 or MCP23017 output - 3.3 V clears the 2.0 V VIH with 1.3 V of margin and the input sinks only 0.4 mA. Use a common-ANODE display with its anodes to the display supply (up to 15 V) and a series resistor per segment sized for <= 24 mA. The open-collector outputs never source, so no 5 V level ever returns into the 3.3 V fabric. Tie LT (pin 3) and RBI (pin 5) HIGH if unused.
- Tie-off checklist for every part on the board, since a floating CMOS input is your stated hard rule: 74HC595 MR HIGH and OE LOW; 74HC165 CE LOW and DS to GND on the first chip in a chain; 74HC138 all three E pins driven or tied (E1/E2 LOW to enable, E3 HIGH); 74HC373 OE LOW and LE driven; 74HC164 MR HIGH and both DSA and DSB driven (they are ANDed - tying DSB HIGH and driving DSA is the normal arrangement); 74HC4051 E driven and VEE to GND; every unused gate input on a '00/'04/'08/'14/'32 tied to VCC or GND, not left open.

### Pitfalls

- The 74LS trap is asymmetric and that is why it fools people: driving INTO a 5 V-powered 74LS input works fine from 3.3 V (2.0 V VIH is cleared with 1.3 V margin), so a one-way test passes on the bench. The failure is on the return path, where a 74LS output high of 3.4 V typ exceeds the Teensy's 3.3 V NVCC, and at 3.3 V supply the LS output high collapses to roughly 1-2 V. If you test only one direction you will conclude 74LS works.
- The MCP23017 will happily ACK at 1.7 MHz on a 3.3 V bench with short wires and then corrupt reads on a longer harness or a cold morning. The 1.7 MHz mode is specified only for 4.5-5.5 V, and the 1.7 MHz bus-capacitance limit also drops from 400 pF to 100 pF. Set 400 kHz in firmware and never raise it while at 3.3 V.
- The 74HC164 chip-select failure is a burst of 8 short selects during the shift, each one clock period wide. At 1 MHz SPI they are 1 us and obvious; at 10 MHz they are 100 ns, i.e. 2.4 samples on your 24 MHz analyser, and you may miss them entirely and blame the slave device. Characterise at 1 MHz.
- 74HC595 MR does not clear the outputs. Firmware that resets a display chain with MR alone leaves the last pattern lit and you will spend an hour looking for a stuck SPI line.
- 74HC373 LE is transparent while HIGH. If you leave LE high and then change the data bus for a different destination, every '373 with LE high captures the new byte. Pulse it, do not level it.
- MAX7219 and TPIC6B595 both look like the obvious 3.3 V shift-register upgrades and both are out of spec: VIH 3.5 V and 0.85 x VCC = 3.825 V respectively. They will work at room temperature on a fresh board and drop bits later. Every internet tutorial that drives a MAX7219 from a 3.3 V MCU is operating it out of spec.
- The 74HC4051 at 1.8 V (for the Luckfox SARADC) is in the region the datasheet explicitly refuses to specify and calls 'extremely non-linear'. A muxed ADC reading there will be repeatable, plausible, and wrong by a channel-dependent offset - the worst possible failure mode.
- Only 8 I2C addresses exist for the MCP23017. If you wire all 12 onto one bus, four of them silently collide with four others and you get plausible-looking data from the wrong chip. There is no error, just wrong numbers.
- Web summaries of datasheet PDFs are actively dangerous for pinouts. In this research pass, AI summarisers returned a 74HC164 pinout with CLK on pin 3 and CLR on pin 4 (both wrong; they are pins 8 and 9) and a 74HC373 with VCC on pin 10 and GND on pin 20 (reversed). I only caught these by extracting the pin-description tables from the PDFs directly. Verify every pinout against the manufacturer's own pin-description table before etching.
- The 74HC00 and 74HC14 pinouts flip ordering between the top and bottom halves of the package. On the '00, pin 3 is an output (1Y) but pin 8 is also an output (3Y) while pin 9 is an input (3A). On the '14, pin 2 is an output but pin 8 is an output and pin 9 is an input. Half the package reads left-to-right and half reads right-to-left.
- A debounce network whose series resistor R2 is too large relative to the pull-up R1 never crosses VT- and the button simply never registers. This produces an intermittent, temperature-dependent, per-unit-varying button that looks like a bad switch. Check R2 < 0.227 x R1 at 3.3 V.
- Gating a clock through a 74HC08 whose enable changes asynchronously produces runt pulses that a 24 MHz analyser may render as a clean edge or may miss entirely, while the downstream flip-flop sees them fine. Never debug a gated clock by analyser alone at 24 MHz - reason about the enable timing.
- Do not put an inverter (74HC04) in a chip-select path to fix polarity. It adds delay on one edge but not symmetrically, which quietly breaks the break-before-make guarantee you rely on for the '138. Fix polarity at the decoder's enable pins instead.
- 74HCT ordering mistake: a single wrong character in a part number buys you a part specified only from 4.5 V. It will function marginally at 3.3 V (74HCT VIH is a fixed 2.0 V so it appears to work) and its VOH will be out of spec. Filter every order for the 'HCT' string.
- 74LS47 is a 5 V island, which means it needs a 5 V rail on a board you have otherwise kept entirely at 3.3 V. That rail plus a >3.3 V display supply is a real complexity cost for four chips. Weigh it against just driving a common-cathode display from a 74HC595 you already own.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Prove the 74HC14 hysteresis exists at your actual 3.3 V rail: put a slow triangle or a 1 Hz RC ramp on the input, probe input and output. Because your analyser is digital-only it will show you the OUTPUT transition times; sweep the ramp and record the input voltage (on a DMM or scope) at which the output flips going up versus going down. The difference is VH at 3.3 V. Expect roughly 0.83 V typical; anything below 0.30 V means you have a 74HC04 in the socket by mistake.
- Prove the 74HC04-on-a-slow-edge failure so you never doubt the rule: feed the SAME RC-debounced signal into a 74HC04 and a 74HC14 on the same board. Capture both outputs on the analyser at 24 MHz during one button press. The '04 output will show a burst of transitions (multiple edges within a few microseconds of the crossing); the '14 output will show exactly one. Save that capture as your reference.
- Prove the 74HC164 chip-select glitch: wire a '164, clock SPI at 1 MHz so each clock period is 1 us = 24 analyser samples, capture Q0 through Q7 on channels 0-7 while shifting 0x80 into a cleared register. You will see Q0 through Q6 each pulse HIGH for exactly one clock period before the bit reaches Q7. That is 7 spurious selects. Then repeat with the '164+'373 pairing and confirm the '373 outputs change exactly once, on the LE falling edge.
- Prove break-before-make on the 74HC138: probe A0, A1, A2, E2 and two adjacent Yn outputs on 6 channels. During an address change, confirm E2 goes HIGH before any address bit moves and returns LOW only after all three have settled, and that no two Yn are ever LOW simultaneously. At 24 MHz (41.7 ns resolution) a violation shorter than ~80 ns will be invisible, so deliberately stretch the sequence with software delays during characterisation.
- Prove the 74HC595 MR gotcha: probe SHCP, STCP, MR, DS and two Qn outputs. Load a nonzero pattern, then pulse MR LOW and back HIGH WITHOUT pulsing STCP. The Qn lines must not move. Then pulse STCP and watch them all go LOW. If they moved on MR alone you have a different part in the socket.
- Measure the actual MCP23017 transaction time at 400 kHz: probe SDA, SCL and the shared open-drain INT line on 3 channels. Trigger on INT falling. Measure INT-fall to INT-rise for one full 2-byte GPIOA+GPIOB read. Expect roughly 113 us of bus time plus your ISR latency. If INT never rises, your ISR is reading only one port - that is the MIRROR bug, and the analyser shows it as a permanently-low INT line.
- Confirm the MCP23017 open-drain wire-OR: with several expanders' INT pins tied together, trigger an interrupt-on-change on chip 3 only and confirm the shared line is pulled fully to GND (the analyser will show a clean LOW) and that no other chip is fighting it. If ODR were 0 the INT pins would be push-pull and two chips disagreeing would show as an intermediate level that the analyser renders as noisy or as a stuck line.
- Verify the 74HC165 CE/CP interchangeability claim before you rely on it: probe PL, CP, CE and Q7. Hold CP LOW and clock CE; confirm data shifts out on Q7. Then hold CE LOW and clock CP; confirm identical output. If only one works you have a part whose datasheet differs from Nexperia's.
- Bound your shift-register clock empirically: run a known walking-1 pattern through the longest '595 chain at 1, 2, 5, 10 and 15 MHz and compare the received pattern. The first frequency at which bits shift or duplicate is your real fmax including wiring capacitance, which will be well below the datasheet's 30 MHz-at-4.5V figure. Stay at half of whatever you measure.
- Use an INA219 in series with the VCC of one 74HC595 driving a fully-lit 8-LED bank to confirm you are under the 70 mA IGND absolute maximum. Measure with all 8 outputs LOW (sinking) and all 8 HIGH (sourcing) - the sink case is the one that runs against IGND.
- Use an INA219 on the 3.3 V rail feeding the whole logic fabric to catch shoot-through: a 74HC gate fed a slow edge draws a current spike every transition. Compare rail current with the offending input debounced (74HC14) versus undebounced (74HC04). A measurable standing-current increase on the '04 version is direct evidence of the dt/dV violation.
- If you do use the 74LS47, confirm with a DMM (not the analyser) that no segment output ever rises above the display supply and that the BCD input pins driven by your 74HC595 sit at 3.3 V HIGH and below 0.4 V LOW under the 0.4 mA IIL load. Also confirm with a scope that the '47's 5 V rail is not coupling into the 3.3 V rail through a shared ground return.

### Open questions for the builder

- Is the 2.4 inch display on Teensy1 an ST7789 SPI TFT (per your BOM) or something else? You have called it a '2.4 inch OLED' elsewhere. ST7789 and SSD1309/SH1106 have completely different init sequences and the ST7789 needs a reset pin the OLED modules often omit. Read the controller marking on the flex or the module silkscreen and tell me which it is.
- Do you actually need bidirectional parallel data between Teensy1 and anything, or is one-way write-plus-serial-readback enough? If one-way is enough you need zero '245s and can build the whole bus from the 74HC373s and 74HC165s you already own. If you need bidirectional, you must buy 74LVC245A because your only '245 is the unusable LS part.
- What is the worst-case bounce duration of the specific switches you are using? Recipe A masks 1.28 ms on release and 206 us on press; recipe B masks 12.8 ms and 2.06 ms. Cheap 6 mm tactiles are usually under 1 ms but panel toggles and microswitches can bounce 20 ms. Scope one switch before committing R and C.
- Are you planning to run any 7-segment display at all, or was the 74LS47 question purely an inventory-disposal question? If you have no 7-segment display in the build, the answer is simply 'bag the four LS47s' and no 5 V rail is needed anywhere on the logic board.
- What is the maximum I2C bus length and how many connectors are in the path between Teensy1 and the furthest MCP23017? Your <400 pF budget at 400 kHz is generous, but ribbon cable runs about 50-70 pF/m per conductor and each connector adds a few pF. I need the physical topology to tell you whether 2.2 kOhm is right or whether you need 1.5 kOhm.
- Do the second-stack Teensys need the same IO fabric as stack 1? If yes, you are 4 short on 74HC14, 74HC00, 74HC08, 74HC32 and 74HC164 before you start, because those exist only in the kits at 4 pieces each total across both kits.
- What total current do you need behind the 74HC595s? If it is LEDs at 5 mA each you need nothing new. If it is relays, solenoids, or 7-segment digit commons above about 20 mA, you need TPL7407LA or discrete logic-level FETs, and I need the number to tell you which.
- Are you muxing anything into the Luckfox SARADC, or is all your analog going into the Teensy 4.1's 12-bit ADC? The answer changes the analog mux recommendation completely - at 1.8 V neither the 74HC4051 nor the 74LV4051 is a good answer and you want an ADS1115 on I2C instead.
- Have you confirmed the 74HC08 and 74HC32 pinouts against their own Nexperia pin-description tables? I verified the '00 directly but inferred the '08 and '32 from family convention. Before a PCB, read those two tables yourself - it is a two-minute check that prevents a board respin.
- What is your actual required input-scan rate? The 400 kHz MCP23017 ceiling gives about 1.1 kHz per chip with 8 chips round-robin. If you need faster than that, the MCP23S17 swap moves from 'nice' to 'mandatory' and I should re-cost the whole IO architecture around SPI.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

No prompt-injection or instruction-shaped text was found in any fetched content this pass. Every source I read was a manufacturer PDF (Nexperia, TI, onsemi, Microchip, NXP, Analog Devices) plus one PJRC documentation page, and none contained embedded directives, fake system-reminders, or attempts to alter commit attribution or any other instruction. I specifically watched for a repeat of the fake system-reminder you warned about and saw nothing. Two things worth flagging that are NOT injections but are data-integrity problems: (1) the WebFetch PDF summariser fabricated incorrect pinouts on at least two occasions - it returned a 74HC164 with CLK on pin 3 and CLR on pin 4 (actual: CP pin 8, MR pin 9) and a 74HC373 with VCC on pin 10 and GND on pin 20 (actual: GND pin 10, VCC pin 20), and it also returned approximate fmax figures prefixed with '~' that did not match the datasheet tables. I discarded all of those and re-extracted the raw text from the downloaded PDFs with pypdf, so every pinout and number reported above comes from the manufacturer's own pin-description or characteristics table. (2) One Microchip URL redirected through microchip.com/filehandler/redirect which then returned HTTP 403; I obtained the MCP23017 datasheet from the direct ww1.microchip.com/downloads/en/DeviceDoc/20001952C.pdf path instead, which is Microchip's own host. The TI SN74LS47 datasheet (SDLS111, 1988) is a scanned image with no text layer and could not be read; I used the onsemi SN74LS47 datasheet (Rev. 7, July 2006) as the primary source for that part instead.

---

## Luckfox Pico Mini B (Rockchip RV1103) as Linux orchestrator: UART3_M1 enable, 921600 baud integrity, IO voltage domains, SARADC, GPIO, boot service, AR9271 USB WiFi

### Bottom line

UART3_M1 on header pins 12/13 is the correct choice and needs no DTS rebuild: `luckfox-config` -> Advanced Options -> UART -> UART3_M1 -> enable applies a live configfs device-tree overlay that sets `&uart3 { pinctrl-0 = <&uart3m1_xfer>; status = "okay"; }`, giving /dev/ttyS3 (DT alias `serial3 = &uart3`). The console is untouched: it is the Rockchip FIQ debugger bound to UART2 (`rockchip,serial-id = <2>`, 115200), exposed as /dev/ttyFIQ0 on header pins 4/5, and it is a different node and a different iomux register from GPIO1_D0/D1. 921600 8N1 is exact, not approximate: the Rockchip fork of 8250_dw sets the UART source clock to baud*16 = 14,745,600 Hz for any baud above 230400, and the RV1106/RV1103 fractional divider reaches that exactly from GPLL 1,188,000,000 Hz with m/n = 256/20625 (both 16-bit), so the 16550 divisor is exactly 1 and the baud error is 0.000%. Datasheet ceiling is 4 Mbps with 64-byte TX and RX FIFOs. The UART3_M1 pads sit in the VCCIO6 domain, which the Luckfox Pico Mini schematic strap-selects to VCC_3V3 through R20 (0R, populated) with the VCC_1V8 option R21 marked DNP - so the pads are 3.3 V and wire directly to a Teensy 4.1, but MEASURE header pin 12 idle before wiring, because the same silicon bank is 1.8V-capable. Two things will bite: the AR9271 needs a kernel rebuild (the stock Luckfox defconfig has no CFG80211/MAC80211/ATH9K_HTC at all) plus a USB dr_mode change that kills the RNDIS 172.32.0.93 link, and the stock Buildroot rootfs has no systemd - it is BusyBox init with /etc/init.d/SXX scripts.

S99usb0config only brings up usb0 at 172.32.0.93 when dr_mode reads "peripheral"; otherwise it prints "usb0 is using host mode". The SPI NAND dtb lives at /dev/mtdblock3. | certain | LuckfoxTECH/luckfox-pico: luckfox-config functions luckfox_usb_app (lines 2018-2040), luckfox_USB (lines 3325-3350), luckfox_config_init media detecti |
| RV1103 USB capability. | One USB 2.0 Host/Device port. High-speed 480 Mbps, full-speed 12 Mbps, low-speed 1.5 Mbps. EHCI 1.0 and OHCI 1.0a. | certain | Rockchip RV1103 Datasheet Rev 1.5, section 1.2 'USB 2.0' bullets |
| RV1103 CPU maximum operating point in the shipping SDK is 1104 MHz, not the widely quoted 1.2 GHz. The datasheet Rev 1.5 states no CPU frequency at all. | rv1103.dtsi deletes opp-1200000000, opp-1296000000, opp-1416000000, opp-1512000000 and opp-1608000000 from cpu0_opp_table, leaving 408 / 600 / 816 / 1104 MHz. rv1103.dtsi sets ARMCLK assigned-clock-rate = <1104000000>. GPLL 1188 MHz, CPLL 1000 MHz. | certain | LuckfoxTECH/luckfox-pico sysdrv/source/kernel/arch/arm/boot/dts/rv1103.dtsi and rv1106.dtsi cpu0_opp_table |
| The 16 ms latency problem is an FTDI USB-serial artifact and does not exist on a native memory-mapped UART. Additionally, the kernel's ASYNC_LOW_LATENCY / setserial low_latency RX path was removed years ago and is a no-op on this 5.10 kernel. | The string "low_latency" does not appear anywhere in drivers/tty/tty_buffer.c in this kernel (645 lines, zero matches). pyserial's set_low_latency_mode() on Linux only does TIOCGSERIAL/TIOCSSERIAL setting bit 0x2000 (ASYNC_LOW_LATENCY) in serial_struct.flags - which ftdi_sio translates to latency_timer=1, and which 8250_dw ignores. | certain | LuckfoxTECH/luckfox-pico sysdrv/source/kernel/drivers/tty/tty_buffer.c (grep low_latency -> no matches); pyserial serial/serialposix.py lines 143-159 |

This matters because TIOCEXCL is bypassed by root, and everything on the Luckfox image runs as root. | serialposix.py _reconfigure_port(): `fcntl.flock(self.fd, fcntl.LOCK_EX \| fcntl.LOCK_NB)` raising SerialException on failure. Kernel tty_io.c line 1352: `if (test_bit(TTY_EXCLUSIVE, &tty->flags) && !capable(CAP_SYS_ADMIN)) return -EBUSY;` - i.e. TIOCEXCL does not stop root. | certain | pyserial serial/serialposix.py lines 392-401; LuckfoxTECH/luckfox-pico sysdrv/source/kernel/drivers/tty/tty_io.c lines 1352, 2665-2675 |
