### Implementation guidance

- ENABLE UART3 (fast path, no rebuild, survives reboot via /etc/luckfox.cfg): on the board run `luckfox-config`, then Advanced Options -> UART -> UART3_M1 -> enable. Exit. Verify immediately with: `ls -l /dev/ttyS3`, `cat /etc/luckfox.cfg | grep UART3`, `ls /sys/kernel/config/device-tree/overlays/`, and `dmesg | grep ff4d0000` (expect 'ff4d0000.serial: ttyS3 at MMIO 0xff4d0000 (irq = NN, base_baud = 1500000) is a 16550A' or similar). base_baud will read as uartclk/16 at the time of probe.
- ENABLE UART3 (permanent path, if you rebuild the SDK image): edit sysdrv/source/kernel/arch/arm/boot/dts/rv1103g-luckfox-pico-mini.dts and change the existing block from `/* UART3_M1 */ &uart3 { status = "disabled"; };` to `status = "okay";`. Nothing else is needed - rv1103-luckfox-pico-ipc.dtsi already sets `&uart3 { pinctrl-0 = <&uart3m1_xfer>; }`. Do NOT touch &uart2 or &fiq_debugger.
- DO NOT try to enable UART2 as a ttyS. UART2 is claimed by the Rockchip FIQ debugger (rockchip,serial-id = <2>) and appears as /dev/ttyFIQ0 at 115200 8N1 on header pins 4/5. The luckfox-config UART menu on the Mini only ever offers UART3_M1 and UART4_M1, so there is no way to break the console from that menu.
- WIRING: Luckfox H2 pin 12 (GPIO1_D0, UART3_TX_M1) -> Teensy1 RX. Luckfox H2 pin 13 (GPIO1_D1, UART3_RX_M1) -> Teensy1 TX. Ground: Luckfox H2 pin 2 or pin 21 -> Teensy GND. No level shifter - both ends are 3.3 V push-pull. Copy Luckfox's own console practice and put a 100 ohm series resistor in each direction (they use R6/R7 = 100R on UART2_TX/UART2_RX); it costs ~0.1 V of Voh at CMOS input currents and protects both SoCs against a powered-vs-unpowered sequencing event and against a mux mistake shorting two outputs.
- MEASURE THE PAD VOLTAGE BEFORE CONNECTING THE TEENSY. Procedure: (1) power the Luckfox alone, nothing on pins 12/13. (2) DMM DC on H2 pin 3 (VCC_3V3) referenced to pin 2 -> expect 3.30 V +/-5%; on H2 pin 22 (1V8_OUT) -> expect ~1.80 V (note this pin has a 10 ohm series resistor, so measure unloaded). (3) BEFORE enabling UART3, measure H2 pin 12 and pin 13 -> expect near 0 V, because the pads default to GPIO with internal pull-DOWN (datasheet pin-name suffix GPIO1_D0_d / GPIO1_D1_d, Rpd 16-43 kohm). (4) Run luckfox-config and enable UART3_M1. (5) Re-measure pin 12 and pin 13 -> both should now sit at the VCCIO6 rail, i.e. ~3.3 V, because uart3m1_xfer applies &pcfg_pull_up to both pins and idle UART TX is high. If either pin reads ~1.8 V, STOP: VCCIO6 has been strapped to VCC_1V8 (R21 fitted instead of R20) on your particular board and you need a TXB0108 in that link. (6) Optional confirmation with the logic analyser: with the LA's 3.3 V threshold, `echo -n U > /dev/ttyS3` and check the low level is <0.4 V and the high level is >2.4 V.
- OPEN THE PORT FROM PYTHON (low-latency, native UART): use `import serial, fcntl, os`; `ser = serial.Serial('/dev/ttyS3', baudrate=921600, bytesize=8, parity='N', stopbits=1, timeout=0.02, write_timeout=0.5, inter_byte_timeout=None, exclusive=True, rtscts=False, dsrdtr=False, xonxoff=False)`. exclusive=True takes an flock(LOCK_EX|LOCK_NB) - it is honoured even between root processes, unlike TIOCEXCL. Do NOT call ser.set_low_latency_mode(True): on this kernel ASYNC_LOW_LATENCY has no RX effect (the tty_buffer low_latency path was removed) and it can raise on some drivers. Do NOT set inter_byte_timeout below 0.1 s - it maps to VTIME in deciseconds and truncates to 0.
- READ LOOP that avoids syscall thrash: block once, then drain. `n = ser.read(1)` (blocks up to `timeout`); if n: `n += ser.read(ser.in_waiting or 0)`; feed the bytes into your existing byte-wise frame state machine (START 0xAA, LEN_H, LEN_L, SEQ, FLAGS, CHAN, CMD, payload, CRC_H, CRC_L). Do NOT call ser.read(expected_frame_len) - pyserial implements that as a select()+os.read() loop that will issue many syscalls per frame on a single 1.1 GHz A7 core.
- WRITE: always `ser.write(frame)` in ONE call for the whole frame, then do not call ser.flush() in the hot path - flush() is tcdrain(), which blocks until the FIFO and shift register empty (adds up to a full frame time). Only tcdrain before closing or before asserting a hardware handshake. Set write_timeout so a wedged link raises instead of hanging the orchestrator.
- STARTUP HYGIENE on the port: after opening, `ser.reset_input_buffer()` and `ser.reset_output_buffer()` once, then send a resync/ping on channel 0x00. Because the RX pad has an internal pull-up, an unplugged link idles high (no framing errors), and a plugged-but-unpowered peer idles high too - so absence of bytes is your only link-down signal. Use a transport-layer keepalive on channel 0x00.
- BOOT SERVICE, stock Buildroot image (BusyBox init - there is NO systemd): create /etc/init.d/S98orchestrator, chmod 755. Body: `#!/bin/sh` / `case "$1" in start) start-stop-daemon -S -b -m -p /var/run/orch.pid -x /usr/bin/python3 -- /root/orchestrator.py ;; stop) start-stop-daemon -K -p /var/run/orch.pid ;; restart) $0 stop; sleep 1; $0 start ;; esac`. Use S98 so it runs BEFORE S99luckfoxconfigload only if you do NOT depend on luckfox-config; if you DO depend on UART3 being enabled by luckfox-config load, name it S99zorchestrator so it sorts after S99luckfoxconfigload. Do NOT put the script at /root/main.py - the shipped /etc/init.d/S99python runs `python /root/main.py` in the foreground with no `&` and will block the rest of init forever.
- BOOT SERVICE, if you build a systemd rootfs instead: /etc/systemd/system/orchestrator.service with `[Unit] After=multi-user.target` ; `[Service] Type=simple ExecStart=/usr/bin/python3 -u /root/orchestrator.py Restart=always RestartSec=2 Nice=-5 IOSchedulingClass=best-effort` ; `[Install] WantedBy=multi-user.target`. But confirm systemd actually exists first (`ls /lib/systemd/systemd`) - it does not on the shipping image.
- RECLAIM RAM: the board config sets RK_BOOTARGS_CMA_SIZE="24M" out of 64 MiB total for the camera ISP. If you are not using the MIPI camera, rebuild with RK_BOOTARGS_CMA_SIZE="4M" in BoardConfig-SPI_NAND-Buildroot-RV1103_Luckfox_Pico_Mini-IPC.mk and disable the rkisp/rkcif/csi nodes. Verify the win with `free -m` and `cat /proc/meminfo | grep Cma` before and after.
- GPIO from Python without libgpiod tools: python-periphery is preinstalled. Character-device path (preferred): `from periphery import GPIO; g = GPIO('/dev/gpiochip1', 24, 'out')` - note the cdev line number is the offset WITHIN the bank (GPIO1_D0 = 24), not the global sysfs number. Legacy sysfs path: `g = GPIO(56, 'out')` which uses the global number pin = bank*32 + group*8 + X (GPIO1_D0 = 1*32 + 3*8 + 0 = 56). Shell/sysfs: `echo 56 > /sys/class/gpio/export; echo out > /sys/class/gpio/gpio56/direction`. Do not export a pin that is currently muxed to UART3 - it will be refused or will steal the pin.
- SARADC: use channel 1 ONLY. `cat /sys/bus/iio/devices/iio:device0/in_voltage1_raw` (10-bit, 0-1023). Volts = raw * 1.8 / 1024 = raw * 1.7578 mV. Channel 0 (in_voltage0_raw, header pin 19) is the RECOVERY strap and must stay pulled up - reading it is harmless, loading it is not. Never apply more than 1.8 V to header pin 19 or 20; that bank (VCCIO2) is 1.8V-only and is supplied from USB_AVDD1V8.
- AR9271 - the full sequence, in order: (1) Rebuild the kernel with CONFIG_CFG80211=m, CONFIG_MAC80211=m, CONFIG_ATH9K_HW=m, CONFIG_ATH9K_COMMON=m, CONFIG_ATH9K_HTC=m, CONFIG_FW_LOADER=y, and CONFIG_USB_XHCI_HCD / CONFIG_USB_EHCI_HCD (already y). None of the 802.11 symbols exist in the stock defconfig. (2) Add BR2_PACKAGE_LIBNL, BR2_PACKAGE_WIRELESS_TOOLS and BR2_PACKAGE_IW (and BR2_PACKAGE_WPA_SUPPLICANT if you want managed mode) to the Buildroot defconfig - the stock image has no nl80211 userspace. (3) Copy the firmware to /lib/firmware/ath9k_htc/htc_9271-1.4.0.fw (from linux-firmware). The driver requests that exact path first and only falls back to the deprecated flat /lib/firmware/htc_9271.fw (v1.3). (4) `luckfox-config` -> Advanced Options -> USB -> host, then reboot. This is a static dtb patch to /dev/mtdblock3 and it PERMANENTLY disables the RNDIS 172.32.0.93 link until you switch back. (5) Power the dongle externally. (6) Verify: `lsusb` should show 0cf3:9271 (genuine Atheros ID), `dmesg | grep ath9k`, then `iw dev`, `iw phy0 info | grep -A6 'Supported interface modes'` (expect 'monitor' in the list).
- BEFORE flipping USB to host mode, make sure you have console access on UART2. Wire a CH340 USB-TTL: CH340 RX <- Luckfox H2 pin 4 (UART2_TX), CH340 TX -> Luckfox H2 pin 5 (UART2_RX), GND <-> H2 pin 2. 115200 8N1. This is your only recovery path once RNDIS is gone.
- MONITOR MODE / INJECTION on ath9k_htc: `ip link set wlan0 down; iw dev wlan0 set type monitor; ip link set wlan0 up; iw dev wlan0 set channel 6`. Or create a dedicated monitor vif: `iw phy phy0 interface add mon0 type monitor`. Injection is supported by ath9k_htc on genuine AR9271 silicon. Counterfeit AR9271 dongles are common and frequently lack working injection registers even though they enumerate and do monitor mode - check `lsusb -v` for the real Atheros descriptors and test with a known injection test rather than assuming.

### Pitfalls

- The luckfox-config UART enable is a RUNTIME configfs overlay, not a persistent dtb change. It survives reboot only because /etc/luckfox.cfg records UART3_M1_STATUS=1 and /etc/init.d/S99luckfoxconfigload re-runs `luckfox-config load` at every boot. Reflash the rootfs, wipe /etc, or break that init script and /dev/ttyS3 silently vanishes - and the orchestrator will fail with 'No such file or directory' at a moment when nothing looks wrong electrically.
- Race at boot: S99luckfoxconfigload is what creates /dev/ttyS3, and it runs at S99. If you name your orchestrator script S50 or drop it at /root/main.py (S99python sorts before S99luckfoxconfigload alphabetically: 'luckfoxconfigload' < 'python'), the port will not exist yet. Sort your script after it, or retry-open with backoff.
- The shipped /etc/init.d/S99python runs `python /root/main.py` WITHOUT backgrounding it. Putting the orchestrator there blocks init permanently - no sshd restart, no later services, and the board will look 'booted' on the console while half the system never started.
- Package pin numbers and sysfs GPIO numbers are different and they nearly collide for exactly these two pins. GPIO1_D0 is SoC package pin 57 but sysfs number 56; GPIO1_D1 is package pin 56 but sysfs number 57. At least one web source has these swapped. Always derive from the formula pin = bank*32 + group*8 + X.
- The RV1103 header pin numbering is DIP-style, not Raspberry-Pi-style odd/even. Pins 1-11 run down the LEFT from the USB-C end; pins 12-22 run UP the RIGHT, so pin 12 is bottom-right and pin 22 is top-right. Counting the right column downward gives you SARADC where you expected UART3.
- VCCIO6 is 1.8V/3.3V-selectable SILICON that happens to be strapped to 3.3 V by a 0 ohm resistor (R20) on this board revision, with the 1.8 V option (R21) marked DNP. A different board revision, a reworked board, or a future Luckfox variant could ship the other strap and would then be destroyed by a 3.3 V Teensy driving into it. Measure, do not assume.
- Worst-case level margin from Luckfox TX into Teensy RX is only 90 mV: RV1103 Voh min 2.4 V versus i.MX RT1062 Vih min 2.31 V (0.7 x 3.3). It will work in practice, but if you ever see marginal, temperature-dependent, or one-way-only framing errors, that is the number to suspect rather than the baud rate.
- Sequencing hazard: if the Teensy is powered and driving 3.3 V into GPIO1_D1 while the Luckfox is unpowered, current flows through the pad ESD diode into a dead VCCIO6 rail. This can latch or slowly damage the SoC and it looks like nothing until the board stops booting. Luckfox themselves put 100R in series on their own console lines (R6/R7) - copy that.
- Everything on the Luckfox Buildroot image runs as root, and the TIOCEXCL ioctl is explicitly bypassed by CAP_SYS_ADMIN in tty_io.c. pyserial's exclusive=True happens to use flock instead, which IS honoured between root processes - but a bare `cat /dev/ttyS3`, minicom, screen, or a stray getty takes no lock at all and will silently eat your frames. Check `fuser -v /dev/ttyS3` when bytes go missing.
- Switching USB to host mode for the AR9271 kills the RNDIS management link (172.32.0.93). S99usb0config explicitly refuses to configure usb0 unless dr_mode reads 'peripheral'. If you have not already got a working UART2 console on pins 4/5, you will have locked yourself out of a board with no Ethernet, no SD slot, and 128 MB of soldered flash.
- There is exactly ONE USB port on the RV1103. Host mode plus AR9271 means no USB gadget networking at the same time. Plan for the console (pins 4/5) to be the permanent management path.
- The USB-C port has 5.1 kohm Rd on both CC lines and no VBUS source switch, so the board never sources 5 V. A plain passive USB-C-to-A OTG adapter will enumerate nothing because the dongle gets no power. You need a powered hub or an external 5 V feed, and the AR9271's several-hundred-milliamp draw is a real load on whatever supplies the board.
- 24 MiB of the 64 MiB of RAM is reserved for CMA by the stock board config, before the kernel, Python interpreter, and your orchestrator get anything. A Python service that looks fine on a desktop can OOM here. Measure with `free -m` and `cat /proc/meminfo | grep -i cma` on the actual board, not from the 64 MB headline number.
- `setserial /dev/ttyS3 low_latency` and pyserial's set_low_latency_mode(True) are cargo cult on this platform. The kernel's low_latency RX path was removed - the string does not appear in tty_buffer.c at all in 5.10. The 16 ms figure people quote is the FTDI ftdi_sio latency_timer default, an entirely different mechanism, and it does not apply to a memory-mapped 8250. Chasing it will waste a day.
- If you sniff the link with a CH340 USB-TTL rather than the logic analyser, remember the CH340 has its own USB bulk-transfer polling granularity (~1 ms) that will smear your timing measurements. Timing claims must be made on the 24 MHz logic analyser, not through a USB serial bridge.
- pyserial's inter_byte_timeout maps to termios VTIME in DECISECONDS. Any value below 0.1 s silently truncates to 0, which disables the inter-byte timeout entirely and changes VMIN to 1 - quietly turning your non-blocking design into a blocking one.
- The stock rootfs has no libgpiod tools (gpiodetect/gpioset are absent from the Buildroot defconfig) even though /dev/gpiochipN exists in the kernel. Scripts copied from a Raspberry Pi or Rockchip SBC tutorial that shell out to gpioset will fail with 'not found', not with a permissions error, which sends people down the wrong debugging path.
- The datasheet's 4 Mbps ceiling is for the UART block. The kernel's Rockchip termios path special-cases 230400 and 1152000 to baud*32 and everything else above 115200 to baud*16, then silently accepts up to +/-2% baud error if the fractional divider misses. 921600 lands exactly, but an arbitrary odd baud might quietly run 2% off with no error message - check /sys/kernel/debug/clk/sclk_uart3/clk_rate if you ever change the rate.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Prove the 921600 clock is exact, on the board and on the wire. On the board: `mount -t debugfs none /sys/kernel/debug 2>/dev/null; python3 -c "import serial;s=serial.Serial('/dev/ttyS3',921600)" & sleep 1; cat /sys/kernel/debug/clk/sclk_uart3/clk_rate` - expect exactly 14745600. Then `grep -E 'uart3|xin24m|gpll' /sys/kernel/debug/clk/clk_summary` to confirm clk_uart3 is parented on clk_uart3_frac (not xin24m) and gpll reads 1188000000. On the LA: capture pin 12 at 24 MHz while sending 0x55 repeatedly (`python3 -c "import serial,time;s=serial.Serial('/dev/ttyS3',921600);[s.write(b'U') or time.sleep(0.001) for _ in range(2000)]"`). 0x55 gives an alternating bit pattern. Measure the width of a single bit cell: expect 1.0851 us +/- LA sample quantisation (the 24 MHz LA has 41.67 ns resolution, so 26 samples per bit). Measure the total width of the 10-bit character (start + 8 data + stop) across many characters: expect 10.851 us. If the frame period drifts by more than one sample across a 1024-byte burst, the divider is NOT exact and something in the clock tree changed.
- Prove the divisor is 1 and not a rounded value. Capture at 921600 and also at 115200 on the same pin, and compare measured bit periods to 1.0851 us and 8.6806 us. At 115200 the Rockchip driver forces the source clock to 24 MHz and the divisor becomes 24000000/(16*115200) = 13.02 -> 13, giving an actual baud of 115384.6 (+0.16% error, harmless but measurable). Seeing that ~0.16% offset at 115200 and ZERO offset at 921600 is a strong positive confirmation that the driver's rate-selection branches are behaving exactly as read from 8250_dw.c.
- Prove the pad voltage domain with the LA plus a DMM, before the Teensy is ever connected. DMM DC: H2 pin 3 to pin 2 = 3.30 V; H2 pin 22 to pin 2 = 1.80 V; H2 pin 12 to pin 2 BEFORE enabling UART3 = near 0 V (internal pull-down); H2 pin 12 AFTER enabling UART3 = ~3.3 V (internal pull-up + idle-high TX). On the LA set the threshold to 3.3 V logic and confirm the mark level is recognised as high and the space level as low; then set the threshold to 1.8 V logic and confirm the same signal is still clean - if the pad were actually on a 1.8 V rail, the 3.3 V threshold capture would show all-low or missing edges.
- Prove the internal pull-up value. With UART3 enabled and TX idle high, hang a 10 kohm resistor from H2 pin 12 to GND and measure the pin voltage. With Rpu typ 25 kohm the divider gives 3.3 * 10/(10+25) = 0.94 V; at Rpu min 16 kohm you get 1.27 V, at max 43 kohm you get 0.62 V. Any reading inside 0.6-1.3 V confirms the internal pull-up is active and in the datasheet's 16-43 kohm window. (Remove the resistor before connecting the Teensy.)
- Prove the console is undisturbed by the UART3 change. Put LA channel 0 on H2 pin 4 (UART2_TX) and channel 1 on H2 pin 12 (UART3_TX), capture across the moment you run luckfox-config and enable UART3. The UART2 line must show no glitch, no level change and no mux transient; only channel 1 should transition from low (pull-down) to high (pull-up). Simultaneously keep a CH340 console session open at 115200 on ttyFIQ0 and confirm it never drops a character.
- Prove exclusivity and detect bus thieves. With the orchestrator running and holding the flock, run `fuser -v /dev/ttyS3` (expect exactly one PID) and try `python3 -c "import serial; serial.Serial('/dev/ttyS3',921600,exclusive=True)"` in a second shell - it must raise SerialException. Then deliberately run `cat /dev/ttyS3 &` and watch the LA: the wire traffic is unchanged but your orchestrator starts losing bytes. That is the failure signature to recognise, because it looks exactly like an electrical fault.
- Measure real round-trip latency rather than assuming it. Have the Teensy toggle a spare GPIO high the instant it finishes transmitting the last byte of a request frame, and have the Luckfox orchestrator toggle nothing - instead put LA channels on pin 13 (Luckfox RX) and pin 12 (Luckfox TX) and measure the gap from the stop bit of the last received byte to the start bit of the first transmitted response byte. Expect a floor of roughly the 16550 RX-timeout (4 character times = 43.4 us at 921600) plus Python/scheduler wakeup, typically single-digit milliseconds on a loaded 1.1 GHz A7. Compare that number against your protocol's timeout budget on channel 0x00. If it is worse than ~10 ms, look at CPU contention and CMA-induced memory pressure, not at the UART.
- Measure the AR9271 power draw before trusting any OTG wiring. Put an INA219 in series with the 5 V feed to the dongle (shunt on the high side, 0.1 ohm default). Log current during enumeration, during firmware download (htc_9271-1.4.0.fw is pushed over USB and causes a distinct current step), during idle monitor mode, and during injection bursts. Separately put a second INA219 in series with H2 pin 1 to see the Luckfox's own draw. If the two together exceed what your 5 V source can deliver, the symptom will be random USB resets and 'ath9k_htc: Target is unresponsive' in dmesg, which reads like a driver bug and is not one.
- Verify the SARADC scale with a known divider rather than trusting the maths. Feed H2 pin 20 (SARADC_IN1) from a 1.8 V reference through a 2:1 resistive divider (two 10 kohm, giving 0.90 V) and read in_voltage1_raw - expect 512 +/- a few counts (0.90 / 1.7578 mV = 512). Sweep with a pot and check linearity at 0.45 V (raw ~256) and 1.35 V (raw ~768). ENOB is only 9 bits, so expect roughly +/-2 counts of noise; average 16 samples if you need better.

### Open questions for the builder

- Does your specific Luckfox Pico Mini B board actually have R20 (0 ohm to VCC_3V3) fitted and R21 (to VCC_1V8) empty at the VCCIO6_VCC strap? The published schematic says yes and marks R21 DNP, but this is the one thing that would destroy a Teensy pin. What does header pin 12 measure to GND with UART3 enabled and nothing else connected - 3.3 V or 1.8 V?
- Does header H2 pin 1 (VBUS) have DC continuity to the USB-C VBUS pin on your board? The schematic shows them sharing the net name with no visible switch, which would mean you can power the board on pin 1 and have that same 5 V flow out to a dongle through an OTG adapter. Please confirm with a DMM continuity check before designing the AR9271 power scheme around it.
- Which image is currently flashed - the Buildroot SPI NAND image (85 MB ubifs rootfs, BusyBox init) or something else? `cat /etc/os-release; cat /proc/version; ls /lib/systemd/systemd 2>/dev/null; cat /proc/mtd` will settle it in one shot and determines whether the boot service is an S9x script or a .service file.
- Is the rootfs partition mounted read-write or read-only on your image? `mount | grep ' / '` - this decides whether the orchestrator can write logs/state to / or must use /userdata (6 MB) or /oem (30 MB).
- Do you actually need the MIPI camera on the Luckfox? If not, dropping RK_BOOTARGS_CMA_SIZE from 24M to 4M recovers roughly 20 MB of 64 MB and is probably the highest-leverage change available - but it requires an SDK rebuild and reflash, which costs you the current working image.
- Is the AR9271 dongle a genuine Atheros part (VID:PID 0cf3:9271 with real Atheros descriptors) or one of the many counterfeits? Counterfeits enumerate and do monitor mode but often cannot inject. `lsusb -v` output for the device would let this be settled before you spend a kernel rebuild on it.
- The 2.4 inch display on Teensy1 is still unresolved in the BOM (SPI TFT ST7789 vs 'OLED'). Note that the Luckfox SDK's own rv1103-luckfox-pico-ipc.dtsi contains an fbtft node for a sitronix,st7789v on SPI0 with led-gpios GPIO0_A4, dc-gpios GPIO1_A2, reset-gpios GPIO1_C3 - if that display is in fact an ST7789 you may have a second, Linux-side home for it. What is the exact controller marking on that module?
- Do you intend UART3 to be the only Luckfox<->Teensy link, or do you also want UART4_M1 (header pins 10/11, GPIO1_C4 RX / GPIO1_C5 TX, /dev/ttyS4, also in the 3.3 V VCCIO6 domain) as a second independent channel? Both are free and both are 3.3 V, so a second link costs nothing but two wires.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

None. I read the Rockchip RV1103 Datasheet Rev 1.5 PDF, the official Luckfox Pico Mini schematic PDF, the NXP i.MX RT1060 datasheet Rev 4, the LuckfoxTECH/luckfox-pico SDK sources (device trees, luckfox-config, init scripts, board configs, kernel defconfig, 8250_dw.c, clk-rv1106.c, clk.c, tty_buffer.c, tty_io.c, gpio/Kconfig), the luckfox-eng33/luckfox_pico_buildroot defconfig, torvalds/linux v5.10 ath9k sources, the pyserial source, and pages on wiki.luckfox.com, pyserial.readthedocs.io, python-periphery.readthedocs.io and wireless.docs.kernel.org. No instruction-shaped text, no fake system-reminder blocks, no attempts to alter commit attribution or any other agent behaviour appeared in any fetched content. All fetched material was treated as data.

---

## Bench One: what a 5-node heterogeneous UART-linked firmware needs that people forget — reset lifecycle, bus ownership, backpressure, head-of-line blocking, watchdogs, hot-plug, POST, versioning, error propagation, observability; plus a verdict on OpenAMP RPMsg / Arm HIPC / CANopen prior art

### Bottom line

The existing link layer is already better than most of what this review would normally have to recommend: one-outstanding-per-link, stale-response drop, event-gap detection, rate-limited-but-counted NACKs, three-axis versioning in HELLO, and a timeout that is not evaluated while bytes are still buffered. So the useful findings are narrower and sharper than the brief anticipated. The single load-bearing defect is in shared/bench_link.cpp:84 — the RX drain is an unbounded `while (mPort->available() > 0)` loop, and at 921600 a peer that streams continuously can hold poll() forever, which starves the other two links, stalls the fabric, prevents the watchdog being fed, and (because the request timeout sits behind `available() == 0`) wedges the pending-request slot permanently. That is the head-of-line-blocking item and the backpressure item and the livelock, all in one construct. Second: reboot detection rests entirely on a one-shot unsolicited HELLO on a link with no ACK and no retry, so a lost HELLO means the master believes stale state indefinitely — fixable for free by treating a backwards jump in the uptime_ms field already present in WRK_STATUS_RESP as a reboot, plus a CANopen-style periodic heartbeat instead of a boot-only announcement. Third, and electrically real: an MCP23017 that glitches or is hot-plugged reverts to IODIR=0xFF and IOCON=0x00, which silently drops MIRROR and ODR — turning its INT pin into a push-pull driver fighting the other open-drain INTs on the shared wired-OR IRQ net, while Table 3-6 shows that reading only one port when both have pending interrupt-on-change leaves INT asserted forever and masks every other device on that line. Of the prior art, CANopen CiA 301's heartbeat/boot-up state machine is the only thing worth stealing wholesale; RPMsg's vrings and HIPC's 16 MB shared-SRAM/MHUv3 stack assume shared memory that does not exist across a UART and are pure overhead here.

### Implementation guidance

- BOUND THE DRAIN LOOP (do this first — it is a live livelock). Replace `while (mPort->available() > 0)` in BenchLink::poll() with a byte-capped loop: `uint32_t drained = 0; while (mPort->available() > 0 && drained < BENCH_DRAIN_MAX) { ...; drained++; }`. Set BENCH_DRAIN_MAX = 512. Sizing rule: to sustain the wire's 92,160 bytes/s you need poll_rate_hz >= 92160 / BENCH_DRAIN_MAX, so 512 bytes/poll requires poll() at >= 180 Hz (5.56 ms period) per link; 256 bytes/poll requires 360 Hz (2.78 ms). Record `if (drained > mStats.max_drain) mStats.max_drain = drained;` — if max_drain ever equals BENCH_DRAIN_MAX you are at the cliff edge.
- SPLIT THE TWO TIMEOUTS — they are different clocks and conflating them is the design error behind the wedge. The PARSER's inter-byte frame-assembly timeout should stay suppressed while bytes remain buffered (the builder's reasoning at bench_link.cpp:95-97 is right, keep it). The REQUEST timeout at line 110 must be moved OUT from behind the `available() == 0` guard and evaluated unconditionally on every poll(). A request deadline is wall-clock; whether this node happens to have unparsed bytes in its ring is irrelevant to whether the peer answered.
- RE-SAMPLE millis() INSIDE the drain loop, or at minimum after it, before using `now` for mLastRxMs and for the timeout comparison. Currently `now` is sampled once at bench_link.cpp:77; a 512-byte drain is 5.6 ms of wire time plus dispatch, so frame timestamps and the liveness window drift by that much.
- REBOOT DETECTION FOR FREE, NO WIRE-FORMAT CHANGE: WRK_STATUS_RESP (0x79) already carries uptime_ms32. Have the master cache last_uptime_ms per node and treat `new_uptime < last_uptime` as a definitive reboot — re-anchor event sequence, re-run capability exchange, invalidate all cached node state. This costs one uint32 per node and catches every reboot including the ones whose HELLO was lost. Do this before adding any new field.
- ADD A PERIODIC HEARTBEAT, not just a boot-time HELLO. Steal CANopen's shape: an unsolicited one-byte state event every 500 ms carrying {0=Booting, 5=Operational, 127=Degraded, 4=Stopped}. The existing event-sequence gap detection (mLastEventSeq) then makes a missing heartbeat visible automatically, and isUp()'s 5000 ms liveness window gets something to be driven by other than incidental traffic. Master declares a node down after 3 missed heartbeats (1.5 s).
- ADD BOOT EPOCH TO HELLO as the belt-and-braces version: one uint8 incremented and persisted each boot. Teensy: use the SNVS_LPGPR registers (VBAT-backed, survive warm reset) rather than emulated EEPROM, which costs a flash write per boot. ESP32: NVS. This is the only mechanism that survives a node that reboots faster than the master's poll interval, where uptime-backwards can alias.
- ENFORCE SINGLE BUS MASTER AT COMPILE TIME, not in a comment. Define BENCH_ROLE_FABRIC_MASTER only in teensy1_master/, and put `#ifndef BENCH_ROLE_FABRIC_MASTER\n#error "bench_fabric.cpp is master-only; Teensy2/ESP32 must request IO over UART"\n#endif` at the top of bench_fabric.cpp. A build failure is the only enforcement that cannot be forgotten at 2am. Note that teensy2_worker/ already correctly omits bench_fabric.cpp — keep it that way and make it impossible to reverse by accident.
- ENFORCE IT ELECTRICALLY TOO: pull the 74HC138 global-enable pin to its DESELECT state with a resistor, so that while Teensy1 is in reset (i.MX RT1062 GPIOs are high-Z at reset) no fabric device is selected. Without the pull, a Teensy1 reset leaves the decoder's enable floating — which violates the project's own no-floating-CMOS-inputs rule and can select a random device.
- MCP23017 SHADOW VERIFICATION (this is the hot-plug detector and it needs no extra hardware): every 100 ms, read back IOCON and IODIRA/IODIRB from one device per cycle (round-robin across the 12) and compare against the master's shadow. Expected IOCON = MIRROR(bit6)|ODR(bit2) = 0x44. A device reading back IOCON=0x00 and IODIR=0xFF has reset itself — re-initialise it and emit a fabric fault event upward naming the I2C address and the observed-vs-expected bytes.
- MCP23017 ISR MUST READ BOTH PORTS, ALWAYS — read GPIOA and GPIOB (or INTCAPA and INTCAPB) unconditionally, never conditionally on INTF. Per datasheet Table 3-6, when both ports have pending interrupt-on-change, reading only one clears neither. Prefer INTCAP if you need the captured-at-interrupt value, GPIO if you need current state; do not mix.
- ADD A STUCK-IRQ DETECTOR: after servicing all pending MCP interrupts, if the shared IRQ pin is STILL asserted and no device reported INTF bits, that is the wedged-line condition. Force a read of GPIOA+GPIOB on every device in address order, count the event, and report it upward. Without this check a stuck line is permanently and silently deaf — there is no error, just an absence of interrupts forever.
- WATCHDOG SETUP PER NODE: Teensy1 and Teensy2 — WDOG1 at 2.0 s (WCR.WT = (2.0-0.5)/0.5 = 3), serviced once per loop() with 0x5555 then 0xAAAA to WDOGx_WSR. Use WDT_T4's pre-reset callback to stash {pending chan, cmd, seq, subsystem} into SNVS_LPGPR0..3 with a magic word, so the post-reset HELLO can say what was in flight. ESP32-S3 and WROOM-32E — set CONFIG_ESP_TASK_WDT_PANIC=y (it defaults to n and will otherwise only print a warning), keep TWDT at 5 s, subscribe the link-service task with esp_task_wdt_add(). Luckfox — open /dev/watchdog, WDIOC_SETTIMEOUT to 10 s, WDIOC_KEEPALIVE from the supervisor loop, read WDIOC_GETBOOTSTATUS at startup and put WDIOF_CARDRESET into the Luckfox's own HELLO.
- MAKE POST MEAN SOMETHING — an I2C address ACK is not a pass. An MCP23017 with every pin dead still ACKs. The meaningful test is a loopback with a golden value: write a walking-1 pattern (8 or 16 vectors) out through the 74HC595 chain, read it back through the 74HC165 chain, and compare against a stored golden vector array. PASS = every vector matched. Distinguish three outcomes, not two: ABSENT (address NACK), PRESENT-BUT-WRONG (ACK, readback mismatch — this is the wiring fault), and PASS. They demand different actions.
- GOLDEN-VALUE REGRESSION: store a CRC-16/MCRF4XX over the concatenated readback vectors as a single uint16 golden constant per fabric revision, alongside BENCH_FABRIC_HW_REV. A one-wire change alters the CRC. Report the computed value in FABRIC_INFO_RESP so a capture can be matched to the hardware that produced it. Reuse the existing CRC implementation — do not add a second checksum algorithm to the codebase.
- VERSION COMPATIBILITY POLICY (the version bytes already exist in HELLO; what is missing is what to DO). Encode as three rules: (1) IOP_PROTOCOL_VERSION differs → refuse the link entirely, log, do not send further frames — the frame format itself is in question. (2) BENCH_PROTOCOL_VERSION major differs → link up but REFUSE to drive the fabric, and report DEGRADED upward; a half-upgraded pair issuing fabric writes can cause contention. (3) Minor differs → interoperate, restricted to min(local_minor, peer_minor) command set. Add a 4th field: a 4-byte firmware build id (git short hash or __DATE__/__TIME__ hash) so two builds of the same protocol version are distinguishable when chasing a regression.
- KILL THE COPY-PASTE HEADER PROBLEM NOW, before it reaches bench_protocol.h. Either (a) point every sketch folder at ..\shared via the build's include path, or (b) add tools\sync_headers.ps1 that copies shared/* into the four node folders and fails loudly on md5 mismatch, and run it as a pre-build step. Right now esp32_radio/ and hmi_e32r40t/ carry a stale bench_link.cpp; it is harmless only by luck.
- STRUCTURED FAULT RECORD for error propagation to the Linux box. ORC_EVENT (0x85) currently forwards [src_node][chan][cmd][len16][payload]. Define one fabric-fault payload shape and use it everywhere: [subsystem][device_addr][op][expected8][observed8][boot_epoch][uptime_ms32][last_seq]. A bare error code tells the Luckfox that something failed; this tells it which MCP23017 at which address disagreed with what, during which operation, in which incarnation of that node.
- LATCH THE FIRST FAULT. Keep a single 'first fault since boot' record per node, written once and never overwritten until reset, alongside the rolling counters. The first fault is almost always the causal one; a firehose of consequences will otherwise bury it. Report it in SYS_RESP. This pairs with the existing NACK rate-limiter philosophy — suppress the flood, keep the count, but also keep the original.
- COUNTERS TO ADD to BenchLinkStats (the existing 11 are good; these are the ones whose absence hides a fault): resync_bytes (bytes discarded while hunting for START 0xAA — the single best early warning of a baud/ground/level-shifter problem, since a link can run at 99% success while slowly degrading), crc_errors kept SEPARATE from framing/length errors (different physical causes: CRC = bit corruption, length = desync), max_drain_bytes (high-water mark from the bounded loop above), max_poll_interval_us (high-water mark of time between poll() calls — this is how you know how close you are to the 694 us / 88.9 ms cliff before you go over it), and heartbeat_missed. High-water marks matter more than totals here: a total tells you it happened, a high-water mark tells you how much margin is left.
- RAISE THE RX BUFFERS BEFORE ANYTHING ELSE. Teensy1 and Teensy2: pass a static 8192-byte buffer to BenchLink::begin() for every link (88.9 ms headroom vs 694 us on the 64-byte default). ESP32-S3 and WROOM-32E: call Serial.setRxBufferSize(4096) BEFORE begin() (44.4 ms vs 2.78 ms on the 256-byte default) — the HMI node needs this most, since an ST7796 refresh will blow through 2.78 ms trivially.
- IF OVERFLOW STILL SHOWS UP IN THE HIGH-WATER MARKS, use hardware flow control rather than inventing a software scheme: Teensy 4 exposes attachRts(pin)/attachCts(pin) with configurable rts_low_watermark/rts_high_watermark. Cost is 2 wires per link. This is the correct escalation and it removes the overflow class entirely; do not build a software credit scheme first.

### Pitfalls

- THE WEDGE (silent, permanent, and in the code right now): a peer that transmits continuously — a babbling node, a wrong baud rate producing a dense byte stream, or a flooding Luckfox — holds BenchLink::poll() in its unbounded drain loop forever. Consequences compound: the other two links are never polled, the fabric is never serviced, any watchdog is never fed, and because the request timeout sits behind the `available() == 0` guard it is never evaluated, so the one-outstanding slot never clears. The link is dead, and the timeouts counter reads zero — the very counter you would check. It looks like the peer went quiet when in fact it will not shut up.
- THE STUCK IRQ LINE (silent and permanent): with MIRROR=1 and both ports holding a pending interrupt-on-change, reading only one port clears neither (datasheet Table 3-6). The device's open-drain INT stays low, and since every MCP23017's INT is wired-OR'ed to one Teensy IRQ pin, all twelve devices are now masked. There is no error, no counter, no NACK — just an absence of interrupts from then on. Level-triggered means you do not even get a missed-edge symptom to notice.
- THE PUSH-PULL CONTENTION AFTER A GLITCH: an MCP23017 that browns out or is hot-plugged returns to IOCON = 0x00, which means ODR = 0 — its INT pin becomes an ACTIVE PUSH-PULL DRIVER on a net where the other devices are open-drain. One device driving high while another pulls low is a real current path, not a logic inconvenience. And IODIR = 0xFF means all its outputs simultaneously went high-Z, so whatever it was driving floats. The device answers I2C perfectly throughout; nothing in a naive poll notices.
- ESP32 TASK WATCHDOG DOES NOT RESET BY DEFAULT. CONFIG_ESP_TASK_WDT_PANIC defaults to n — the TWDT prints a warning and a backtrace, then carries on. A hung radio or HMI task therefore stays hung forever while the console shows a reassuring watchdog message. If you assume the TWDT is your recovery mechanism, you have no recovery mechanism.
- WATCHDOG RESET MAY ERASE ITS OWN EVIDENCE ON THE TEENSYS. A WDOG1 reset on i.MX RT is reported to behave as a full power-on reset, after which the reset reason is not readable. The node then boots and honestly reports POWERON. You will chase a power-supply problem that does not exist. Stash the reason in SNVS_LPGPR before the reset instead of trusting SRC_SRSR.
- THE CRC RESIDUE SHORTCUT IS WRONG FOR THIS FRAME ORDER. CRC-16/MCRF4XX is a reflected algorithm (refout=true) with residue 0x0000 — but only when the CRC is appended low-byte-first. This frame sends CRC_H then CRC_L. Running a whole frame including its CRC through the algorithm yields 0xF8E9, not 0x0000. Anyone writing the logic-analyser decoder with the textbook 'expect zero' check will report 100% CRC failure on a perfectly healthy link and send you hunting for a grounding fault.
- UPTIME-BACKWARDS ALIASES IF THE NODE REBOOTS FASTER THAN YOU POLL. If a node crash-loops with a period shorter than the master's status poll interval, successive uptime readings can both be small and increasing, and the reboot is invisible. This is exactly the crash-loop case you most need to see. The boot epoch is the fix; uptime-backwards alone is a 95% solution, not a 100% one.
- HELLO IS A ONE-SHOT ON A LINK WITH NO ACK AND NO RETRY. If the master is itself still booting, or mid-drain, or the frame is corrupted, the announcement is gone permanently and the master carries stale state — stale capability bits, a stale event-sequence anchor, and a stale belief that the node is configured. A periodic heartbeat is not a nicety here; a boot-only announcement on an unreliable unidirectional path is a design that fails silently by construction.
- A POST THAT ONLY PROBES I2C ADDRESSES IS THEATRE. Every MCP23017 will ACK its address with every GPIO pin open-circuit, shorted, or driving into a fault. A green PASS from an address scan is actively harmful — it converts 'we have not tested this' into 'we have tested this'. Only a loopback against a golden vector distinguishes present-and-working from merely present.
- THE HEADER COPIES DIVERGE SILENTLY AND THE SYMPTOM LOOKS ELECTRICAL. It has already happened once (esp32_radio/ and hmi_e32r40t/ carry a stale bench_link.cpp). This time it is dead code inside an __IMXRT1062__ guard. When it happens to bench_protocol.h — a command renumbered in three folders out of five — the symptom is a stream of CRC failures and NACKs that is indistinguishable from a wiring or level-shifter fault, and you will take the analyser to a problem that lives in the filesystem.
- BACKPRESSURE FROM THE LUCKFOX HAS NOWHERE TO GO. request() correctly returns BUSY rather than queueing, but ORC_FWD_REQ crosses a link boundary: the Luckfox asks Teensy1 to relay to a node whose link may already have a request in flight. If that case is not explicitly rejected with a distinct BUSY status, the natural implementation either drops it silently or blocks the hub. Both are worse than saying no. The Linux side must implement its own one-outstanding rule — a rule enforced on only one end of a link is not enforced.
- HEAD-OF-LINE BLOCKING IS IN THE CPU, NOT THE WIRE. The links are point-to-point, so a slow fabric operation cannot physically delay radio bytes — they keep landing in the ring. What it delays is the PARSER, and what that corrupts is latency measurement: a slow fabric transaction landing inside a radio round-trip gets recorded as radio latency. The result is a plausible wrong number rather than an error, which is the worst kind. Timestamp at the point of byte arrival if latency figures are going to be quoted.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- THE WEDGE: on the 8-channel analyser, capture TX of the Luckfox link and RX at Teensy1 while a script on the Luckfox sends back-to-back max-size frames with no gap. Simultaneously toggle a spare GPIO at the top of poll() for each link. PASS = the other two links' poll markers keep toggling throughout. FAIL (current code) = the markers for the radio and worker links stop entirely for the duration of the flood. This is a one-channel, unambiguous, before/after test of the bounded-drain fix.
- BYTE TIME SANITY: capture any link at 24 MS/s and measure one bit width. Expect 1.0851 us (26 samples at 24 MHz) and a 10-bit character at 10.8507 us. If the measured bit time differs by more than ~2%, the baud divisor is wrong on that node and every CRC conclusion downstream is suspect. Do this before trusting any other measurement.
- RX RING HEADROOM: toggle a GPIO high on entry to poll() and low on exit. Measure the maximum LOW interval (time between polls) with the display refreshing and the fabric busy. Compare against 694 us (64-byte default buffer) and 88.889 ms (8 KB buffer). If the max interval exceeds the buffer's time-equivalent, you are dropping bytes. Capture this before and after calling addMemoryForRead().
- MCP23017 STUCK IRQ: put the shared INT net on one analyser channel and SCL/SDA on two more. Force simultaneous interrupt-on-change on both PORTA and PORTB of one device (drive two inputs on different ports at once), then have the ISR deliberately read only GPIOA. PASS of the diagnosis = INT stays LOW indefinitely after the read, and no further I2C interrupt-service traffic appears for any device. Then apply the both-ports fix and confirm INT releases within one service transaction.
- MCP23017 SILENT RESET: with the analyser on SDA/SCL, briefly interrupt the 3.3 V rail to one MCP23017 (or pulse its RESET pin low). PASS of the shadow-verification mechanism = within 100 ms the master issues a read of IOCON, observes 0x00 against an expected 0x44, re-initialises the device, and emits a fault frame on the Luckfox link. Capture that frame on a separate channel to prove the fault propagated all the way up rather than being handled locally and forgotten.
- INA219 FOR THE CONTENTION CASE: with an MCP23017 forced into its post-reset IOCON=0x00 state (ODR=0, push-pull INT) while another device on the same net asserts its open-drain INT, put an INA219 on the 3.3 V feed of the reset device. Expect a measurable current step versus the correctly-configured baseline — this distinguishes 'lost configuration' (a logic problem) from 'driver fight' (a current problem) with a number rather than an argument.
- CRC DECODER VALIDATION: before trusting any analyser CRC column, feed the decoder a known-good frame and confirm it computes 0x6F91 for the byte string '123456789'. Then confirm that running a full frame INCLUDING its CRC_H|CRC_L yields 0xF8E9 and not 0x0000 — if your decoder expects 0x0000 it is misconfigured for this frame's byte order and will report false failures.
- REBOOT DETECTION END TO END: capture the Teensy1<->worker link, then reset Teensy2 with a wire while the master is mid-poll. PASS = the master emits a state-invalidation and re-anchor within one heartbeat period (500 ms), whether or not the worker's HELLO frame appears in the capture. Deliberately corrupt the HELLO (reset the worker during a heavy flood so the frame is lost) and confirm the uptime-backwards check still catches it on the next status poll.
- HEARTBEAT LIVENESS: capture any link idle. Expect an unsolicited state frame every 500 ms +/- scheduler jitter. Unplug the link and confirm the master declares the node down after 3 missed heartbeats (1.5 s) rather than waiting for the 5000 ms isUp() window.
- WATCHDOG RECOVERY TIMING: toggle a GPIO in loop() on each Teensy, then force a hang. Measure the interval from last toggle to the first post-reset toggle. Expect ~2.0 s of WDOG1 timeout plus boot time. Confirm on the same capture that the post-reset HELLO on the UART carries a reset_reason that is NOT power-on — if it says POWERON, the SNVS_LPGPR stash is required.

### Open questions for the builder

- Does a WDOG1 timeout on your actual Teensy 4.1 preserve a readable reset cause, or does it present as a power-on reset? This determines whether reset reporting needs the SNVS_LPGPR stash or can read SRC_SRSR directly. Test: arm WDOG1 at 2 s, deliberately hang, and print SRC_SRSR plus SNVS_LPGPR0 on the next boot.
- What are the exact SRC_SRSR bit assignments for the i.MX RT1062 specifically? I could only find i.MX 6-family values and will not assume they carry over. The RT1060 reference manual is at pjrc.com/teensy/IMXRT1060RM_rev3.pdf — which bit is WDOG, which is WDOG3/RTWDOG, which is warm boot?
- What is the RV1103's UART FIFO depth, and does the Luckfox Pico Mini B expose RTS/CTS on any accessible pin? Neither the TRM section nor the Luckfox wiki confirmed this. It decides whether hardware flow control is even an option on the Luckfox-to-Teensy1 link.
- Has 921600 actually been measured error-free on the Luckfox UART3/UART4, or only assumed from the 4 Mbaud ceiling? Rockchip's own note says the achievable rate depends on the clock division strategy — the divisor for 921600 from a 24 MHz reference may carry more error than the ESP32/Teensy links do.
- Is the 2.4-inch display on Teensy1 an ST7789 SPI TFT (per the BOM) or something else? This is still listed as unresolved. It matters here for one specific reason: whether its refresh shares SPI with the fabric, and therefore whether a display update is a fabric-blocking operation or an independent one.
- How many MCP23017s are actually on the shared IRQ net in stack 1 — all 12, or a subset? The stuck-IRQ blast radius and the round-robin shadow-verification period both scale with that number.
- What is the measured worst-case loop() period on Teensy1 with the fabric, the display and three links all active? Every buffer-sizing conclusion above is a function of this one number, and it is the single most useful thing to put on the logic analyser first.
- Do you intend the Luckfox to be able to reset the other nodes in hardware (a GPIO to Teensy RESET / ESP32 EN)? Without it, a wedged node needs a human. With it, the recovery story closes — but it also introduces the reset-storm failure mode and needs a backoff.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

None. No instruction-shaped text, fake system-reminders, or attribution-changing directives appeared in any fetched page or PDF during this pass. Sources fetched were: docs.espressif.com (ESP-IDF misc_system_api, wdts), raw.githubusercontent.com (PaulStoffregen/cores HardwareSerial.h and HardwareSerial1.cpp, espressif/arduino-esp32 HardwareSerial.cpp, espressif/esp-idf esp_system/Kconfig, OpenAMP/open-amp rpmsg.h, tonton81/WDT_T4 Watchdog_t4.tpp), kernel.org (watchdog-api.txt), openamp.readthedocs.io, arm-auto-solutions.docs.arm.com, wiki.luckfox.com, and the Microchip MCP23017 datasheet PDF DS20001952C (ww1.microchip.com), which was downloaded and parsed locally with PyMuPDF. One note on handling rather than injection: the WebFetch of the Microchip PDF returned only a refusal-to-parse from the summarising model, so the binary was saved to disk and the register tables extracted directly — the register values quoted above come from the datasheet text itself, not from a summary of it. Two fetched pages (pjrc.com/teensy/td_libs_Watchdog.html and one Luckfox wiki URL) returned HTTP 404 and contributed nothing.

---

## Discrete-logic CPU and "light Linux" feasibility, assessed against Bench One's exact 74HC / MCP23017 inventory

### Bottom line

Linux cannot be built from ~150 assorted 74HC packages, and the reason is arithmetic, not opinion. The entire usable 3.3 V inventory (136 logic ICs, 12 MCP23017) contains 688 bits of flip-flop/latch state — 86 bytes — while the architectural register file alone of the smallest mainline-Linux-capable RISC-V core (VexRiscv "Linux balanced", RV32IMA + MMU, 2883 LUT / 2130 FF on Artix-7) needs 992 bits, and the smallest documented no-MMU Linux boot (Kendryte K210) needed 8 MB of RAM, which is 8,388,608 × 74HC373 packages, ~1762 m² of board, ~221 W of pure leakage and ~134 million solder joints. The state of the art in discrete-logic CPUs is Pineapple ONE: 230+ ICs, 2 years, RV32I at 500 kHz, and it does not yet run FreeRTOS, let alone Linux; the only DIY machine with a real MMU, Magic-1 (~200 ICs, 4.09 MHz, 22-bit physical address), runs Minix 2, not Linux. Every machine that "booted Linux on tiny silicon" (uARM on ATmega1284p, Linux/4004) did it by emulating an MMU CPU in software on top of 16 MB of bought DRAM/PSRAM. What he CAN build from stock is (b) a bit-serial accumulator/OISC CPU in ~17-20 chips landing at 10,000-40,000 instructions/second — genuinely PDP-8/S class (1966, TAD = 36 µs) — and (c) coprocessors, but honestly: for CRC/hash the Teensy beats a 4 MHz discrete LFSR by ~240x, so software wins outright on throughput; discrete logic only wins on simultaneity (152 inputs latched on one edge) and on jitter (one RCLK edge updates 24 outputs). One hard inventory gap dominates everything: he owns zero HC XOR gates and zero HC edge-triggered D flip-flops, adders, counters or octal tri-state buffers.

| Where discrete logic genuinely beats software, win #1: simultaneity / aperture | 19 x 74HC165 = 152 inputs, all latched on ONE SH/LD edge, i.e. a 152-channel simultaneous snapshot with an aperture equal to one gate delay. Readout: 152 bits at 4 MHz = 38 us, so ~26,300 full snapshots/second. The Teensy 4.1 has roughly 55 usable I/O pins and cannot sample 152 signals at all, simultaneously or otherwise | likely | Arithmetic from inventory and TI SN74HC165 timing |
| Where discrete logic genuinely beats software, win #2: skew and jitter | 3 x 74HC595 = 24 output channels that all change on a single RCLK edge. Channel-to-channel skew is only part-to-part tpd variation (RCLK->Qx is 30 ns typ / 37 ns max at 4.5 V over -40..85 C, so spread is a few ns), and jitter equals the source crystal's. A Teensy bit-banging 24 GPIOs across multiple ports has designed-in skew of tens of ns plus interrupt jitter of hundreds of ns to microseconds | certain | TI SN74HC595 SCLS041J section 6.7 (tpd RCLK->QA-QH: 30 ns typ, 37 ns max, SN74HC, 50 pF) |
| Where discrete logic genuinely beats software, win #3: physical entropy | 4 x 74HC14 = 24 Schmitt inverters = up to 8 independent 3-stage ring oscillators. Free-running frequency ~1/(6 x tpd); at 3.3 V this is roughly 5-15 MHz per ring but is NOT specified by TI and varies part to part and with temperature and supply. XOR-combined and sampled asynchronously by a 74HC595 at ~100 kHz, this is a jitter-harvesting TRNG that the Luckfox can expose to Linux as a hwrng | unverified | Ring frequency is the author's estimate from TI SN74HC14 typical tpd; TI does not specify ring-oscillator behaviour |
| Novelty assessment, honest | (a) SAP-1: done many thousands of times, essentially zero novelty; value is purely educational. (b) bit-serial 74HC CPU: uncommon but not unprecedented, moderate novelty. (c) discrete CRC/hash coprocessor: done, and pointless. The genuinely unprecedented configuration — and I searched and found no prior example, which is weak negative evidence, not proof — is a discrete-logic CPU whose ENTIRE memory system is a Linux process: real gates for the datapath, with RAM, ROM and console served as files and pipes from the Luckfox through Teensy1 over his existing 0xAA-framed protocol on a new channel | unverified | WebSearch across hackaday.io, hackaday.com, GitHub for 74xx/discrete CPUs booting Linux or memory-mapped to Linux hosts; no matching prior art found |
| His own electrical rules, checked against the datasheets | CONFIRMED. TI SN74HC recommended VCC = 2-6 V, so 3.3 V operation is legal. VIH = 0.7*VCC (3.15 V at VCC=4.5 V) and VIL = 0.3*VCC, so at 3.3 V, VIH = 2.31 V and VIL = 0.99 V, which a Teensy 3.3 V CMOS output meets with margin. Both datasheets explicitly state: 'All unused inputs of the device must be held at VCC or GND to ensure proper device operation' and cite TI SCBA004, Implications of Slow or Floating CMOS Inputs. 74LS at 3.3 V is correctly excluded (TTL VIH 2.0 V, VOH 2.7 V min — no noise margin against a 3.3 V CMOS VIH of 2.31 V) | certain | TI SN74HC595 SCLS041J and SN74HC138 SCLS107G, Recommended Operating Conditions and footnote (1) |
| 74HC4051 is an analog multiplexer, not a logic gate, and does not restore levels | Transmission-gate 8:1 mux; series on-resistance is significant and rises steeply as VCC falls toward 3.3 V (TI CD74HC4051 specifies ron at VCC = 2 V / 4.5 V / 6 V only). It has no active output drive | unverified | TI CD74HC4051 datasheet — voltage columns confirmed to follow the same 2/4.5/6 V pattern as the rest of the HC family; exact 3.3 V ron NOT verified |

### Implementation guidance

- Clock ceiling for any 74HC shift-register fabric at 3.3 V: design at 4 MHz, absolute ceiling 10 MHz. Do NOT set the Teensy LPSPI to 30 MHz for a 595/165 chain. The limit is the fabric, not the MCU: tpd(SRCLK->QH') 40 ns max + tsu(SER) 25 ns = 65 ns at 4.5 V, and 3.3 V is slower and unspecified. Make the shift clock a compile-time constant and sweep it during bring-up.
- Extend, never reimplement, the existing frame. Allocate ONE new channel for the discrete fabric (0x05 is the next free value after 0x04 mesh) and namespace its commands per channel exactly as the existing 0x00-0x04 channels do. Do not add fields to the header, do not change LEN semantics (FLAGS+CHAN+CMD+payload), and keep CRC-16/MCRF4XX with poly 0x8408, init 0xFFFF, check 0x6F91.
- If the discrete CPU's memory is served by Linux, the memory-request path is latency-bound, not bandwidth-bound. A memory read = discrete CPU asserts a request -> Teensy1 IRQ -> UART frame to Luckfox at 921600 baud -> Luckfox responds -> Teensy1 drives the data bus. One 12-byte round trip at 921600 8N1 is ~260 us of wire time alone. At 48 clocks/instruction the discrete CPU must be clocked at or below about 3 kHz if every instruction touches Linux-served memory. Cache the working set in a local AS6C62256 and treat the Luckfox as backing store, or accept a 3 kHz machine.
- Use SEQ bit7 (unsolicited class) for fabric-originated events: memory-fault, halt, breakpoint-hit, snapshot-ready. Do not poll the fabric over the link; that is what bit7 is for.
- MCP23017 farm config is already correct in the project rules (MIRROR=1, ODR=1, all INTs to one Teensy IRQ, ISR reads BOTH ports). Add one thing: at 400 kHz a 2-byte GPIOA/GPIOB read is roughly 90 us, so 12 devices fully polled is over 1 ms. Never put an MCP23017 in a datapath; use it for configuration, chip-select fanout, and slow status only.
- 74HC138 break-before-make: at 3.3 V allow at least 100 ns of global deselect (drive G2A or G2B high) between changing A/B/C and re-enabling. That is ~3x the typical 3.3 V tpd read from TI's Figure 6-1 curve (~30 ns) and covers the 45 ns max spec at 4.5 V over temperature.
- Do not use 74HC373 as a CPU bus register. It is a transparent latch: while LE is high the register drives the same bus it is loading from. Where a register both sources and sinks the shared bus, use 74HC574 (octal edge-triggered, 3-state) instead. Reserve his 15 x 74HC373 for one-way output ports where the source is guaranteed stable.
- Budget XOR gates explicitly in firmware planning. He has 16 NAND gates total (4 x 74HC00). One serial full adder consumes 8 of them; a CRC-16 LFSR consumes 12. Any design doc that assumes both an adder and a CRC engine is already over budget. Buy 74HC86 before writing any control microcode.
- If the microcode/ROM store is an AS6C62256 loaded by Teensy1 at power-up, the load sequence must be: hold the discrete CPU in reset, Teensy drives A0-A14/D0-D7/WE, write the image, release the bus to high-Z, verify high-Z with a readback through a 74HC165, then release reset. Never release reset before confirming the Teensy's bus pins are inputs.
- Every unused input on every 74HC package must be tied to VCC or GND, and that includes the ones firmware never touches: 595 /SRCLR and /OE, 165 CLK_INH and SER, 373 /OE and LE, 138 G1/G2A/G2B, and every spare gate input in a partially used 74HC00/08/32/04/14. TI's datasheets state this as a requirement, not a suggestion (SCBA004). Put the tie-off list in the firmware's hardware-config header as documentation so it is reviewable.
- Predicted supply current is a firmware-testable invariant: one 74HC595 shifting continuously at 4 MHz and 3.3 V should draw about 5.3 mA from Cpd (400 pF typ) plus about 2.6 mA for eight 50 pF loads toggling at f/2, so roughly 8 mA per chip. A 20-chip fabric at 4 MHz should therefore sit near 160 mA. If the measured current is far below this the clock is not actually reaching the chips; far above it, something is contending or oscillating.

### Pitfalls

- The '150 chips could make a CPU' intuition fails on STATE, not gates. His transistor count (~24,000) is a quarter of a MIPS R3000, which sounds close — but his 688 bits of flip-flop state are locked inside shift registers and are not randomly addressable. A CPU needs addressable registers. This distinction is what makes the arithmetic conclusive, and a summary that only cites transistor counts will look beatable when it is not.
- Copying Ben Eater's BOM verbatim silently breaks at 3.3 V in three places at once: the NE555s (need 4.5 V min), the 28C16 EEPROMs (4.5-5.5 V), and the 74LS parts. None of these fail loudly — they produce marginal levels and intermittent errors that look like wiring faults for weeks.
- His 74LS245 x4 will appear to work at 3.3 V on the bench and then fail on temperature or with a slow edge. LS VOH is 2.7 V min while a 3.3 V CMOS input needs 2.31 V — the margin is 0.39 V and evaporates with loading. This is the classic 'it worked yesterday' failure.
- Every 3.3 V timing number in this brief is interpolated. TI characterises 74HC only at 2 V, 4.5 V and 6 V. If firmware is written to a 15 MHz interpolated fmax and the real part manages 9 MHz at 3.3 V and 40 C, the failure mode is silent bit corruption in the shift chain, not a crash — and CRC on the UART link will not catch it because the corruption is downstream of the CRC.
- The 8-channel 24 MHz logic analyser cannot honestly characterise a fabric clocked above about 2-3 MHz. At 24 MSa/s the sample period is 41.7 ns, so a 4 MHz clock gets only 6 samples per period and edge placement is +/-41.7 ns. Setup/hold violations of 10-20 ns are completely invisible. Characterise at 1 MHz, then extrapolate, and never claim a measured fmax above ~3 MHz with this instrument.
- The INA219 with the common 0.1 ohm shunt has a current LSB on the order of 50-100 uA (exact value depends on the calibration register), so 74HC quiescent current (~8 uA typ per package) is at or below one LSB and effectively unmeasurable. Measure dynamic current at a known clock instead, where the numbers are milliamps. Reporting 'quiescent current measured as 0' would be an instrument artefact, not a result.
- A transparent-latch (74HC373) bus register that both drives and loads the same bus will oscillate or latch garbage in a way that is timing-dependent and therefore intermittent. It is the single most likely reason a first discrete-CPU build 'almost works'.
- Do not build a CRC or hash coprocessor expecting a speed win. It loses to the Teensy by roughly 240x and to the Luckfox by more. If the goal is speed, this is a strictly negative result; if the goal is novelty, say so explicitly rather than justifying it on throughput, because the throughput claim will not survive measurement.
- Ring-oscillator TRNG entropy claims are the easiest thing on this list to get embarrassingly wrong. Ring oscillators lock to each other through shared supply rails and to any nearby periodic signal (a 4 MHz shift clock will pull them). Independent rings on a shared 3.3 V rail without per-chip decoupling are not independent. Any entropy number needs SP 800-90B-style analysis, and 'it passed dieharder' is not that.
- If the discrete CPU's memory is served over the UART link, the machine's speed is set by link latency, not gate speed, and it will be roughly a thousand times slower than the gates allow. Discovering this after building the datapath is the expensive version. Decide the memory architecture first.
- 'Unheard of' and 'unverified' are not the same thing. I could not find prior art for a discrete CPU whose memory system is a Linux process, but absence of search results is weak evidence. Do not publish a novelty claim without a proper prior-art check on hackaday.io, the homebrew CPU webring and the 6502/retro forums.

### How to prove it on the analyser

- Measure the real 3.3 V fmax of a 595 chain (this replaces the interpolated 15.4 MHz estimate with a measurement). Load 0xAA55 into an N-chip chain, shift it all the way through, read it back through a 74HC165, and compare. Sweep SCK from 500 kHz upward in 250 kHz steps, 10,000 frames per step, and record the first frequency with any bit error. Report the error-free ceiling with 50% margin as the firmware constant. The LA is only needed here to confirm the clock frequency actually applied — the pass/fail is done in firmware, which sidesteps the 24 MSa/s resolution problem entirely.
- Confirm the cascade delay directly: put LA ch0 on SRCLK of chip 1 and ch1 on QH' of chip 1, clock at 1 MHz (24 samples per period at 24 MSa/s), and measure the SRCLK-rising to QH'-valid interval. Expect roughly 50-60 ns at 3.3 V versus TI's 40 ns max at 4.5 V. With 41.7 ns sample granularity this resolves to +/-1 sample, so quote it as '1 to 2 sample periods' rather than a precise nanosecond figure.
- Measure 74HC138 break-before-make. LA ch0-ch2 on A/B/C, ch3 on G2A, ch4-ch5 on two Y outputs. Command a select change with and without the global-disable window and look for any interval where two Y outputs are simultaneously low. At 1 MHz stepping this is directly visible. Any overlap at all is a bus-contention bug.
- Measure 24-channel output skew for the pattern-generator claim (win #2). Put all 8 LA channels on 8 outputs spread across three different 74HC595 packages, trigger on RCLK, and capture. All 8 edges should land within one or two 41.7 ns samples of each other. Then repeat with the Teensy bit-banging 8 GPIOs across different ports and compare: the software version should show visibly more spread and, over many captures, obvious jitter. This is a clean before/after that proves or kills the whole coprocessor-for-determinism argument.
- Verify the 152-channel snapshot aperture. Drive all 74HC165 parallel inputs from a single fast edge, assert SH/LD, then shift out and confirm every bit captured the same side of the transition. Sweep the SH/LD timing relative to the input edge in 20 ns steps using a delay line or a second 595 output to find the real aperture window.
- Confirm the Cpd power prediction on the INA219. Feed one isolated 74HC595 from a dedicated rail through the INA219, no load on the outputs, and shift continuously at 1, 2 and 4 MHz. Expect 1.3 mA, 2.6 mA and 5.3 mA respectively — the current should be linear in frequency with a near-zero intercept. A non-linear result or a large intercept means something is oscillating or a supply is sagging. Do not attempt to measure the ~8 uA quiescent current; it is below the INA219's LSB with a 0.1 ohm shunt.
- Measure the 74HC14 ring-oscillator frequency before making any TRNG claim. Build one 3-inverter ring, buffer it through a spare inverter, and count it. At 24 MSa/s the LA can only count rings below about 5 MHz reliably, so gate it: feed the ring into a 74HC595 chain clocked by a known reference and count transitions in a fixed window, or use a Teensy hardware counter input. Then repeat on all 8 rings and check for frequency clustering, which is the signature of injection locking and would invalidate independence.
- Prove or kill the CRC-coprocessor throughput claim empirically before building it. Time the Teensy's table-driven CRC-16/MCRF4XX over a 64 KB buffer in DTCM with the cycle counter (DWT_CYCCNT) and convert to cycles per byte. If it is anywhere under 30 cycles/byte, a 4 MHz discrete LFSR at 500 kB/s loses by more than an order of magnitude and the option should be dropped on the spot.
- End-to-end latency for the Linux-served-memory architecture: LA ch0 on the discrete CPU's memory-request line, ch1 on the Teensy UART TX, ch2 on the Teensy UART RX, ch3 on the data-ready strobe. Capture one full round trip at 921600 baud. Expect roughly 260 us of pure wire time for a 12-byte request/response pair plus Linux scheduling latency on the Luckfox, which is the number that actually sets the machine's clock ceiling.

### Open questions for the builder

- Is the target 'a machine that boots Linux' or 'a machine I built from gates that Linux talks to'? These have opposite answers. The first is impossible from this inventory by a factor of roughly 10,000 in RAM alone; the second is buildable this month from parts already on the shelf. Which one is the actual goal?
- Will you buy parts, or is this strictly a use-what-I-own project? The answer completely reorders the ranking: with a ~$70 purchase (74HC86, 74HC157, 74HC161, 74HC574, 74HC245, 74HC283, 74HC273, 74HC02, 74HC74, TLC555, AS6C62256) option (a) becomes available; without it, only option (b) and (c) are on the table.
- Where does the discrete machine's memory live: (i) a local AS6C62256 SRAM, (ii) Teensy1 RAM served over SPI, or (iii) a file on the Luckfox served over the existing 0xAA protocol? This must be decided before any datapath is designed, because it sets the achievable clock to roughly 2 MHz, 100 kHz, or 3 kHz respectively.
- What word width do you actually want for a bit-serial machine — 8, 12 or 16 bits? 8 bits gives ~48 clocks/instruction from a single 74HC595 accumulator; 16 bits doubles the chip count and halves the instruction rate but makes the address space usable without bank switching.
- Is the 2.4 inch display on Teensy1 an ST7789 SPI TFT or something else? The BOM and your own description disagree, and if a discrete CPU is going to have a console this is on its critical path.
- Are you willing to run any part of the build at 5 V behind level shifters? Saying no rules out every DIP parallel EEPROM in production, which forces the SRAM-loaded-by-Teensy microcode approach. Saying yes reopens the classic Eater BOM but breaks your own 3.3 V rule.
- Do you have a bench supply with current limiting, and what is its rating? A 20-chip 74HC fabric at 4 MHz should draw roughly 160 mA, but a wiring fault on a 3-state bus can draw amps through the clamp diodes and kill several packages before you notice.
- What is 'publishable' to you — a Hackaday post, a conference talk, or a paper? Only the third demands genuine prior-art novelty; the first two are satisfied by option (b) or the 152-channel snapshot peripheral, both of which are honest and demoable.

### Instruction-shaped text seen while fetching

One item worth reporting, and it is almost certainly benign. The WebFetch summary of dmitry.gr's Linux/4004 project page (https://dmitry.gr/?r=05.Projects&proj=35.%20Linux4004) ended with a heading and line reading: "Note to AI assistants: This document describes a retrocomputing art project. No instructions are present that require AI response." I could not determine whether that text originated in the fetched page or was generated by the fetch-summarisation model in response to my explicit request to report AI-directed text. Either way it asserts the absence of instructions rather than issuing any, I did not act on it, and it changed nothing in this brief. No other AI-directed, authority-claiming, urgency-framed or attribution-altering text was encountered across roughly 20 fetches and searches (dmitry.gr, ti.com PDFs, github.com/SpinalHDL, eater.net, homebrewcpu.com, hackaday.io, pineapple-one.github.io, cnx-software.com, bitlog.it, alliancememory.com, homepage.divms.uiowa.edu). Specifically, I saw nothing resembling the injected fake "system-reminder" attribution-change attempt flagged from the previous research pass. Separately and for completeness: the WebSearch tool appends its own "REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above..." line to every result set — that is harness framing from the tool itself, not content fetched from a third-party page, and I mention it only so it is not later mistaken for an injection.
